<SYSTEM>This is the abridged developer documentation for Napwake</SYSTEM>

# Napwake

> Pauses idle Kubernetes workloads in staging, preview and dev, and wakes them on the first request. It never deletes a workload or its data.

Napwake docs # Non-prod environments that sleep when nobody's using them, and wake when someone does. Pauses idle Kubernetes workloads in staging, preview and dev, and wakes them on the first request. It never deletes a workload or its data. [Install](/next/getting-started/installation/)[Quickstart](/next/getting-started/quickstart/) A request has reached staging while it was asleep. The doorman holds it at the door while staging wakes, then lets it through. Dev stays asleep. Preview is already awake. ## See what’s idle first A team that works 10 hours a day, five days a week, uses staging 50 of 168 hours. The other 70%, it’s idle. `kubectl napwake scan` shows your cluster’s own number before you install anything. [![The scan report for an example cluster. It leads with 19 of 31 workloads running for nobody and $691 that could be freed a month. Beside it, the cluster drawn as a hotel at night, twice: now, with most rooms lit, and with Napwake, the rooms lit for nobody dark.](/next/home/scan-report-light.webp)](https://napwake.dev/examples/scan-report/) [![](/next/home/scan-report-dark.webp)](https://napwake.dev/examples/scan-report/) 
```sh
kubectl napwake scan
```
 * It runs with your kubeconfig and installs nothing in the cluster. * It shows what's idle right now as rooms lit for nobody, and what each costs at your nodes' list prices. * With Prometheus, it replays the last week of each workload's CPU and rollouts, and says what pausing would have freed. One file to send your team. It loads nothing from elsewhere and prints to PDF. [Open the example report ↗](https://napwake.dev/examples/scan-report/) [Scan, in the plugin's docs](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#scan-a-cluster) ## Four doors Asleep · paused Scaled to zero. Its Services are routed to the doorman, which wakes it on the first request. Waking up · resuming A request, an activity annotation, or `autoResume` ahead of demand woke it. Its replicas are restored and its dependencies woken. Awake · running Its pods are Ready and held requests passed through. With no activity for `idleAfter`, and no forecast of demand, it pauses again. Needs you · needs attention A note pinned on the door. Something stops Napwake doing its job, and `kubectl napwake status` says what to fix. ## How it works Label a Deployment, StatefulSet or namespace `napwake.dev/managed: "true"` and Napwake creates a ManagedWorkload for it, with its settings from annotations. Then it keeps an activity clock: CPU, deploys, activity annotations, and Prometheus. 1. Awake · running Running Activity clock: CPU, deploys, activity annotations, Prometheus 2. No activity for `idleAfter`, and no forecast of demand 3. Asleep · paused Paused Scaled to zero, its Services routed to the doorman 4. A request, an activity annotation, or `autoResume` ahead of demand 5. Waking up · resuming Resuming Replicas restored, dependencies woken Pods Ready, held requests passed through ## What it does * ### Opt in with a label Label a workload or namespace `napwake.dev/managed: "true"`. Settings are annotations in your Git, so Argo CD and Flux work as today. * ### Activity clock Napwake records each workload's last activity: CPU over `cpuThreshold`, a deploy, an activity annotation or a Prometheus query. After `idleAfter` with none, it pauses. No learning period. * ### Demand forecasting A Holt-Winters double seasonal model learns each workload's day and week from up to four weeks of Prometheus. It scores itself from week two and acts when accurate enough: `eager`, `balanced` or `cautious`. It re-learns after drift, never from a holiday. * ### Safe by default Napwake never pauses what it can't measure. A confident forecast can hold a pause off. `dryRun` pauses nothing and shows what would. Anyone else's scale-up is a wake. If a GitOps tool undoes a pause, Napwake tells you how to fix it. * ### Cost tracking Napwake tracks each workload's cost, what pausing frees, and what came off the bill, from each node pool's spare capacity. Freed capacity is money once an autoscaler removes nodes. `kubectl napwake nodes` shows what keeps them. [How to let them go](/next/guides/node-pools/). * ### Metrics and alerts Prometheus metrics for operator health, lifecycle transitions, wakes on request and forecast confidence, with alerting rules. ## Quick example deployment.yaml 
```yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: my-api
  namespace: dev
  labels:
    napwake.dev/managed: "true"
  annotations:
    napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"
    napwake.dev/idle-after: "1h"
```
 Napwake watches `my-api` and records each time it’s active: CPU above `cpuThreshold` (10% of its requests by default), a deploy, or an activity annotation, and, with a [Prometheus query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/), its request rate. In dry-run it only measures. Once you run `kubectl napwake enable my-api -n dev`, it pauses the Deployment after an hour with no activity, and wakes it when a request reaches its Service. For every setting, see [Opting in](/next/guides/opt-in/). For settings annotations don’t cover, write a [ManagedWorkload](/next/guides/managed-workload/) yourself. Get started * [Installation →Helm or the Kustomize manifests, with Argo CD or Flux.](/next/getting-started/installation/) * [Quickstart →One workload from idle to paused while idle, woken when used.](/next/getting-started/quickstart/) * [kubectl plugin →`scan`, `status`, `pause`, `wake` and the rest.](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/)

# Installation

> Install Napwake's operator and doorman with the Helm chart, or with the Kustomize manifests that install.yaml is built from.

Before you start * Kubernetes v1.30+, tested in CI on 1.30 and 1.37 * kubectl v1.30+ * [metrics-server](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/metrics-server), for pod CPU through the Kubernetes Metrics API * Helm v3.8+, to install the OCI chart ## Install with Helm 1. Put your settings in a values file, starting with your users’ [time zone](#time-zone): napwake-values.yaml 
   ```yaml
   timezone: Europe/London
   ```
 2. Install from the OCI registry, with no `helm repo` to add: 
   ```bash
   helm install napwake oci://ghcr.io/napwake/charts/napwake \
     --version 0.2.0-rc.9 \
     --namespace napwake-system \
     --create-namespace \
     -f napwake-values.yaml
   ```
 Checkpoint 
```bash
# Check the operator and the doorman are running
kubectl get pods -n napwake-system


# Verify the CRD is installed
kubectl get crd managedworkloads.napwake.dev
```
 Check any install this way. Keep the file and upgrade with it, since an upgrade given `--set` alone drops your other values ([Keeping your settings](/next/operations/upgrading/#keeping-your-settings)). The chart installs and upgrades the ManagedWorkload CRD. The chart’s version has no `v`. Its image’s tag has one, such as `v0.2.0-rc.9`. [Helm values](/next/reference/helm-values/) lists every value. ## With Argo CD or Flux The chart is only in an OCI registry, which Argo CD and Flux name differently. Commit one of these where your GitOps tool syncs from. * Argo CD application.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Application
  metadata:
    name: napwake
    namespace: argocd
  spec:
    project: default
    source:
      repoURL: ghcr.io/napwake/charts
      chart: napwake
      targetRevision: 0.2.0-rc.9
      helm:
        valuesObject:
          timezone: Europe/London
    destination:
      server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
      namespace: napwake-system
    syncPolicy:
      automated: {}
      syncOptions:
        - CreateNamespace=true
  ```
 * `repoURL` is the registry and path without `oci://`, which tells Argo CD it’s OCI. `chart` is the chart’s name. * The registry is public, so Argo CD needs no credentials. * For the Kustomize manifests, point an Application’s `path` at your kustomization. * Flux napwake.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: source.toolkit.fluxcd.io/v1
  kind: OCIRepository
  metadata:
    name: napwake
    namespace: flux-system
  spec:
    interval: 1h
    url: oci://ghcr.io/napwake/charts/napwake
    ref:
      tag: 0.2.0-rc.9
    layerSelector:
      mediaType: application/vnd.cncf.helm.chart.content.v1.tar+gzip
      operation: copy
  ---
  apiVersion: helm.toolkit.fluxcd.io/v2
  kind: HelmRelease
  metadata:
    name: napwake
    namespace: flux-system
  spec:
    interval: 1h
    chartRef:
      kind: OCIRepository
      name: napwake
    releaseName: napwake
    targetNamespace: napwake-system
    storageNamespace: napwake-system
    install:
      createNamespace: true
    values:
      timezone: Europe/London
  ```
 * It needs Flux 2.6 or later, where `OCIRepository` is `v1`. * `releaseName` keeps the release `napwake`, as these docs call it, instead of Flux’s default, `napwake-system-napwake`. * `storageNamespace` keeps its Helm history beside it, where `helm list -n napwake-system` shows it. * For the Kustomize manifests, point a Flux `Kustomization` at your kustomization. To upgrade, change `targetRevision` or `tag` to the new version. ### Moving a Helm install to Argo CD or Flux Argo CD or Flux can take over a `helm install` where it runs, with the same values. Nothing is reinstalled or restarted, and paused workloads stay paused. Don’t uninstall first. Doing what [`helm uninstall`](#uninstall) asks, stopping managing every workload, wakes every paused one. It also loses each one’s forecast and its tally of what pausing freed. 
```bash
helm get values napwake -n napwake-system
```
 Give the tool these values, and the version in the `CHART` column of `helm list -n napwake-system`. * Argo CD 1. Commit the Application above, with those values in `valuesObject` and that version as `targetRevision`. Argo CD finds every resource as it would make it. It shows `Synced` and `Healthy` without applying anything, so an automated sync doesn’t start. 2. Sync it once by hand, with **Sync** in its UI or with kubectl, giving the Application’s sync options: 
     ```bash
     kubectl patch application napwake -n argocd --type merge -p '{"operation":{"sync":{"syncOptions":["CreateNamespace=true"]}}}'
     ```
 This applies the resources and marks them as the Application’s own, with `argocd.argoproj.io/tracking-id`. It warns that each lacks `kubectl.kubernetes.io/last-applied-configuration`, which Helm doesn’t set and the sync adds. 3. Check it’s `Synced` and `Healthy`: 
     ```bash
     kubectl get application napwake -n argocd
     ```
 4. Delete Helm’s record of the release, its `sh.helm.release.v1.napwake.v<revision>` Secrets, so `helm` no longer acts on it: 
     ```bash
     kubectl delete secrets -n napwake-system -l owner=helm,name=napwake
     ```
 `helm list -n napwake-system` no longer shows it. From now on, change the values or `targetRevision` in Git. * Flux Commit the `OCIRepository` and `HelmRelease` above, with those values in `values` and that version as `tag`. Their `releaseName` and `storageNamespace` are the release’s own. So Flux’s helm-controller finds it in Helm’s storage and takes it over in place. It upgrades it to a new revision, with the same values, and deletes nothing. `helm list -n napwake-system` still shows it, its chart version followed by `+` and the start of the chart’s digest. From now on, change it in Git, since Flux undoes a `helm upgrade` by hand at its next reconcile. ## Install without Helm Both install the CRD, RBAC, the operator (`deployment/napwake-controller-manager`), and the doorman’s Deployment, Service and PodDisruptionBudget, all in `napwake-system`. * install.yaml 
  ```bash
  kubectl apply -f https://github.com/napwake/napwake/releases/download/v0.2.0-rc.9/install.yaml
  ```
 `install.yaml` is the Kustomize manifests in one file. Set your users’ [time zone](#time-zone) on the operator: 
  ```bash
  kubectl patch deployment napwake-controller-manager -n napwake-system --type=json \
    -p '[{"op":"add","path":"/spec/template/spec/containers/0/args/-","value":"--timezone=Europe/London"}]'
  ```
 An `install.yaml` that changes the operator’s arguments replaces them. After upgrading, check `--timezone` is still there, or use Kustomize, which keeps it. * Kustomize kustomization.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: kustomize.config.k8s.io/v1beta1
  kind: Kustomization
  resources:
    - https://github.com/napwake/napwake//config/default?ref=v0.2.0-rc.9
  patches:
    - target:
        kind: Deployment
        name: napwake-controller-manager
      patch: |-
        - op: add
          path: /spec/template/spec/containers/0/args/-
          value: --timezone=Europe/London
  ```
 
  ```bash
  kubectl apply -k .
  ```
 This uses the manifests as a remote base, pinned to the release, in your own kustomization. It sets your users’ [time zone](#time-zone) on the operator. Set its other [flags](/next/reference/configuration/) the same way. To upgrade, change `ref`. Kustomize fetches bases with git, so `kubectl` or the GitOps tool running it must reach github.com. * From source 
  ```bash
  git clone https://github.com/napwake/napwake.git
  cd napwake
  make docker-build IMG=napwake:dev
  ```
 The image is only in your local Docker. On a [Kind](https://kind.sigs.k8s.io/) cluster, load it into the nodes: 
  ```bash
  kind load docker-image napwake:dev --name <cluster>
  make deploy IMG=napwake:dev
  ```
 Elsewhere, push it to a registry the nodes can pull from: 
  ```bash
  make docker-build docker-push IMG=<registry>/napwake:dev
  make deploy IMG=<registry>/napwake:dev
  ```
 `make deploy` installs the Kustomize manifests, `config/default`, with the operator and the doorman running `IMG`. It writes `IMG` into `config/manager/kustomization.yaml`, and `git checkout config/manager` puts it back. ### Prometheus and NetworkPolicies without Helm Two more bases, pinned to the same release, add what these chart values turn on. Add them to your kustomization’s `resources`, or apply one alone: 
```bash
kubectl apply -k "https://github.com/napwake/napwake//config/prometheus?ref=v0.2.0-rc.9"
```
 From a clone: 
```bash
kubectl apply -k config/prometheus
kubectl apply -k config/network-policy
```
 | Base | Adds | Chart values | | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `https://github.com/napwake/napwake//config/prometheus?ref=v0.2.0-rc.9` | ServiceMonitors for the operator and the doorman, and the [alerting rules](/next/operations/monitoring/#alerting-rules). Needs the Prometheus Operator’s CRDs, and your Prometheus [allowed to read the metrics](/next/operations/monitoring/#scraping) | `metrics.serviceMonitor.enabled`, `metrics.prometheusRule.enabled` | | `https://github.com/napwake/napwake//config/network-policy?ref=v0.2.0-rc.9` | NetworkPolicies. Only namespaces labelled `metrics: enabled` may scrape the operator and the doorman. The doorman’s routing ports (TCP 20000-29999) stay open to every caller of a paused workload’s Services | `networkPolicy.enabled` | ## Time zone The [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/) learns when each workload is busy, by hour of day and day of week, in the operator’s time zone. It holds off pausing before busy hours and, with `autoResume`, wakes workloads ahead of them. Set Helm’s `timezone` value, or the operator’s `--timezone` flag, to your users’ IANA zone, such as `Europe/London`, `America/New_York` or `Asia/Kolkata`. The chart’s install notes say which zone the forecast uses. * UTC is the default. A team on daylight saving then finds its workloads woken an hour early or late for half the year. * Teams in different zones can pick the zone most of them work in, or have [an install each](#more-than-one-install). * [Wall-clock alignment](/next/concepts/forecasting/#wall-clock-alignment) has the details. To change it on a Helm install, change your values file. Then upgrade with it at the version you run, as in [Keeping your settings](/next/operations/upgrading/#keeping-your-settings): napwake-values.yaml 
```yaml
timezone: America/New_York
```
 
```bash
helm upgrade napwake oci://ghcr.io/napwake/charts/napwake \
  --version 0.2.0-rc.9 \
  --namespace napwake-system \
  -f napwake-values.yaml
```
 The operator restarts with it. With Argo CD or Flux, change `valuesObject` or the HelmRelease’s `values` in Git. ## More than one install One install per cluster is usual. Several can share one, such as one per team, if each: * has a namespace and a release name of its own, since the chart names its ClusterRoles and admission policies after the release * sets `watchNamespaces`, with no namespace in two installs’ lists All but one set `crds.install: false`. They share the ManagedWorkload CRD, which belongs to the release that installed it and is upgraded with it. Make that one the newest Napwake. The Kustomize manifests, and so `install.yaml`, are a single install, in `napwake-system`, watching every namespace. Each operator marks the ManagedWorkloads it takes on, and the doorman EndpointSlices it writes, with [`napwake.dev/operator`](/next/reference/labels-annotations/#annotation-napwake-dev-operator), its namespace. Another install leaves those alone, with a `ManagedByAnotherInstall` warning event. So two installs watching one namespace by mistake don’t both pause and wake its workloads. The first to take each one on keeps it. ## Uninstall Stop managing workloads while the operator still runs, so it can scale paused ones back up. 1. Remove the `napwake.dev/managed` label from your workloads and namespaces, in Git if they live there. Napwake deletes the ManagedWorkloads it created from them. 2. Delete the ManagedWorkloads you wrote yourself. Each scales its workload back to the replicas it had before the pause. 
   ```bash
   kubectl delete managedworkloads --all --all-namespaces
   ```
 3. Remove the operator: 
   ```bash
   helm uninstall napwake -n napwake-system
   ```
 This keeps the CRD, since deleting it deletes every ManagedWorkload. Once none is left, delete it yourself: 
   ```bash
   kubectl delete crd managedworkloads.napwake.dev
   ```
 Without Helm, delete what you applied: `kubectl delete -f` with the `install.yaml` URL, `kubectl delete -k .` in your kustomization, or `make undeploy` from a clone. These delete the CRD too, unguarded, so first check `kubectl get managedworkloads -A` lists none. Caution Removing the operator or the CRD first skips step 2’s restore. Paused workloads stay at zero replicas, and their Services still send traffic to the doorman’s deleted pods. [Restore them by hand](#restoring-workloads-after-the-operator-is-gone). ### When `helm uninstall` refuses `helm uninstall` refuses while any ManagedWorkload is left. Its pre-delete hook, the `<fullname>-uninstall-guard` Job, first waits up to two and a half minutes for those being deleted. That lets the operator restore them and drain their doorman ports, which takes up to a minute. Then the hook lists any left and fails, before anything is removed. One deleted moments before you uninstall doesn’t hold it up. | Helm | Error | Above it | | ----------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | | 3, before 3.18 (such as 3.16) | `job napwake-uninstall-guard failed` | Nothing | | 3.18 and later | `job napwake-uninstall-guard failed` | The hook’s output | | 4 | `resource Job/napwake-system/napwake-uninstall-guard not ready` | The hook’s output, as one log message with its line breaks escaped | The output names the ManagedWorkloads left after the wait, says what to do and links here. After the first `\n`, it lists each one with the steps above. From Helm 4: 
```text
level=INFO msg="Napwake refuses to uninstall: these ManagedWorkloads' workloads would stay paused with no operator: staging/web. Remove the napwake.dev/managed label from them and their namespaces, and delete ManagedWorkloads you created, then uninstall again; see https://docs.napwake.dev/getting-started/installation/#uninstall\n\n..."
```
 When Helm prints only the error, `kubectl logs job/napwake-uninstall-guard -n napwake-system` shows the output, line breaks and all, for an hour. No Helm prints it for a revision that a Helm before 3.18 wrote, as a `helm rollback` after a refused uninstall can. That Helm drops the hook’s `helm.sh/hook-output-log-policy` until a newer Helm upgrades the release. ### Restoring workloads after the operator is gone If the operator was removed while ManagedWorkloads were left, nothing restores their workloads. That happens with `helm uninstall --no-hooks`, `kubectl delete -f install.yaml`, or deleting an Argo CD Application before Argo CD 3.3. Their `napwake.dev/cleanup` finalizers, which only the operator removes, also leave the ManagedWorkloads, and the CRD, hanging in `Terminating` when deleted. The simplest fix is to reinstall the operator and follow the steps above. Without it: 1. Scale each paused workload back to the replicas its ManagedWorkload records: 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkloads -A -o jsonpath='{range .items[?(@.status.pause)]}{.metadata.namespace} {.spec.target.kind} {.spec.target.name} {.status.pause.previousReplicas}{"\n"}{end}' |
     while read -r ns kind name replicas; do
       kubectl scale "$kind/$name" -n "$ns" --replicas="$replicas"
     done
   ```
 A workload scaled by KEDA was held at zero through the ScaledObject in its ManagedWorkload’s `status.pause.scaledObject`. Remove that ScaledObject’s `autoscaling.keda.sh/paused-replicas` annotation, or set it back to `status.pause.scaledObjectPausedReplicas` if that’s set. Each paused workload carries the same record in its `napwake.dev/paused-from` annotation, readable once its ManagedWorkload is gone. Remove it once restored: 
   ```bash
   kubectl annotate deployments,statefulsets --all -A napwake.dev/paused-from-
   ```
 2. Delete the EndpointSlices that route paused workloads’ Services to the doorman, so the Services use their own pods again: 
   ```bash
   kubectl delete endpointslices -A -l endpointslice.kubernetes.io/managed-by=doorman.napwake.dev
   ```
 3. Remove the finalizers, so the ManagedWorkloads and the CRD can be deleted: 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkloads -A -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.namespace} {.metadata.name}{"\n"}{end}' |
     while read -r ns name; do
       kubectl patch managedworkload "$name" -n "$ns" --type=merge -p '{"metadata":{"finalizers":null}}'
     done
   kubectl delete managedworkloads --all --all-namespaces
   kubectl delete crd managedworkloads.napwake.dev
   ```
 Next * [Quickstart →Manage your first workload.](/next/getting-started/quickstart/) * [kubectl plugin →The `kubectl napwake` commands the Quickstart uses.](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/) * [Upgrading →Keep your settings from one release to the next.](/next/operations/upgrading/)

# kubectl plugin

> Every kubectl napwake command, from scan to enable. What it's for, what it prints, and the flags you'll use most.

## Which command [No operator needed · scan →](#scan-a-cluster) [What’s idle, what it costs, and what pausing could free.](#scan-a-cluster) [Needs Prometheus history · forecast →](#replay-the-forecast) [How the forecast would do on your history, before `autoResume`.](#replay-the-forecast) [Needs the operator · status →](#see-what-napwake-is-doing) [What Napwake is doing with each workload it manages.](#see-what-napwake-is-doing) [No operator needed · nodes →](#see-why-nodes-dont-scale-down) [Why what pausing frees isn’t coming off the bill.](#see-why-nodes-dont-scale-down) [Needs the operator · pause →](#pause-a-workload) [Pause a workload now, such as a preview at the end of the day.](#pause-a-workload) [Needs the operator · wake →](#wake-a-workload) [Wake a workload now, or keep one awake a while, such as for a demo.](#wake-a-workload) [Needs the operator · deps →](#show-a-workloads-dependencies) [What Napwake wakes and holds awake with a workload, and what depends on it.](#show-a-workloads-dependencies) [Needs the operator · enable →](#enable-a-workload) [End dry-run for a workload or namespace, once you trust what Napwake measured.](#enable-a-workload) `scan` and `status` both show idle workloads and money, from opposite ends. `scan` surveys a whole cluster, managed or not, for what could pause and what that could free. `status` shows what Napwake does with what it manages. The rest answer narrower questions. Every flag, JSON field, exit status and permission is in the [kubectl plugin reference](/next/reference/cli/). ## Installation `kubectl napwake version` prints the plugin’s version, and needs no operator. * Homebrew On macOS and Linux: 
  ```bash
  brew install napwake/tap/kubectl-napwake
  kubectl napwake version
  ```
 Homebrew installs the newest release, or the newest release candidate until the first stable release. `brew upgrade` keeps it current. For this version of the docs exactly, use Krew or a download. * Krew With [krew](https://krew.sigs.k8s.io/docs/user-guide/setup/install/), kubectl’s plugin manager, installed: 
  ```bash
  kubectl krew install --manifest-url \
    https://github.com/napwake/napwake/releases/download/v0.2.0-rc.9/krew-napwake.yaml
  kubectl napwake version
  ```
 Krew installs it on Linux and macOS (amd64 and arm64) and Windows (amd64). `kubectl krew upgrade` doesn’t upgrade a plugin installed from a manifest URL. For a later release, run `kubectl krew uninstall napwake`, then install that release’s manifest. Once the plugin is accepted into the [krew-index](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/krew-index), `kubectl krew install napwake` will install it, and `kubectl krew upgrade` upgrade it. * Download Download your platform’s archive from the release, and put the binary on your `PATH` as `kubectl-napwake`. On macOS and Linux: 
  ```bash
  os=$(uname -s | tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]')
  arch=$(uname -m | sed -e 's/x86_64/amd64/' -e 's/aarch64/arm64/')
  curl -fLO "https://github.com/napwake/napwake/releases/download/v0.2.0-rc.9/kubectl-napwake-${os}-${arch}.tar.gz"
  tar xzf "kubectl-napwake-${os}-${arch}.tar.gz"
  sudo install "kubectl-napwake-${os}-${arch}" /usr/local/bin/kubectl-napwake
  kubectl napwake version
  ```
 On Windows, download `kubectl-napwake-windows-amd64.tar.gz` from the [release](https://github.com/napwake/napwake/releases/tag/v0.2.0-rc.9) and extract it with `tar xzf`. Rename `kubectl-napwake-windows-amd64.exe` to `kubectl-napwake.exe`, in a folder on your `PATH`. To check an archive came from the release workflow, see [Verifying a release](/next/reference/security/#verifying-a-release). * Source 
  ```bash
  git clone https://github.com/napwake/napwake.git
  cd napwake
  make build-plugin
  # Binary is at bin/kubectl-napwake
  ```
 ## Namespaces and connections | Commands | Namespaces | | ------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | | `scan`, `forecast`, `status` | Every namespace you can read | | The same, if your access doesn’t allow listing them all | Your kubeconfig context’s namespace, with a note | | The same, with `-n` | The namespaces you name | | The same, with `-A` (`--all-namespaces`) | Every one, failing rather than falling back | | `pause`, `wake`, `deps`, `enable` | One namespace, `-n` or your context’s, as in kubectl | Every command also takes kubectl’s connection flags, such as `--context`. See [Every command](/next/reference/cli/#every-command). ## Scan a cluster Find what’s idle, what it costs, and what pausing it could free. Nothing needs installing in the cluster. 
```
kubectl napwake scan
staging (EKS us-east-1): 214 workloads in 38 namespaces
Your bill: drops only as Karpenter removes nodes (it consolidates when underutilized after 1m)
History: the last 7 days, from monitoring/prometheus-operated

  Napwake has 12 workloads paused right now, freeing $1.84 an hour.
  Napwake has freed $96 this month pausing 14 live workloads, and saved $71 of it.
  3 workloads are in dry-run: measured by Napwake since starting, they would have slept 312 hours, freeing $41,
  about $140 a month.
  4 workloads are scaled to zero by hand; Napwake can pause them while idle and wake them on the
  next request instead.
  61 workloads are idle right now, reserving 48.0 vCPU and 190.0 GiB of memory.
  They cost $3,180/month while running, $4.36 an hour: each hour asleep frees that.
  Replaying the last 7 days, Napwake would have paused 58 of 190 unmanaged workloads for 6,120 hours in all,
  and freed $1,240: about $5,380/month.
  Not counted: 4 scaled to zero by hand and 1 without CPU history to replay.

  NAMESPACE    WORKLOAD               STATE                BECAUSE                                                  COST/MO   FREED THIS MONTH   SAVED   COULD SLEEP   WAKES   COULD FREE/MO
  preview-42   statefulset/postgres   idle (unmanaged)     CPU 0.3% of its request; last deployed 23 days ago   $140      -                  -       167h          0       $139
  preview-7    deployment/api         idle (dry-run)       no activity for 5h; measuring since Oct 1                $96       -                  -       41h           4       $40
  preview-3    deployment/web         paused (live)        paused 3h ago                                            $48       $12                $9      -             -       -
  preview-9    deployment/demo        paused (unmanaged)   scaled to zero, not by Napwake                           $0        -                  -       -             -       -
  payments     deployment/ledger      active (unmanaged)   CPU 64% of its request                                   $210      -                  -       0h            0       $0
  ...

Notes:
  - 2 workloads set no CPU requests, so their use can't be measured: kube-tools/agent, ops/exporter
  - 23 infrastructure workloads are left out of the totals and next steps: monitoring (9), argocd (7), ingress-nginx (3) and 2 more. Napwake never pauses Kubernetes' own workloads or itself; anything else, infrastructure included, pauses only if you opt it in
  - the history replay sees CPU and rollouts only; requests and activity annotations aren't in Prometheus, so a workload used with little CPU can look like it would sleep more than it would

Prices: AWS us-east-1 list prices · 4 node types · 6 workloads on spot, priced at spot
Pass --cpu-price and --memory-price for yours.
```
 `scan` only reads, with your kubeconfig, one cluster at a time. That’s your context, or the one you name with `--context`. Run it before you install Napwake, or after, to see what’s left to opt in. Once Napwake manages a workload, the scan reads its activity clock and shows what Napwake paused and freed. | Line or column | What it says | | ------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | First line | The cluster, and the workloads listed in how many namespaces | | Its namespace count | Only the namespaces listed workloads are in. 15 namespaces, 12 of them infrastructure, read `14 workloads in 3 namespaces`. | | Its cluster name | Shortened for EKS and GKE, such as `staging (EKS us-east-1)` for `arn:aws:eks:us-east-1:123456789012:cluster/staging` | | Other contexts | Shown as named | | `Your bill:` | What turns freed capacity into savings, from the node autoscaler [`nodes`](/next/reference/cli/#nodes-autoscaler) finds ([what it says](/next/reference/cli/#scan-columns-more)) | | `History:` | The Prometheus read, how much was replayed, and others found. See [History from Prometheus](/next/reference/cli/#scan-history). | | STATE | What the workload is doing now, and in brackets how Napwake is involved (below) | | STATE `idle` | No activity for `--idle-after`, or a managed workload’s own wait, so Napwake would pause it now | | BECAUSE | The evidence for the state, then facts such as when it was last deployed, if a week or more ago | | BECAUSE `won't pause: PVCs deleted on scale-down` | A StatefulSet whose `persistentVolumeClaimRetentionPolicy.whenScaled` is `Delete` | | COST/MO | What its requests cost a month while running. See [Prices](/next/reference/cli/#scan-prices). | | FREED THIS MONTH | Live workloads only. What Napwake freed pausing it since the 1st (`status.cost.freedThisMonth`). | | Its rates | Those Napwake uses for the workload | | SAVED | The part of that which came off the bill as nodes scaled down (`savedThisMonth`) | | COULD SLEEP, in dry-run | The hours Napwake measured it would have slept, since dry-run started. BECAUSE says when. | | COULD SLEEP, unmanaged | From the replay. The running hours it would have slept, waiting `--idle-after` after each activity, or longer after a pause made in use. | | WAKES | How often activity would have woken it, making whoever caused it wait. Measured in dry-run, replayed otherwise. | | COULD FREE/MO | What pausing would free a month, for workloads Napwake doesn’t pause yet. Never called a saving. | | COULD FREE/MO, in dry-run | What Napwake measured, the figure BECAUSE gives | | COULD FREE/MO, unmanaged | The replay’s estimate | | Money | Whole dollars, as in `status`. `<$1` is above zero but rounds to nothing. `$0` is none. | | `Prices:` | At the end, a summary of what costs were priced at ([Prices](/next/reference/cli/#scan-prices)) | | In STATE’s brackets | What it means | | -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `unmanaged` | Napwake isn’t involved | | `dry-run` | Napwake measures it, and never pauses it | | `live` | Napwake pauses it while idle | | `protected` | Its namespace is labelled `napwake.dev/protected`, so Napwake won’t manage it | | `paused (unmanaged)` | Someone scaled it to zero. It costs nothing, but nothing wakes it on a request. | Run in a terminal, the scan also writes the report as a web page and opens it in your browser. It’s one self-contained file to email or attach, and it prints cleanly to PDF. See [The web report](/next/reference/cli/#scan-report). 
```bash
# Another cluster in your kubeconfig
kubectl napwake scan --context staging


# Some namespaces, as JSON or YAML, at your own prices
kubectl napwake scan -n preview-42 -n preview-918 -o json --cpu-price 0.045 --memory-price 0.006


# A month of history from a Thanos that holds many clusters, with a 2h idleAfter
kubectl napwake scan --window 30d --idle-after 2h --prometheus-url https://thanos.example.com \
  --prometheus-selector 'cluster="staging"'


# CPU right now only
kubectl napwake scan --window 0


# Save the report to send around
kubectl napwake scan --html workload-scan.html


# The terminal only
kubectl napwake scan --open=false
```
 | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--namespace` `-n` | all you can read | Namespace to scan. Repeat for several. | | `--output` `-o` | `table` | `table`, `json` or `yaml` | | `--window` | `7d` | Prometheus history to replay, such as `7d` or `36h`, up to `366d`. `0` judges from CPU now. | | `--cpu-price` | `0.031` | Your price per vCPU-hour, in dollars, zero or more | | `--memory-price` | `0.004` | Your price per GiB-hour of memory, in dollars, zero or more | The [`scan` reference](/next/reference/cli/#scan) has every flag, JSON field, exit status and permission. It also says how the scan judges idle, reads history and prices workloads. ## Replay the forecast See how Napwake’s [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/) would do on your workloads’ own CPU history, before you trust `autoResume`. It needs Prometheus history, not the operator, and only reads. 
```
kubectl napwake forecast -n preview-42
staging: the forecast replayed over 28 days of CPU history from monitoring/prometheus-operated, times in UTC

NAMESPACE   NAME              FORECAST             READY  COLD  WOKEN FOR NOTHING  WOKEN             WAKES ON
preview-42  api               acts from day one    9/10   1     0                  08:45, 25m ahead  request
preview-42  worker (managed)  acts from Mon 2 Nov  3/10   7     2 (3.5h)           09:45, 20m ahead  query
preview-42  reports           not within 4 weeks   0/6    6     0                  -                 nothing
```
 It runs the operator’s own forecast and decision rules over the history `scan` reads, through the [simulator](/next/concepts/forecasting/#proving-it). | Column | What it says | | ------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | FORECAST | When it would act if the workload were opted in today, having [learned all its history](/next/concepts/forecasting/#learning-from-history) | | FORECAST `acts from day one`, or a date | It would act from then | | FORECAST `not within 4 weeks` | A pattern too irregular for the setting | | FORECAST, `(managed)` | A workload Napwake manages shows its own forecast, from `status.prediction` | | `(managed)` `acting`, `learning`, or a date | Its forecast’s state, or when it’s projected to act | | `(managed)` `not within 4 weeks` | It’s earning its confidence, or relearning | | `(managed)` `not learning while paused:` and why | Paused with nothing to see its demand, such as `no Service routes requests to it` | | READY | Mornings it had the workload up before its first use | | COLD | Mornings whose first use found it paused and woke it | | WOKEN FOR NOTHING | Wakes ahead of demand that didn’t come, and how long they kept it up | | WOKEN | When it usually woke it | | WAKES ON | What wakes it once paused (below) | The forecast learns all its history as the operator does for a new workload. [Name a managed workload](#one-workloads-forecast) for its whole forecast, and the fix from `status.prediction.message`. READY to WOKEN replay the history’s last half under `autoResume`, from a forecast that learned only the first half. Having learned less, it’s a little less ready than opting in now would be. A workload counts as used when its CPU is above `--cpu-threshold`, as the activity clock counts it. Use seen only in requests or activity annotations isn’t in Prometheus. | WAKES ON | When | | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `request` | A request through the [doorman](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) would wake it | | `request`, needs | A Service with a cluster IP that selects its pods on a TCP port | | `request`, also needs | The Service keeps traffic cluster-wide | | `request`, behind GKE NEGs | Only with the doorman’s [forwarder](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs) on | | `query` | Its ManagedWorkload has a [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused), so only for a managed workload | | `request, query` | Both | | `people` | It [asks before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking), and the doorman would route its Services. Only people wake it, and the forecast learns its paused hours from whether they did | | `people`, not routed | One the doorman wouldn’t route is `nothing` or `query` | | `people, query` | The same, with a query too | | `nothing` | People wake it by hand, and the forecast doesn’t learn the hours it’s paused | | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--namespace` `-n` | all you can read | Namespace to replay. Repeat for several. | | `--window` | `28d` | History to read, from `2d` to `34d`. The operator learns up to four weeks. | | `--forecast` | `balanced` | How soon the forecast acts for unmanaged workloads. See [Forecast setting](/next/concepts/forecasting/#forecast-setting). | | `--timezone` | the operator’s | The zone whose hours and weekdays the forecast learns, and times show in | | `--output` `-o` | `table` | `table`, `json` or `yaml`. JSON and YAML give every workload’s days. | The [`forecast` reference](/next/reference/cli/#forecast) has every flag and JSON field, how it reads history, and what would wake a workload. ### One workload’s forecast Name a workload, as `api` or `statefulset/postgres`, to see its forecast. Its own forecast needs the operator, and no history. 
```
kubectl napwake forecast api -n preview-42
staging (EKS us-east-1): the forecast of preview-42/api, times in Europe/London

Napwake's forecast, from ManagedWorkload api: acting
  acting, on 4 weeks of CPU history and what it has seen since: its forecasts of the last day were 91% right, and it acts from 75%, so it holds off pauses before hours it expects to be busy and wakes the workload 15 minutes ahead of them
  Phase:       daily Active, weekly Active
  Hours seen:  24 of the 24 hours of the day, 168 of the 168 hours of the week
  Learned:     790 hours: 672 from its CPU history in Prometheus, and 118 live in the 5 days Napwake has managed it
  Confidence:  91% right over the last day, 88% over the last week; it acts from 75%
  Acts from:   now
  Busy hours:  Thu 22 Oct 09:00–18:00, up to 64% of its CPU requests
  autoResume:  on: it holds off pauses before the hours it expects busy, and wakes the workload 15 minutes ahead of them

Replayed over its last 28 days of CPU history, from monitoring/prometheus-operated:
  Opted in today: acts from day one, at a confidence of 75%.

From Thu 8 Oct to Thu 22 Oct, with autoResume, from a forecast that learned only the history before; while it's paused, a request through the doorman wakes it:
DAY         FIRST USE  FOUND IT  WOKEN AT  FORECAST  WOKEN FOR NOTHING
Thu 8 Oct   09:12      ready     08:45     acting    -
Fri 9 Oct   09:20      ready     08:45     acting    -
Sat 10 Oct  -          -         -         acting    -
...

Ready on 9 of 10 mornings, woken 25m ahead; it never woke it for nothing.
```
 For a managed workload it first shows Napwake’s own forecast. That’s from `status.prediction` and the state saved there, as the operator restores it. Then, for any workload, the replay. | Line | What it says | | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | First line | `acting`, `learning`, `relearning` or `not learning`, as in `status`’s FORECAST | | Under it | The operator’s `status.prediction.message`, what it waits for and why | | Phase | Each season’s phase, daily and weekly, `Observing`, `Suggesting` or `Active`. See [Phase Lifecycle](/next/concepts/forecasting/#phase-lifecycle). | | Hours seen | Hours of the day seen since it started or last relearned, and hours of the week | | To act | It needs all 24 hours of the day | | Learned | Hours of demand learned | | Learned, from history | How many came from Prometheus history when Napwake began managing it (`status.prediction.history`) | | Learned, live | How many it learned since, out of how long it’s been managed | | **History** | With no history learned, the operator’s reason | | Confidence | How right the last day’s and last week’s forecasts were, and the confidence it acts from | | Acts from `now` | It’s acting | | Acts from a time | When it’s expected to start (`status.prediction.startsActing`), if the coming days repeat the last week | | Acts from `not known until it has seen every hour of the day` | While it’s still observing | | Acts from `not known until it has scored a day of its forecasts` | While it’s still observing | | Acts from `not expected within 4 weeks` | A pattern too irregular for its setting | | Busy hours | Hours of the next 24 it expects demand in, at or above `cpuThreshold` | | Busy hours, peak | The most it expects, as a share of the CPU requested | | Busy hours, effect | It holds off a pause before each. With `autoResume`, it wakes the workload 15 minutes ahead. | | Busy hours, none | Until it’s acting | | autoResume | Whether it’s on, and what that changes | | autoResume, off | How to turn it on (below) | | Its state | Why the plugin couldn’t read the saved state, such as a plugin of another version than the operator | | Its state, then | The lines read from it are left out | | Replayed | When the forecast would act if the workload were opted in today | | The days | The history’s last half, one row a day | | Each day | Its first use, how it found the workload, when it woke it, and whether it was acting | To turn on `autoResume` for a workload opted in with the label, annotate it `napwake.dev/auto-resume: "true"`. For a ManagedWorkload written by hand, which annotations don’t change, set `spec.idlePolicy.autoResume: true`. The [`forecast` reference](/next/reference/cli/#forecast-one-edge-cases) has how it finds the workload, gaps, and its exit status. ## See what Napwake is doing See every workload Napwake manages on one screen, from their ManagedWorkloads and events. 
```
kubectl napwake status
staging (EKS us-east-1): Napwake manages 4 workloads.
  1 paused, 2 running, 1 resuming; 1 in dry-run, never paused: pausing it would have freed $7 since Oct 1
  Your bill: drops only as Karpenter removes nodes (it consolidates when underutilized after 1m)
  Forecast time zone: Europe/London
  Freed $42 this month, and saved $9 of it.

Needs attention:
  Pausing freed $42 this month, but only $9 of it came off the bill: nodes aren't scaling down.
  kubectl napwake nodes shows why.
  preview-7/worker   GitOpsConflict   Argo CD set the replicas from Git, undoing the pause; ...
  preview-7/worker   Stuck            resuming for 25m: waiting for preview-42/statefulset/postgres

  NAMESPACE    WORKLOAD               STATE               FOR   LAST ACTIVITY      NEXT                                        FORECAST                    FREED THIS MONTH   SAVED   WOULD FREE
  preview-42   deployment/api         paused              3h    request, 4h ago    wakes on a request or activity              acting                      $12                $9      -
  preview-42   statefulset/postgres   running             5h    cpu, 2h ago        held awake while preview-7/worker uses it   -                           $30                $0      -
  preview-7    deployment/web         running (dry-run)   2h    rollout, 15m ago   would pause in 45m                          learning, acts from Oct 8   -                  -       $7
  preview-7    deployment/worker      resuming            25m   -                  -                                           -                           <$1                $0      -

Recent pauses and wakes:
  30m ago   preview-7/worker   scaled up to 2 replicas by argocd-controller outside Napwake; counted as activity
  3h ago    preview-42/api     paused
  5h ago    preview-42/api     request on Service api, waking
```
 In a terminal, the states are in colour, each beside a door, so they read without the colour too: | STATE | Colour | Door | | ---------- | ------ | ---- | | `paused` | Blue | Shut | | `running` | Green | Open | | `resuming` | Yellow | Ajar | What needs attention is red, and the table’s header and dry-run grey. Set `NO_COLOR` for no colour. Piped or redirected, as scripts read it, the output is plain text, with no colour and no doors. Doors need a UTF-8 locale. They aren’t drawn in Chinese, Japanese or Korean ones, where terminals may draw them two characters wide. | Line or column | What it says | | ------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Summary | How many workloads are in each phase | | Summary, dry-run | How many are in dry-run, with what pausing them would have freed since the first started measuring | | That figure | What dry-run measured (`status.dryRun.estimatedFreed`), the would-be pause under way included | | `Your bill:` | From the node autoscaler [`nodes`](/next/reference/cli/#nodes-autoscaler) finds, as `scan` says it | | `Forecast time zone:` | The zone the [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/#wall-clock-alignment) counts hours in, from each `status.prediction.timeZone` | | That zone | The operator’s `timezone`. When it’s UTC, the default, it says how to set your users’ zone. | | Why set it | So `autoResume` follows your users’ clock through daylight saving | | `Freed … this month` | What pausing live workloads freed this month, its requests at list price | | `… and saved` | How much of that came off the bill as nodes scaled down | | Saved unmeasured | It says why, once per reason | | `Needs attention:` | What keeps Napwake from its job ([every case](/next/reference/cli/#needs-attention)). `kubectl describe managedworkload` has the rest. | | Nodes not scaling down | The first line, pointing at `kubectl napwake nodes` | | When | Pausing freed at least a dollar this month, and under half was saved | | Counting | Only workloads whose savings are measured | | WORKLOAD | `kind/name`, with its ManagedWorkload’s name in brackets if you wrote one under another name | | STATE, FOR | Its state, and for how long | | LAST ACTIVITY | Its last activity, and where it came from, such as `cpu, 2h ago` | | LAST ACTIVITY, none since its clock started | What started it, which isn’t activity ([the three](/next/reference/cli/#the-last-activity-column)) | | NEXT | When it pauses, or what holds it awake or wakes it ([every value](/next/reference/cli/#the-next-column)). One that asks reads `wakes on a click, a wake request or activity` | | FORECAST | Its [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/#when-it-acts)’s state ([every value](/next/reference/cli/#the-forecast-column)) | | FREED THIS MONTH, SAVED | What pausing it freed this month, and what of that was saved | | `-` | It was only ever in dry-run | | `unknown` | What’s saved can’t be measured | | Earlier months | Totals last brought up to date in an earlier month count as none | | In dry-run now | A workload keeps what its pauses freed and saved before | | WOULD FREE | What pausing would have freed since dry-run started measuring it | | WOULD FREE, shown | When any workload is in dry-run | | WOULD FREE `$0` or `-` | `$0` until a would-be pause has. `-` if it isn’t in dry-run. | | `Turned away while asleep` | Under the table, the workloads that ask and turned requests away in their current or last pause. One reads `preview-42/web 312 requests during the pause from 3h ago`. Many mean something automated calls it ([What it turns away](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-it-turns-away)) | | `Recent pauses and wakes:` | Newest first, as far back as the cluster keeps events, or `--since` ([more](/next/reference/cli/#recent-pauses-and-wakes)) | Freed capacity is only money once a node autoscaler removes its nodes. Money is in whole dollars or `<$1`, as in [`scan`](#scan-a-cluster). 
```bash
kubectl napwake status --context staging -n preview-42
kubectl napwake status --since 1h -o json
```
 | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--namespace` `-n` | all you can read | Namespace to show. Repeat for several. | | `--output` `-o` | `table` | `table`, `json` or `yaml` | | `--since` | `24h` | How far back to list pauses and wakes | | `--html` | | Also write the status as a web page to this file, or without one, to a temporary file | The [`status` reference](/next/reference/cli/#status) has every flag, every value each column can show, and permissions. ### Share it as a web page 
```bash
kubectl napwake status --html              # opens it in your browser
kubectl napwake status --html status.html  # saves it, to send around
```
 `--html` writes the same status as a web page, for a team or a manager. It writes to the file you give, or a new temporary file. Either is readable only by you, since it names the cluster’s workloads. In a terminal, it opens in your browser, as the [`scan`](#scan-a-cluster) report does, unless `--open=false`. Otherwise it prints where the page is. The terminal table, or `-o json`, prints as well. | The page holds | | | ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The summary | With your bill and the forecast’s time zone | | Money | What pausing freed this month, and what of that was saved | | Dry-run | What dry-run measured pausing would have freed | | Needs attention | What needs attention | | Workloads | Every workload, in a table to sort and filter | | Each workload | Its state and for how long, last activity, what’s next, and what it freed or would free | | Pauses and wakes | Every one since `--since`, with its cause, at its time in UTC | | Nothing from elsewhere | It loads nothing, so it works offline and attached to an email | ## See why nodes don’t scale down Pausing frees what a workload’s pods requested. It comes off the bill only once a node autoscaler removes their node. `nodes` shows what’s in the way, and needs no operator. 
```
kubectl napwake nodes
staging (EKS us-east-1): 3 nodes
Node autoscaler: Karpenter (NodePool batch consolidates only empty nodes after 1m; NodePool default consolidates when underutilized after 1m)
Napwake's 2 paused workloads would request 2.0 vCPU and 4.0 GiB of memory if awake.
  in pool batch: 0.5 vCPU and 1.0 GiB
  in pool default: 1.5 vCPU and 3.0 GiB

  NODE      POOL      INSTANCE TYPE   CPU REQUESTED     MEMORY REQUESTED   UTILISATION   BLOCKED BY
  batch-1   batch     c5.large        0.1 of 1.9 vCPU   0.3 of 3.0 GiB     10%           NodePool consolidates only empty nodes
  quiet-1   default   m5.large        0.2 of 1.9 vCPU   1.0 of 7.0 GiB     14%           PDB allows no disruption (shop/db)
  busy-1    default   m5.xlarge       3.0 of 3.9 vCPU   8.0 of 14.0 GiB    77%           -

Next steps:
  1. A NodePool consolidates only empty nodes, which a node running anything never is. Set spec.disruption.consolidationPolicy: WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized.
     For example: NodePool batch consolidates only empty nodes, and this one runs 1 pod
  2. A PodDisruptionBudget allows no disruptions, so its pods can't be moved. Raise maxUnavailable, lower minAvailable, or run more replicas than it requires.
     For example: shop/db: PodDisruptionBudget db allows no disruptions
  See https://docs.napwake.dev/guides/cluster-autoscaler/
  To have pausing empty whole nodes, put non-production workloads on a node pool of their own: https://docs.napwake.dev/guides/node-pools/
```
 | Line or column | What it says | | ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `Node autoscaler:` | What scales the nodes. `status` and `scan` say the same in their `Your bill:` line. See [how it’s found](/next/reference/cli/#nodes-autoscaler). | | `Napwake's 2 paused workloads would request …` | What paused workloads would request if they woke, once Napwake is installed | | `in pool …` | The same by node pool their pods ran in, the pools that would need room | | NODE | Each node, least used first | | POOL | Karpenter NodePool, EKS node group, GKE node pool or AKS agent pool | | CPU REQUESTED, MEMORY REQUESTED | What its running pods request, of what it can allocate | | DaemonSet pods | They count, as they do for the autoscaler | | UTILISATION | The larger of the two, as Cluster Autoscaler measures it against its scale-down threshold | | BLOCKED BY | Why the autoscaler won’t remove it, however little is used ([every reason](/next/reference/cli/#the-blocked-by-column)) | | BLOCKED BY, how many | The first two reasons. `-o json` has all. | | `Next steps:` | How to clear each kind of reason shown, with one example | | `Next steps:`, links | The [node autoscaler guide](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/) and the [node pools guide](/next/guides/node-pools/) | The paused workloads’ requests are what each pause recorded it freed. A workload whose pods ran in several pools is shared between them, by how many ran in each. Pauses that recorded no pool count in all, not by pool. A node with nothing in BLOCKED BY that stays is still in use, shared by paused and busy workloads. [Node Pools for Real Savings](/next/guides/node-pools/) gives non-prod workloads nodes that pausing empties. 
```bash
kubectl napwake nodes --context staging -o json
```
 | Flag | Default | What it does | | --------------- | ------- | ------------------------------------------------- | | `--output` `-o` | `table` | `table`, `json` or `yaml` | | `--timeout` | `1m` | How long to wait for the cluster before giving up | The [`nodes` reference](/next/reference/cli/#nodes) has every reason a node is blocked, how it finds the node autoscaler, and permissions. ## Pause a workload Pause a workload now, before its idle clock runs out, such as a preview at the end of the day. 
```
kubectl napwake pause my-api -n staging
pausing staging/my-api...
staging/my-api is Paused after 4s; a request to it, or kubectl napwake wake, wakes it
```
 It’s an ordinary pause, the same as an idle one. A request through the [doorman](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/), [activity](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations), `autoResume` or [`wake`](#wake-a-workload) wakes it. With [`ask`](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking), a person’s click on its asleep page does, not any request, and `pause` says so. It works with or without an `idlePolicy`. On a ManagedWorkload Napwake hasn’t taken on yet, such as one created a moment ago, it waits until Napwake has. [Pause and Resume](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now) says exactly what Napwake does with the request. | Phase or case | What happens | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `Running` | It pauses, and `pause` waits until it’s `Paused` | | `Idle` | It pauses now | | `Resuming` | It pauses once it’s up | | `Pausing` | Already pausing. `pause` waits until it’s `Paused`. | | `Paused` | Already paused. `pause` returns at once. | | dry-run | Never paused. Napwake counts a would-be pause, and `pause` says so. | | Scaled to zero outside Napwake | Off already. `pause` says so, and exits 0. | | Awake workloads depend on it | `pause` exits 1 with Napwake’s reason | | A `dependsOn` cycle runs through it | The same | | Its namespace is protected | The same | | It doesn’t exist, is labelled `napwake.dev/ignore`, or another ManagedWorkload manages it | The same | | A GitOps tool undid its last pause | Asking does override the hour Napwake waits | | Not `Paused` within `--timeout`, with no reason from Napwake | `pause` exits 1 with the phase, and the `kubectl describe` command that says why | Napwake pauses it by the same rules as an idle pause, so it refuses in the same cases. 
```bash
# Pause it even if the forecast expects it to be busy soon, without asking
kubectl napwake pause my-api -n staging --yes


# Request the pause and return straight away
kubectl napwake pause my-api -n staging --wait=false
```
 NAME is found as for [`wake`](#wake-a-workload). | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--namespace` `-n` | your kubeconfig context’s | Namespace of the workload | | `--wait` | `true` | Wait until it’s Paused, or Napwake says why not | | `--yes` `-y` | `false` | Pause even if the forecast expects it busy within the hour, without asking | | `--clear-hold` | `false` | Clear a `napwake.dev/active-until` hold, on its ManagedWorkload or the workload, without asking | | `--timeout` | `5m` | How long to wait, cluster calls included. It starts again after you answer a question. | The [`pause` reference](/next/reference/cli/#pause) has its questions about the forecast and holds, the GitOps warning, and permissions. ## Wake a workload Wake a paused workload now, or keep one awake a while, such as for a demo. 
```
kubectl napwake wake my-api -n staging
waking staging/my-api...
staging/my-api is Running after 23s
```
 `wake` asks Napwake to wake the workload now, whatever its [wake setting](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#the-wake-setting). It sets a fresh `napwake.dev/wake-requested` value on the ManagedWorkload, as any tool can ([Wake now](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now)). A paused workload wakes, with the workloads it [depends on](/next/concepts/dependencies/). A running one has its idle clock restarted. `wake` then waits for Napwake’s answer, in the `WakeRequest` condition, and until it’s Running. Another tool asking meanwhile replaces `wake`’s token with its own, and Napwake’s answer to that one stands for both. To wake a preview from a pull request’s workflow or a chat bot, see [Public preview environments](/next/guides/preview-environments/#waking-it-from-tooling). | Phase or case | What happens | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `Paused` | It wakes, and `wake` waits until it’s `Running` | | `Pausing` | The pause is [called off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off), and `wake` waits until it’s `Running` | | `Resuming` | It’s already waking | | `Idle` | Its idle clock restarts, so it stays up | | `Running` | Already running. Its idle clock restarts, and `wake` returns once Napwake has answered. | | dry-run, not paused | Never paused. Its idle clock restarts, and `wake` returns once Napwake has answered. | | It doesn’t exist, is labelled `napwake.dev/ignore`, or another ManagedWorkload manages it | Napwake isn’t managing it, so `wake` refuses before asking, saying why | | Napwake answers that it won’t wake it, such as when it’s scaled to zero outside Napwake | `wake` exits 1 at once with Napwake’s reason ([every reason](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now)) | | Not `Running` within `--timeout` | `wake` exits 1 with its phase, whether Napwake took the request up, and the `kubectl describe` command that says why. Not taken up at all usually means its operator isn’t running | | A slow start | The timeout covers the answer and `Running`. A request made as the workload pauses is answered at once, and calls the pause off. One made as it wakes is answered only once the wake finishes. For a start over 5 minutes, raise it, such as `--timeout 10m` | 
```bash
# Keep it awake for the next two hours, e.g. for a demo
kubectl napwake wake my-api -n staging --for 2h


# Request the wake and return straight away
kubectl napwake wake my-api -n staging --wait=false
```
 NAME is the workload as `status` shows it, such as `api` or `statefulset/postgres`, or its ManagedWorkload’s name. A bare name can match more than one ManagedWorkload. A Deployment and a StatefulSet can share it, or one ManagedWorkload can be named after a workload another manages. Then `wake` lists them, and asks for `kind/name`, which matches only workloads. | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `--namespace` `-n` | your kubeconfig context’s | Namespace of the workload | | `--for` | | Also keep it awake this long, by setting `napwake.dev/active-until` on its ManagedWorkload | | `--wait` | `true` | Wait for Napwake’s answer, then until it’s Running | | `--timeout` | `5m` | How long to wait, cluster calls included | The [`wake` reference](/next/reference/cli/#wake) has how `--for` treats an existing hold, and permissions. ## Show a workload’s dependencies See what Napwake holds awake and wakes with a workload, and what depends on it. 
```
kubectl napwake deps postgres -n preview-42
preview-42/postgres (StatefulSet, Paused)
Depends on:
  nothing
Depended on by:
  preview-42/api      learned from PGHOST                   Running
  preview-42/worker   declared in dependsOn, waitForReady   Paused
Learned links come from the dependent's environment or its requests; napwake.dev/ignore-dependencies drops one.
```
 `deps` reads a ManagedWorkload’s links both ways, in any namespace. Each says where it comes from, a `dependsOn` or what Napwake [learned](/next/concepts/dependencies/#learned-dependencies) from the dependent’s environment or a wake. Each also says what the other workload is doing now. One Napwake doesn’t manage shows as `not managed`. NAME is found as for [`wake`](#wake-a-workload). | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | | `--namespace` `-n` | your kubeconfig context’s | Namespace of the workload | | `--timeout` | `1m` | How long to wait for the cluster before giving up | Permissions are in the [`deps` reference](/next/reference/cli/#deps). ## Enable a workload End dry-run for a workload, so Napwake starts pausing it while idle. A workload in dry-run is measured but never paused. 
```
kubectl napwake enable checkout-api -n preview-42
deployment/checkout-api: dry-run ended, Napwake will pause it while idle
```
 | Dry-run from | What `enable` does | | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The workload’s own `napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"`, or its namespace’s | Sets the workload’s own `napwake.dev/dry-run` to `"false"` | | The cluster default | The same. It reads the ManagedWorkload to tell, so this counts too. | | `spec.dryRun` on a ManagedWorkload you wrote | Sets `spec.dryRun: false` | Setting the annotation is for a workload opted in with the `napwake.dev/managed` label. The workload’s own wins over its namespace’s and the cluster default. `enable` doesn’t wait. Napwake pauses the workload once its clock runs out. 
```bash
kubectl napwake enable statefulset/postgres -n preview-42
kubectl napwake enable --all -n preview-42
```
 A bare name is looked up as a Deployment and as a StatefulSet. If both have it, `enable` lists them and asks for the kind. Prefix `deployment/` or `statefulset/` to be exact. | Flag | Default | What it does | | ------------------ | ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--namespace` `-n` | your kubeconfig context’s | Namespace of the workload | | `--all` | `false` | Enable every workload in the namespace | | `--force` | `false` | Change the cluster even when a GitOps tool applies the setting from Git | | `--timeout` | `1m` | How long to wait for the cluster before giving up | The [`enable` reference](/next/reference/cli/#enable) has when it changes nothing, what `--all` does, GitOps tools and Helm, and permissions. Next * [Opting in →The label, and every setting.](/next/guides/opt-in/) * [Dry run →Measure what pausing would free, without pausing.](/next/guides/dry-run/) * [Troubleshooting →Common problems, and how to fix them.](/next/operations/troubleshooting/)

# Quickstart

> Take one workload from idle and costing money to paused while idle and woken when used. You measure it first, so nothing pauses until you say so.

1. [1A sample workload](#1-a-sample-workload) 2. [2See what's idle](#2-see-whats-idle) 3. [3Opt it in, measuring firstdry-run](#3-opt-it-in-measuring-first) 4. [4Watch it measuredry-run](#4-watch-it-measure) 5. [5Let it pause](#5-let-it-pause) 6. [6Wake it with a request](#6-wake-it-with-a-request) 7. [7Stop managing itnot managed](#7-stop-managing-it) Before you start You need Napwake [installed](/next/getting-started/installation/), the [kubectl plugin](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/) and metrics-server. Napwake reads CPU from it, and won’t pause a workload whose CPU it can’t read. 
```bash
kubectl get apiservice v1beta1.metrics.k8s.io
```
 If the Metrics API is missing or not `Available`, install metrics-server: | Cluster | metrics-server | | -------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | GKE, AKS | Runs by default | | minikube | Off when new. `minikube addons enable metrics-server` | | EKS | Off. The `metrics-server` add-on, or the manifest | | kind | Off. The manifest, then the patch | | Others | The manifest | 
```bash
kubectl apply -f https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/metrics-server/releases/latest/download/components.yaml
```
 On kind, kubelet certificates aren’t signed for their addresses. The patch tells metrics-server not to verify them. Local test clusters only. 
```bash
kubectl patch deployment metrics-server -n kube-system --type=json \
  -p '[{"op":"add","path":"/spec/template/spec/containers/0/args/-","value":"--kubelet-insecure-tls"}]'
```
 `kubectl top pods -A` shows numbers within a minute or so once it works. If a workload still won’t pause, see [Troubleshooting](/next/operations/troubleshooting/#workload-is-idle-but-not-being-paused). ## 1. A sample workload Skip it to use your own. 
```bash
kubectl create namespace dev
kubectl create deployment my-api --image=nginx:1.27-alpine --replicas=2 -n dev
kubectl set resources deployment my-api -n dev --requests=cpu=100m,memory=64Mi
kubectl expose deployment my-api -n dev --port=80
kubectl rollout status deployment/my-api -n dev
```
 CheckpointAwake `my-api` runs 2 replicas, with a Service on port 80. ## 2. See what’s idle 
```bash
kubectl napwake scan -n dev
```
 The scan shows whether each workload is idle and why, and what it costs while it runs. Leave out `-n` to scan every namespace. CheckpointAwake `my-api` is active, as Napwake would treat any workload deployed in the last hour. ## 3. Opt it in, measuring first 
```bash
kubectl annotate deployment my-api -n dev napwake.dev/dry-run=true napwake.dev/idle-after=5m
kubectl label deployment my-api -n dev napwake.dev/managed=true
```
 The annotations are its settings, set first so it starts in dry-run, measured and never paused. `idle-after=5m` keeps the example quick. The default is an hour. The label opts it in. In real use, they go in the manifest or Helm values, or on the namespace for everything in it. See [Opting in](/next/guides/opt-in/), Kustomize included. CheckpointAwake · dry-run Napwake creates a ManagedWorkload for it: 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkloads -n dev
```
 
```plaintext
NAME     PHASE     FREED   SAVED   WOULD FREE   AGE
my-api   Running   $0.00   $0.00   $0.00        10s
```
 ## 4. Watch it measure 
```bash
kubectl napwake status -n dev
```
 For `my-api`, STATE reads `running (dry-run)`. LAST ACTIVITY says when it was last active and from what. Until there’s been any, it reads `none since Napwake began managing it`. NEXT says when it would pause. 
```bash
kubectl describe managedworkload my-api -n dev
```
 After `idle-after` with no activity, STATE reads `idle (dry-run)` and an event says it would have paused. Nothing is scaled down. Mark it active, as a developer portal or your tooling would, to end the would-be pause: 
```bash
kubectl annotate deployment my-api -n dev --overwrite napwake.dev/last-activity=$(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ)
kubectl napwake status -n dev
kubectl get managedworkloads -n dev
```
 `status` now leads with what dry-run measured. WOULD FREE is what the would-be pauses would have freed. CheckpointAwake · dry-run WOULD FREE reads `$0.00`. A few minutes of a sample this small free less than a cent. ## 5. Let it pause Applied by Argo CD or Flux? Skip this for the sample. A tool that applies `replicas` from Git undoes every pause, and Napwake waits an hour to pause again (`GitOpsConflict`). Before you enable, have it leave replicas to Napwake, as in [GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/). The scan’s notes list affected workloads. Those with an HPA are already set. 
```bash
kubectl napwake enable my-api -n dev
```
 It sets `napwake.dev/dry-run` to `"false"`. After `idle-after` with no activity, which NEXT counts down, Napwake scales `my-api` to zero and remembers its 2 replicas. CheckpointAsleep 
```bash
kubectl get deployment my-api -n dev
# READY: 0/0
```
 ## 6. Wake it with a request 
```bash
kubectl run curl --rm -it --image=curlimages/curl:8.7.1 -n dev --restart=Never -- curl -s http://my-api
```
 The [doorman](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) holds the request and wakes `my-api`. A browser coming through an Ingress or a Gateway gets a waking-up page, which loads the app once it’s up. `kubectl port-forward` goes straight to a pod, so it can’t reach a paused workload. Wake it first: 
```bash
kubectl napwake wake my-api -n dev
```
 CheckpointAwake curl gets its answer once `my-api` is Ready. ## 7. Stop managing it 
```bash
kubectl label deployment my-api -n dev napwake.dev/managed-
```
 CheckpointAwake · not managed `my-api` has no ManagedWorkload. Had it been paused, it would be scaled back to its replicas first, never left switched off. Next * [Opting in →Every setting, namespaces, Kustomize and GitOps.](/next/guides/opt-in/) * [Idle detection →How the activity clock decides.](/next/concepts/idle-detection/) * [Wake on request →How a request wakes a paused workload.](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/)

# Architecture

> Napwake is a Kubernetes operator built on controller-runtime.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    subgraph operator["Operator Deployment"]
        optin["Opt-in reconcilers<br/>Deployments, StatefulSets"]
        mwr["ManagedWorkload reconciler<br/>activity clock, pause and resume,<br/>dependencies, cost"]
        forecast["Forecast engine<br/>one per workload"]
        optin --> mwr
        mwr --- forecast
    end
    subgraph doorman["Doorman Deployment"]
        door["Holds requests to paused workloads,<br/>wakes them, passes requests through"]
    end
    clients["Clients, ingress controllers,<br/>load balancers"] -- "Service of a paused workload" --> door
    door -- "napwake.dev/last-request" --> api
    mwr --> api["Kubernetes API server"]
    mwr --> ms["metrics-server"]
    mwr -.-> prom["Prometheus<br/>optional"]
```
 Napwake is a Kubernetes operator built on [controller-runtime](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/controller-runtime). The [doorman](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) wakes paused workloads on request. ## Reconcilers The operator runs two kinds of reconciliation loop. ### ManagedWorkload reconciler The main one. It watches `ManagedWorkload` CRs and drives each workload through its [lifecycle](/next/concepts/lifecycle/). * A reconcile times out after 2 minutes. * `--max-concurrent-reconciles` workloads are reconciled at once, 8 by default. One slow metrics or Prometheus query doesn’t hold up another workload’s wake. * Each workload’s forecast engine runs in memory. It’s saved to `status.prediction.state` each hour it learns, so it survives operator restarts and leader changes. * Reconciles wait in one queue, so a wake isn’t queued behind every workload’s checks: | Order | Reconciles | | ---------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | First | What wakes a workload. A request the doorman holds, an activity or pause annotation, a scale-up from zero | | Also first | A resume under way | | Then | Other changes | | Last | Periodic checks of awake and paused workloads, with their metrics and Prometheus queries | ### Opt-in reconcilers One for Deployments, one for StatefulSets. Each watches its workloads, their namespaces, and the ManagedWorkloads created from them. It turns the `napwake.dev/managed: "true"` label into a ManagedWorkload, as [Opting in](/next/guides/opt-in/) describes. ## Internal packages | Package | Does | | --------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `internal/controller` | The reconcilers | | `internal/forecast` | Holt-Winters model, phase lifecycle, confidence scoring, anomaly detection | | `internal/signal` | Prometheus activity queries | | `internal/autoscaler` | Finds HPAs and KEDA ScaledObjects. Holds a KEDA one at zero while paused | | `internal/gitops` | Tells from field managers whether a GitOps tool sets the replicas, and how to tell it to leave them | | `internal/lifecycle` | Pause and resume, through the scale subresource | | `internal/discovery` | `kubectl napwake scan` | | `internal/doorman` | The doorman | | `internal/cost` | Rates, node list prices, monthly projection | | `internal/metrics` | Metric definitions, and the K8s Metrics API reader | ## External dependencies | System | Napwake uses it to | | --------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Kubernetes API server | Scale workloads, and route Services’ EndpointSlices to the doorman | | Kubernetes API server, for pricing and dependencies | Read pods, PVCs, nodes’ labels, ConfigMaps, HPAs and KEDA ScaledObjects | | metrics-server, required | Read pod CPU | | Prometheus, optional | Run PromQL activity queries | ## Freed and saved Napwake works at the workload layer, and doesn’t manage nodes. What a pause or scale-down frees comes off the bill once a cluster autoscaler removes nodes. Every 5 minutes, the leader measures what’s [saved](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#how-saved-is-measured), from cached nodes and pods. Every 10 minutes, it exports which node autoscaler runs as `napwake_node_autoscaler`. ## Leader election For high availability, run several operator replicas with `--leader-elect`, controller-runtime’s leader election. Only the leader reconciles, and a standby takes over if it fails. Every doorman replica serves traffic, with no leader. ## Security model * The operator runs as non-root, in a distroless container. * RBAC is scoped to the minimum. See [Data and access](/next/reference/data-and-access/#rbac). * Metrics are served over HTTPS, with authentication, by default. * HTTP/2 is off by default, to mitigate known vulnerabilities. Next * [Lifecycle →The phases a workload moves through.](/next/concepts/lifecycle/) * [Cluster Autoscaler →Turning what a pause frees into savings.](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/)

# Cost tracking

> Napwake tracks what each workload costs, what pausing it frees, and what that has saved, in its ManagedWorkload's status.cost.

CPU 1 vCPU3 vCPU 4 vCPU paused, 1 vCPU spare. **3 vCPU short: saved** Memory 12 GiB 12 GiB paused, 14 GiB spare. **None short: nothing saved** **Fits in spare capacity: freed**Wouldn't fit: freed, and saved The [worked example](#worked-example)'s node pool.
