Next * [Wake on request →A request, or with `ask` a click, still wakes a paused workload, whatever the forecast says.](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) * [Idle detection →The activity clock decides pauses, unless `pauseEarly` is on.](/next/concepts/idle-detection/) * [Replay the forecast →See what it would make of your own Prometheus history.](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#replay-the-forecast)

# Idle detection

> Napwake keeps an activity clock for every ManagedWorkload: the last time anything showed the workload was in use.

The clockidleAfter: 1h The clock: awake from 08:00 to 10:14, asleep from 10:14 to 12:00. Asleep Waking up Awake**09:14 last activity**10:14 pauseAt The [status example](#restarts-and-outages). The last activity was at 09:14. With the default `idleAfter` of an hour, it pauses at 10:14. Napwake keeps an **activity clock** for every ManagedWorkload. It’s the last time anything showed the workload was in use. Any sign of activity resets it. Once the clock is older than `idlePolicy.idleAfter`, Napwake pauses the workload. It’s scaled to zero, keeping its replica count and a resource snapshot. * There’s no learning period. A new workload can pause after its first `idleAfter` without activity. * With `idlePolicy.pauseEarly`, a confident forecast can pause it sooner ([Pausing early](/next/concepts/forecasting/#pausing-early)). managedworkload.yaml 
```yaml
spec:
  idlePolicy:
    idleAfter: 1h        # default
    activity:
      cpuThreshold: 10   # percent of CPU requests, 0.1 to 100; default
```
 Write a threshold that isn’t a whole number as a string, such as `cpuThreshold: "0.5"`. Kubernetes APIs write decimals that way. It suits a workload that idles at a fraction of a percent. ## What counts as activity Every source is checked. The most recent wins, and none has priority. | Source | Counts when | Setup | | --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | CPU | Above `activity.cpuThreshold`% of what the reporting pods request, from 0.1 to 100 | metrics-server | | Deploy | The pod template changes, such as an image, an environment variable, any edit | None | | `napwake.dev/last-activity` | It holds a time newer than the last activity | Your tooling | | `napwake.dev/active-until` | It holds a time in the future. A hold, not an activity time | Your tooling | | `napwake.dev/last-request` | It holds a time newer than the last activity | The [doorman](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/), when it holds a request for the paused workload | | Prometheus | A query in `activity.prometheus` returns above zero | `--prometheus-url` ([Prometheus activity](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/)) | * A change to the replica count isn’t a deploy, so Napwake’s own pause and resume never look like activity. * Memory isn’t a source. It stays allocated whether the workload is used or not. ### Activity annotations Set either on the ManagedWorkload or its target, as an RFC 3339 time. 
```bash
# Record activity now, e.g. from a developer portal when someone opens an environment
kubectl annotate deployment my-api -n staging --overwrite \
  napwake.dev/last-activity="$(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ)"


# Keep the workload awake until a time, e.g. during a demo
kubectl annotate deployment my-api -n staging --overwrite \
  napwake.dev/active-until="2026-10-03T18:00:00Z"
```
 [`kubectl napwake wake`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#wake-a-workload) wakes it through [`napwake.dev/wake-requested`](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now) instead, and waits until it’s Running. * A `last-activity` time in the future counts as now. For a hold, use `active-until`, which says when it ends. * Annotations only keep a workload awake or wake it. They never pause it. * A value that isn’t RFC 3339, such as epoch seconds or a space for the `T`, is ignored everywhere. Each gets one `InvalidActivityAnnotation` warning event on the ManagedWorkload, naming the annotation and where it is. ## The decision Each check runs these steps in order. The first that holds decides. | Step | If | Then | | ---- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | 1 | CPU or a configured query [can’t be read](#when-a-source-cant-be-read) | Stay awake | | 2 | `active-until` is in the future | Stay awake | | 3 | The last activity was under `idleAfter` ago, or longer after [a pause made in use](#paused-while-in-use) | Stay awake, unless the forecast pauses it early (below) | | 4 | The [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/) is confident (`DailyActive` or later, `dailyPhase: Active`) and predicts above `cpuThreshold` in this hour or the next | Stay awake, with `IdleVetoed` | | 5 | An awake workload [depends on](/next/concepts/dependencies/) this one, or `dependsOn` forms a cycle | Stay awake, with `HeldByDependents` or `DependencyCycle` | | 6 | None of these | `Idle`, then [paused](/next/guides/pause/), with an `IdleDetected` event. Or `PausedEarly`, naming the hours forecast quiet | With `pauseEarly`, step 3 goes on to step 4 as if the clock had run out, when: * the last activity was at least 20 minutes ago, * the forecast is confident that every hour until `idleAfter` runs out is quiet, and at least this hour and the next, * and a request would wake the workload through the doorman once it’s paused. While step 4 holds a pause off, `IdleVetoed` is `True` with reason `ForecastExpectsDemand`, its message naming the hour. An `IdleVetoed` event marks each veto’s start. After an hour far quieter than forecast, as on a holiday, the forecast doubts what it expects. For the next hour it holds nothing back ([Anomaly detection](/next/concepts/forecasting/#anomaly-detection)). It never blocks a pause until it’s confident, so a workload with no history is left to the clock. | Case | What changes | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | At zero replicas, not by Napwake | Not checked. It’s already off, and [left that way](/next/guides/pause/#workloads-already-at-zero) | | No `idlePolicy` | None of this runs. It pauses only when someone asks | | A [pause request](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now) (`kubectl napwake pause`) | The clock runs out at once | | Skipped for a request | Steps 1 and 3, and the hour after a GitOps tool undid its last pause | | Still holding a request up | Steps 2 and 5. Step 4 too, unless the request has `napwake.dev/pause-overrides-forecast: "true"`, as `kubectl napwake pause --yes` sets | | A request held back | It’s answered with the reason | | `dryRun: true` | `Idle`, with the `IdleDetected` or `PausedEarly` event prefixed `[dry-run]`, but nothing changes. Activity sets it back to `Running` ([Dry run](/next/guides/dry-run/)) | ## Paused while in use Napwake can’t see requests to an awake workload. One in steady light use, under its CPU threshold, can look idle and be paused while people use it. The next request wakes it, with a cold start. A pause is wrong when the workload’s own use undoes it soon after it begins. Soon means within `idleAfter` or 15 minutes, whichever is shorter. Any of these undoes it: * a request, or a person’s click for a workload that [asks first](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking) * a [wake request](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now) * an activity annotation One that comes while the workload is still pausing [calls the pause off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off). That pause was wrong too. After a wrong pause, the idle clock waits longer before the next. Each wrong pause in a row doubles the wait, up to `idleAfter` plus an hour: | Wrong pauses in a row | Wait with `idleAfter: 5m` | Wait with `idleAfter: 1h` | | --------------------- | ------------------------- | ------------------------- | | 0 | 5 minutes | 1 hour | | 1 | 10 minutes | 2 hours | | 2 | 20 minutes | 2 hours | | 3 | 40 minutes | 2 hours | | 4 or more | 1 hour 5 minutes | 2 hours | A pause that lasts the window ends the wait. So do these, which no pause can last through: * dry-run * protecting the workload * removing its `idlePolicy` * ignoring it * its target going A would-be pause in dry-run never waits longer. The count is kept in `status.idleWait`, so a restart keeps it. The wait only ever delays a pause. These don’t make a pause wrong, since Napwake didn’t misjudge the workload’s use: * a pause someone asked for * the forecast waking it ahead of demand * a Prometheus activity query * a wake for an awake dependent that uses it ### What Napwake tells you A `PausedWhileInUse` warning event says how soon the pause was undone. It also says how long the clock waits now, and what to change. The `PausedWhileInUse` condition says the same until a pause holds, or the wait ends otherwise. [`kubectl napwake status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) lists it under Needs attention. 
```text
a request came 40 seconds after it paused, so it was in use: it now waits 10 minutes without activity
before pausing, not 5 minutes, until a pause holds. It used up to 0.6% of its CPU requests while in use,
and up to 0.2% idle: try napwake.dev/cpu-threshold: "0.4"
```
 The suggested number comes from the CPU Napwake measures through each stretch the clock counts as idle: * A wrong pause shows what the workload uses in use. That’s the most in the stretch before it. * A pause that holds shows what it uses idle. That’s the most in the 15 minutes before it, or `idleAfter` if shorter. Napwake keeps the most over the last week. Napwake suggests a threshold when the CPU in use is at least twice the idle CPU, and 0.2 points more. It’s halfway between, to a tenth. A workload you opted in with the label is told the annotation, and one you wrote the field. Otherwise CPU can’t tell its use from its idle. Then Napwake suggests a [Prometheus query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#example-queries) that counts its requests. Until a pause has held, it hasn’t measured idle use, and says so rather than guess. 
```yaml
status:
  idleWait:
    wrongPauses: 1
    lastWrongPause:
      pausedAt: "2026-10-26T09:02:11Z"
      after: 40s
      by: request          # request, click, wakeRequest or annotation
      from: 10.4.1.7
      inUseCPU: "0.6"
    idleCPU: "0.2"
    idleCPUAt: "2026-10-25T17:06:00Z"
```
 `napwake_wrong_pauses_total` counts wrong pauses, by workload. Dry-run never pauses, so its would-be pauses aren’t judged. ## When a source can’t be read Napwake never pauses while a source can’t be read. Without that data, a busy workload looks idle. Nor does that time count as idle. * A check that can’t read a source isn’t watching. A source out for longer than the [allowed gap](#restarts-and-outages) restarts the clock (`lastActivitySource: unobserved`). * Once the source is back, an `Unobserved` event says so. A pause then needs a full `idleAfter` in which every source saw nothing. * A shorter blind spot leaves the last activity alone. One is the minute before a woken workload’s pods have metrics. * A Prometheus query may come back saying “no requests in the last 5 minutes”. That doesn’t make the hour before look idle. For CPU, the `MetricsAvailable` condition says why, and for queries, `PrometheusAvailable`. ## Restarts and outages The clock lives in `status.activity`, so it survives operator restarts. An outage can delay a pause, but never cause one. 
```yaml
status:
  activity:
    lastActivityTime: "2026-10-02T09:14:00Z"
    lastActivitySource: cpu    # created, woke, request, cpu, rollout, annotation, prometheus, unobserved, or scaled-up
    pauseAt: "2026-10-02T10:14:00Z"
    lastEvaluatedTime: "2026-10-02T09:41:00Z"
```
 If the operator stops watching for a while, it can’t know what happened. So an awake workload’s clock restarts when it watches again (`lastActivitySource: unobserved`). An `Unobserved` event says how long the gap was: | The gap | The `Unobserved` event says | | -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The operator didn’t check it | `Napwake wasn't watching for 9 minutes, so its idle clock starts again` | | A source was out | `Napwake couldn't read its activity for 9 minutes, so its idle clock starts again` | | A restart, or a change of leader | `the operator restarted, or another took over, so its idle clock starts again; it wasn't watching for up to 4 minutes` | * The gap allowed is half of `idleAfter`, at least 2 minutes (two missed checks), at most 7. * A restart or a change of leader always restarts an awake workload’s clock, however recently it was written. * The event comes with the first check that can read every source. * A gap or a restart isn’t activity, so a paused workload stays paused. In dry-run, a would-be pause goes on, as the real pause would ([Dry run](/next/guides/dry-run/#what-happens-in-dry-run)). * A restart can delay a pause by up to `idleAfter`, but never brings one forward. ## Waking up | While it’s paused | | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A request reaches one of its Services | Wakes. The request is held while it starts, then answered ([Wake on request](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/)) | | A `last-activity`, `last-request` or `active-until` annotation, on the ManagedWorkload or its target, changes to an RFC 3339 time | Wakes, whatever time it states, so a clock behind the operator’s can’t lose a wake | | `kubectl napwake wake`, or a tool setting [`napwake.dev/wake-requested`](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now) on the ManagedWorkload, such as a portal’s “start environment” button | Wakes, whatever the wake setting, with a `WakeRequested` event. The tool reads the answer in the `WakeRequest` condition | | An annotation set before the pause, even to a later time | Stays asleep. The pause records the annotations as they stood, and only a change counts | | A [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) shows activity. Queries run about once a minute, as while awake | Wakes. One active as a [requested pause](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now) began must go quiet first, so it doesn’t undo the request | | A query can’t be evaluated | Never wakes it | | `autoResume: true`, and a confident forecast (`dailyPhase: Active`) predicts above `cpuThreshold` this hour, or the next from 15 minutes before | Wakes, ready before people arrive. Not in the hour after one far quieter than forecast | | Something else scales it up, such as `kubectl scale` | Wakes | | Dry-run is turned on | Wakes. Dry-run never leaves a workload paused | Whatever would wake it, coming while it’s still pausing, [calls the pause off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off) instead. Each wake restarts the clock, so the next pause is a full `idleAfter` away. After [a pause made in use](#paused-while-in-use), it’s longer. With `pauseEarly` and a quiet forecast, the pause can come 20 minutes after the wake. `lastActivitySource` says what woke it: | `lastActivitySource` | Woken by | | -------------------- | ---------------------------- | | `request` | A held request | | `prometheus` | An activity query | | `scaled-up` | Something else scaling it up | | `woke` | Anything else | Next * [Wake on request →How a request wakes a paused workload.](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) * [Forecasting →How the forecast holds off a pause, or brings it forward.](/next/concepts/forecasting/)

# Lifecycle

> Every ManagedWorkload moves through a defined set of phases.

1. Awake Running Active. Its activity clock is checked on each reconcile. 2. No activity for `idleAfter`, or a pause asked for 3. Awake Idle Napwake pauses it, or in dry-run reports the pause it would make. 4. At once, unless held back 5. Asleep Pausing What the pause will change is recorded. Then it's scaled to zero and its pods stopped. 6. 7. Asleep Paused At zero replicas. 8. A request, activity, or `autoResume` 9. Waking up Resuming Its previous replica count is being restored. Running when its pods are Ready, or when Napwake stops waiting for those that aren't The operator moves a workload on from its activity, its forecast, and pauses asked for with `kubectl napwake pause`. * A new ManagedWorkload has no phase until its first reconcile sets `Running`. `kubectl napwake status` shows `creating`. The API accepts `Creating` but never sets it. * A workload a person, a pipeline or KEDA scaled to zero stays `Running`, with the `ScaledToZero` condition. Napwake only pauses what’s running, and only wakes what it paused. See [Workloads already at zero](/next/guides/pause/#workloads-already-at-zero). ## Transition triggers ### Automatic | Transition | When | | ---------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Running → Idle | No activity for `idleAfter`. Or, with [`pauseEarly`](/next/concepts/forecasting/#pausing-early), for 20 minutes before hours a confident forecast expects quiet. And nothing may [hold it up](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#the-decision) | | Idle → Pausing | At once, unless it’s in dry-run, or a GitOps tool undid its last pause within the hour | | Idle → Running | Activity resumes while dry-run or a GitOps tool’s undoing holds the pause off. Or it’s scaled to zero outside Napwake | | Paused → Resuming | A request to its Service, or with [`wake.mode: Ask`](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking) a person’s click on **Wake it**. A new `napwake.dev/wake-requested` value. A change to an activity annotation since the pause. A [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) showing activity. `autoResume` ahead of forecast demand. Dry-run turned on | | Pausing → Resuming → Running | Whatever would wake it once paused comes as it pauses, which [calls the pause off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off). A `PauseCancelled` event says what came. It’s then scaled back up if its pods had begun to stop | | Paused → Running | Something else scales it up ([GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/)), or Napwake [lets go of it](/next/guides/pause/#when-napwake-lets-go). Also from `Pausing`, once it’s at zero (`status.pause.pausedAt`) while its pods stop. It isn’t scaled back down | | Resuming → Running | Every replica it was restored to is Ready, or Napwake stops waiting (below). Scaled back to zero by something else after the resume scaled it up (`status.pause.resumedAt`), it’s left off with `ScaledToZero` | The resume’s wait starts once its `waitForReady` [dependencies](/next/concepts/dependencies/) serve. Napwake stops waiting 2 minutes after the first replica is Ready. With none Ready, it stops at the Deployment’s `progressDeadlineSeconds` after the scale-up. That’s 10 minutes by default, and for a StatefulSet. Stopping short sets `ResumeIncomplete`. [Resume](/next/guides/pause/#resume) has what follows. ### On request | Transition | When | | ---------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Running or Idle → Pausing → Paused | [`kubectl napwake pause`](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now), which sets a new `napwake.dev/pause-requested` value | | Running → Idle | The same in dry-run, as a would-be pause | | Paused → Resuming → Running | [`kubectl napwake wake`](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now), which sets a new `napwake.dev/wake-requested` value, whatever the wake setting. Or any automatic wake. A requested pause wakes like any other | | Pausing → Resuming → Running | `kubectl napwake wake` as it pauses, which calls the pause off, with a `PauseCancelled` event | | Idle → Running | The same in dry-run, ending a would-be pause. Awake, a wake request restarts the idle clock | A request runs the idle clock out at once, and the rules of an idle pause apply. The hour after a GitOps conflict doesn’t hold it back. A forecast expecting demand within the hour declines it, saying when, unless the request overrides the forecast. ## Idempotency Every lifecycle operation is idempotent. The operator can crash and restart at any point without leaving a workload inconsistent. Before anything is scaled, the status write that enters `Pausing` records what the pause changes in `status.pause`. That’s `previousReplicas`, the KEDA ScaledObject, and its own `paused-replicas` value. So: * Wherever a restart or a conflicting write interrupts a pause, it’s finished or undone from the record. * Resuming restores the recorded replica count, not a hardcoded value. * Pausing an already-paused workload does nothing. Before scaling, the record is also copied to the target’s `napwake.dev/paused-from` annotation, until it’s handed back. If the ManagedWorkload loses its status while paused, by a restore without status or being created again: | It finds | Then | | ------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------- | | The copy, and the workload still at zero | Back to `Paused` (`PauseRecovered`) | | The copy, and the workload scaled up since | Released (`PauseRecovered`) | | Neither, while `Pausing`, `Paused` or `Resuming` | Nothing was scaled. `Running` again (`PauseRecordLost`) | `spec.target` can’t change once set, so a paused target is never left at zero. To manage another workload, create another ManagedWorkload. ## Status conditions A ManagedWorkload’s conditions report what the operator found, such as what holds it awake or why it won’t pause. [`IdleVetoed`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#condition-idlevetoed) and [`HeldByDependents`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#condition-heldbydependents) hold a pause off. [`ScaledToZero`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#condition-scaledtozero) leaves a workload off. [`ResumeIncomplete`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#condition-resumeincomplete) says a resume stopped waiting. [Conditions and events](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#status-conditions) lists every condition, with each reason. ## Events Each user-visible change emits one event, as it happens, shown by `kubectl describe`. The phases above show as [`IdleDetected`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#event-idledetected), [`Paused`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#event-paused) and [`Resumed`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#event-resumed), and a wake by a request as [`WokenByRequest`](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#event-wokenbyrequest). [Conditions and events](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#events) lists every event, by what it’s about. Next * [Idle detection →When a running workload goes idle.](/next/concepts/idle-detection/) * [Pause and resume →Pausing and waking by hand.](/next/guides/pause/)

# Wake on request

> A paused workload has no pods, so nothing answers its Service.

A request reached staging while it was paused. The doorman holds it while staging starts, then passes it to the first Ready pod. A paused workload has no pods, so nothing answers its Service. The first request wakes it. The doorman holds the connection until a pod is Ready, then passes it through. The caller usually sees one slow response ([the exceptions](#what-the-caller-sees)). On a public URL, the doorman can [ask before waking](#asking-before-waking) instead. Then only a person’s click wakes it. * Default On for every workload Napwake pauses on its own, with nothing to configure * On a public URL `ask`: a browser gets a **Wake it** button, and robots wake nothing * Protocols Any TCP, such as HTTP, gRPC, Postgres and Redis * Holds a request Up to `wake.maxWait`, 2 minutes by default * Browsers A waking-up page that reloads every 3 seconds ## How it works The doorman is a small proxy, its own Deployment beside the operator, with two replicas and a PodDisruptionBudget. 1. Asleep · paused Routed to the doorman Its Services point at the doorman. 2. A caller connects and sends something 3. Waking up · resuming Held, and woken The doorman holds the connection and wakes the workload. 4. A pod is Ready 5. Awake · running Passed through A Ready pod gets the connection. | When | What happens | | ---------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The workload starts to pause | The operator adds an EndpointSlice, one per IP family, to each Service that selects its pods. It points at the doorman, on a port of its own (20000-29999). The Service, ClusterIP and DNS name don’t change, so callers and Ingresses need no changes. IPv4, IPv6 and dual-stack all work. The slice is in place before the pods stop, so no request is refused as they do ([What happens in a pause](/next/guides/pause/#what-happens-in-a-pause)) | | A connection arrives | If a pod of the Service is already Ready, it goes straight there. That happens while it’s still pausing or already waking, or when another workload’s pods serve the Service. While it’s pausing, a request passed on that would wake it [calls the pause off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off). Otherwise the doorman waits for the caller to send something | | The caller sends something | The doorman holds the connection and sets `napwake.dev/last-request` on the ManagedWorkload, which wakes it like an [activity annotation](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations). A `WokenByRequest` event names the Service. Once Running, the clock shows `lastActivitySource: request` | | A pod is Ready | The doorman, watching the Service’s own EndpointSlices, passes the held connection to a Ready pod, with the bytes already sent | | The workload is `Running` | The operator removes the doorman’s EndpointSlices. For 30 seconds the doorman still passes stray connections to Ready pods, waking nothing, while kube-proxy and load balancers catch up | The doorman works on TCP connections, not HTTP requests. Waking a workload wakes its [dependencies](/next/concepts/dependencies/) too. Ingress controllers, Gateway API implementations and cloud load balancers that read EndpointSlices reach the doorman the same way ([the ones checked](/next/reference/compatibility/)). A routed workload reports this: 
```plaintext
WakeOnRequest=True   DoormanRouted   requests are held and wake the workload
```
 ### Readiness probes The doorman lets a held request through once a pod is Ready. Ready comes from the readiness probe. Without one, a pod is Ready when its containers start, often before the app can serve. The request then gets whatever the app answers, such as a `503`. Give the app a probe that passes only once it can serve. If the app isn’t listening yet, the doorman retries every second until `maxWait`. deployment.yaml 
```yaml
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: api
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /ready   # answers 200 only once the app can serve requests
              port: 8080
            periodSeconds: 2
```
 | Probe | Effect | | --------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A short `periodSeconds` | Passes held requests on sooner | | A `startupProbe` | Gates readiness too | | A `tcpSocket` probe on the app’s port | Enough for an app that listens only once it’s ready | | None in any container, and no readiness gates | Napwake reads the pod template when it routes the workload. The `WakeOnRequest` condition says so, and a `NoReadinessProbe` warning event points here, once each pause | ### What wakes a workload, and what doesn’t | The caller | What happens | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Sends bytes, such as an HTTP request, a TLS handshake or a database protocol’s first message | Held. It wakes | | Connects, then sends nothing for 3 seconds | Held. It wakes, taken for a protocol where the server speaks first, such as MySQL | | Connects and closes without sending, like a TCP health check | Closed | | `GET` or `HEAD` from a known probe, scraper or uptime monitor, by `User-Agent` (below) | `503 Service Unavailable`, `Connection: close`. These would otherwise keep it awake forever | | `GET` or `HEAD` from Google Cloud’s load balancer health check, `GoogleHC/` | `200 OK`. Google’s load balancers send nothing to a backend that fails its check, so they’d drop the request that should wake it | | `GET` or `HEAD` from a prefix you added in the Helm value `doorman.healthCheckUserAgents` | `503` | | Any other method from those agents, such as a Prometheus remote write or an Alertmanager `POST` | Held. It wakes, since that’s data for it | | A hidden file, whose path has a part that starts with a dot, such as `/.env`, `/.git/config` or `/.aws/credentials`, except under `/.well-known/` | `404 Not Found`. No app serves these to its users, and scanners hunt them for leaked secrets | Checkers get these answers only while no pod is Ready. Then they pass through. Add your own checkers’ prefixes, cluster-wide: 
```yaml
doorman:
  healthCheckUserAgents: ["MyCorpMonitor/", "status-checker"]
```
 To keep the doorman off a port, list it on the Service by name or number. A metrics port something else scrapes is an example. Listed ports are never routed, so they fail while the workload is paused. 
```yaml
kind: Service
metadata:
  annotations:
    napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports: "metrics"   # or "9090", or both: "metrics,9090"
spec:
  ports:
    - name: http
      port: 80
    - name: metrics
      port: 9090
```
 ### Health checks the doorman can’t recognise The doorman knows a health check only by reading it. Two kinds can’t be read, and wake a paused workload on its traffic port every time they run. So it never stays paused longer than their interval. | Check | Why it can’t be read | | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | HTTPS and other TLS checks | The `User-Agent` is encrypted. AWS target groups that use HTTPS check over HTTPS by default, as do Google Cloud backend services on HTTPS or HTTP/2 | | HTTP checks and scrapers with a `User-Agent` the doorman doesn’t know | It looks like a caller, as from an uptime monitor you haven’t added to `doorman.healthCheckUserAgents` | `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports` can’t help on the traffic port, since ignoring it would stop requests waking the workload too. Do one of these instead: | Fix | What to know | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Check over plain HTTP | AWS’s and Google’s checkers send a `User-Agent` the doorman knows, and get `503` and `200`, waking nothing | | Check a port served only for health checks, listed in `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports` | Not behind a GKE Ingress or Gateway. Nothing answers that port while the workload is paused, so Google’s load balancer sees no healthy backend and sends nothing on | Most load balancers see a paused workload as unhealthy, as they would without Napwake. Then they do this: | Load balancer | With every target unhealthy | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | AWS Application and Network Load Balancers | Fail open. The first request reaches the doorman and wakes the workload | | Google Cloud Application Load Balancers, behind a GKE Ingress or Gateway | Answer `502` and send nothing on, until a backend passes its check. So the doorman answers `GoogleHC/` with `200 OK`, waking nothing, and holds the request that follows | | Any other | Check it before relying on wake on request behind it | ### Environments on the public internet On the public internet, have the doorman [ask before waking](#asking-before-waking), with `napwake.dev/wake: "ask"`. A browser then gets a **Wake it** button, and robots wake nothing. [Public preview environments](/next/guides/preview-environments/) walks through it. | The environment | `wake` | A request while it’s paused | | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- | | Behind a VPN or single sign-on at the ingress, or an allowlist of your offices’ and VPN’s addresses | `request`, the default | Held while the workload wakes | | Anyone can reach it | `ask` | A browser gets a **Wake it** button. Anything else gets a `503` | With `request`, the doorman wakes for any request it can’t tell is a health check. On the public internet most are robots. They’re search engine crawlers, and scanners that try every address for known weaknesses. Many send a browser’s `User-Agent` on purpose, so no list keeps up. Once an environment has an HTTPS certificate, its hostname is in public certificate logs within minutes. Scanners then reach it by name. A paused workload anyone can reach would wake every few minutes, and free little. `request` turns away only the hidden-file scanners. With `ask`, it still wakes in other ways. Tooling sets the [wake annotation](/next/guides/preview-environments/#waking-it-from-tooling), as [`kubectl napwake wake`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#wake-a-workload) does, whatever the wake setting. Behind the doorman, the [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/#what-an-hour-records) learns from people’s wakes, and with `autoResume` has it up before they come. It also wakes from CI through an [activity annotation](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations), when a [dependent](/next/concepts/dependencies/) wakes, or when something else scales it up. A [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/) that counts ingress requests counts crawlers’ requests too, so it would let them wake it. Don’t count requests there, or filter bots out. ### Services shared with other workloads A Service goes to the doorman only while none of its pods is Ready. | Behind one Service | What happens | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Another workload’s pods are Ready, as with a canary beside a stable Deployment | Traffic goes to them. A paused workload reports `WakeOnRequest=False`, reason `ServedByOtherPods` | | Every workload is paused | The Service is routed, and a request wakes them | | The other pods all go | The Service is routed within 30 seconds, and until then requests to it fail. The operator doesn’t cache other workloads’ endpoints. It reads the Service’s EndpointSlices from the API server, and decides again every 30 seconds while other pods serve it | ## Asking before waking 
```yaml
spec:
  wake:
    mode: Ask     # the default is Request, any request wakes it
```
 The doorman then wakes the workload only when a person asks. Crawlers, scanners and API clients still reach the doorman, and nothing they send wakes it. With the label, set `napwake.dev/wake: "ask"` on the workload or its namespace. For the cluster, set `defaults.wake: ask` ([The wake setting](#the-wake-setting)). [Public preview environments](/next/guides/preview-environments/) walks through it. | `wake.mode` | A browser opening a page, while it’s paused | Any other caller | | ---------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `Request`, the default | Wakes it, and gets the [waking-up page](#browsers) | Held while it wakes, unless it’s a [known probe](#what-wakes-a-workload-and-what-doesnt) | | `Ask` | Gets the asleep page. Only a click on **Wake it** wakes it | Gets a `503`, and wakes nothing | `WakeOnRequest` says the doorman asks: 
```plaintext
WakeOnRequest=True   DoormanRouted   the doorman asks a person's browser before waking the workload, and turns away other requests
```
 ### What browsers, robots and API clients see | The caller | What happens | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A person’s browser opening a page | The asleep page, with a **Wake it** button | | A click on **Wake it**, from a browser that says it came from the page | Wakes it, as a held request does. The waking-up page follows | | A click whose button has run out, as on a page left open while its reloads failed | The asleep page again, a `403`, saying nothing woke, as the page may have been open too long, with a fresh button | | A click the browser says came from a page at another address. That’s another site or subdomain. Or, over plain HTTP, an `Origin` that isn’t the page’s, as behind a proxy that rewrites `Host` | The asleep page again, a `403`, saying the click came from another address. It says someone with access to the cluster can wake it with `kubectl napwake wake` | | A click within a minute of the last wake request | The asleep page again, a `429` with `Retry-After: 60`, saying to try again in a minute | | A click from a browser that doesn’t say it came from the page, one from before about 2020 | The asleep page again, saying so. It says to use a current browser, or that someone with access to the cluster can wake it with `kubectl napwake wake` | | A browser posting a form that isn’t the button’s, such as the app’s login after the pause | The asleep page, a `200`, as for a page load. It isn’t counted as [turned away](#what-it-turns-away) | | Anything else | `503 Service Unavailable` with `Retry-After: 300`, and a short body saying it’s asleep and how to wake it | | A connection that isn’t HTTP, such as a database protocol, or TLS the doorman can’t read | Closed, with nothing sent | | A known probe, health check or hidden file | Answered as for any workload ([above](#what-wakes-a-workload-and-what-doesnt)) | | Anything, once the workload is waking | As with `Request`. A browser gets the waking-up page, and the rest is held until it’s Ready | A robot that passes for a browser gets the page, and wakes nothing either. Only a post of the page’s token wakes the workload. It must be fresh, and from a browser’s `User-Agent` that says the click came from the page: | Where | The browser says so with | | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | It sends fetch metadata, as current browsers do over HTTPS | `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin`, a page of the same scheme, host and port | | The same, from Chrome, Edge, Firefox and other Chromium browsers | `Sec-Fetch-User: ?1` too, which marks a navigation a person started. Safari, and every browser on iOS, never send it, so their clicks are judged by the rest | | It doesn’t, as no browser does over plain HTTP | An `Origin` that’s the address the page was served at. That’s the host its request named, shown at the top of the page, and its port | | Over plain HTTP, behind a proxy that rewrites the `Host` header | The two differ. Serve the workload over HTTPS there | The page’s `Referrer-Policy: same-origin` lets the browser send that `Origin`, and sends nothing to other sites. So a spider that fetches the page and posts its token wakes nothing, even with a browser’s `User-Agent`. Nor does a script that submits the form. A robot that forges those headers on purpose, or drives a real browser and clicks, still can. That’s rare, and costs at most a wake a minute. A browser from before about 2020 may send neither. Its click can’t be told from a spider’s, so it wakes nothing. The page says so. It says to use a current browser, or that someone with access to the cluster can wake it with `kubectl napwake wake`. The pages name no namespace, workload or Service, so they give nothing away to whoever reaches them. ### The Wake it button The button posts back to the address of the page the person opened. So it reaches the same workload however an ingress routes it, a path prefix included. | | The button’s token | | ----------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Signed for | That workload and that pause, named by when the pause began (`status.pause.startedAt`). It wakes no other, nor the same one in a later pause | | Good for | 10 minutes | | On a page shown as the workload pauses | It’s for that pause. Its click [calls the pause off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off), or wakes the workload once it’s paused | | Kept fresh | By the page reloading every minute. That also takes a tab left open on to the app once someone has woken it | | Expired anyway, as on a page whose reloads failed | Wakes nothing. The page says so, as a `403`, and offers the button again | | Posted from a page of another site, subdomain or port | Wakes nothing, as `Sec-Fetch-Site` or the `Origin` says. The page says how else to wake it | | Shown in a frame | No other site can show the page in a frame | The doorman’s replicas sign with one key, since a click can reach any of them. It’s in a Secret the chart creates empty, `napwake-doorman-wake-key` for a release named `napwake`. The first doorman pod to find it empty fills it with a random key. Helm and GitOps tools never set its data, so they leave the key alone. To change the key, empty the Secret. Each doorman pod uses the new one within a minute. It stops accepting the old one 11 minutes after it switches, once every token signed with it has expired. 
```bash
kubectl patch secret napwake-doorman-wake-key -n napwake-system --type=json -p '[{"op":"remove","path":"/data"}]'
```
 A click wakes a workload at most once a minute, whichever doorman pod it reaches. A click within a minute of the last wake request gets a `429`, with `Retry-After: 60`. It asks to try again in a minute. That can be one moments before the workload paused again. People’s tooling can wake it too, without a browser. A pull request bot, a developer portal or a chat command sets [`napwake.dev/wake-requested`](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now), as `kubectl napwake wake` does. Both work whatever `wake.mode` says. ### What it turns away The doorman counts the requests it turns away during each pause. That’s the `503`s and closed connections, not a person shown the page, nor probes and health checks. It’s a count only, with nothing of the requests themselves, no paths, addresses or headers. It’s in the ManagedWorkload’s status, brought up to date about once a minute. It covers the current pause, or the last one once the workload is awake. 
```yaml
status:
  turnedAway:
    pausedAt: "2026-10-23T18:04:12Z"
    requests: 312
```
 [`kubectl napwake status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) lists the workloads that turned requests away. `napwake_doorman_wakes_total{result="turned_away"}` counts them as they happen ([Metrics](/next/reference/metrics/#doorman)). Many mean something automated calls the workload, such as a monitor, a webhook or another service. A person can’t be turned away, so find out what it is. ### When to keep waking on request Asking suits what people open in a browser. Keep `Request`, the default, for a workload that other workloads, scripts or webhooks call. They’re turned away while it’s paused, and nothing they send wakes it. | Asking needs | What that means | | ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Plain HTTP/1.1 to the doorman | TLS ended at an ingress or load balancer in front of it. They speak HTTP/1.1 to their backends unless told otherwise | | Fine | A browser’s HTTP/2 or HTTP/3 to the ingress, and a click’s form sent on chunked | | Never woken by a click | A workload that ends TLS itself | | Turned away | A proxy told to speak HTTP/2 or gRPC to it, such as ingress-nginx with `backend-protocol: GRPC` | A workload that asks never [pauses early](/next/concepts/forecasting/#pausing-early), even with `pauseEarly` on. A request doesn’t wake it, so someone who came while the forecast expected quiet would find it asleep. It pauses when `idleAfter` runs out. ## Browsers A browser gets a page at once, saying the environment is starting. The page reloads every 3 seconds, and the first reload after a pod is Ready reaches the app. With `Ask`, a browser gets the [asleep page](#asking-before-waking) first, and this one after the click. ![The waking-up page for pr-412.preview.example.dev, 14 seconds into starting](/next/_astro/waking-page.CY2dEeRz_q6ibx.webp) The page a browser sees while the workload starts. | | The waking-up page | | ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Shows | The address the person opened, so it reads as part of their environment. Once the workload is `Resuming`, how long it has been starting. It names no namespace, workload or Service | | Goes to | A browser opening a page, by a `GET` or `HEAD` with `Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate`, or one that asks for HTML, from older browsers. Scripts’ requests, API clients, form posts, WebSockets, TLS and other protocols are held as usual | | Tells callers apart | By the first bytes they send, which the doorman passes on unchanged when it holds the connection. It reads up to 16 KiB of headers. Large single sign-on cookies can make them longer. Then the first 16 KiB decide, and browsers send those before their cookies | | Is sent as | `503 Service Unavailable` with `Cache-Control: no-store`, `Retry-After: 3` and `X-Robots-Tag: noindex`, so caches, crawlers and monitors don’t take it for the app. It answers at once, so proxy timeouts don’t matter for browsers | | Its tab icon | Drawn in the page, so browsers don’t ask the doorman for `/favicon.ico` | | Its `Content-Security-Policy` | Allows its own style and no script, and images only from `data:` | | Can be missed | A browser reaches the doorman only when it asks the server. A page served without `Cache-Control`, as static file servers often do, can reload from the browser’s cache. Apps that send `Cache-Control: no-cache` for their HTML, as most do so releases take effect, always reach the doorman | To hold browsers like any other caller, set this. With the label, set the annotation `napwake.dev/wake-page: "false"` instead. With `Ask`, a browser always gets a page. 
```yaml
spec:
  wake:
    page: false
```
 ## How long a connection is held The doorman holds a connection for up to `wake.maxWait`. That’s 2 minutes by default, and at most 1 hour, the most the ManagedWorkload accepts. With no Ready pod by then, it closes the connection. The wake carries on, so a retry a little later succeeds. With the label, set the annotation `napwake.dev/wake-max-wait: "5m"` instead. 
```yaml
spec:
  wake:
    maxWait: 5m     # a slow starter, such as a database replaying its log
```
 Callers time out too. Most HTTP clients wait at least 30 seconds, and browsers minutes. A proxy can give up sooner, and ingress-nginx closes an upstream request after 60 seconds by default. For a slower starter, raise it to match `maxWait`: 
```yaml
metadata:
  annotations:
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-read-timeout: "120"
```
 ### Limits The caps stop a flood of connections exhausting the doorman, or waking workloads faster than the cluster can start them. | What | Cap | Over the cap | | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Connections held, per doorman pod | 2048. Per workload 256, per source IP 1024, per source IP for one workload 128 | Closed at once, with nothing sent | | Passed through at once, per doorman pod | 1024. Per source IP 512 | Held as if the workload weren’t Ready, until a place frees or `maxWait` runs out | | Clicks on **Wake it**, with [`Ask`](#the-wake-it-button) | One wake a minute per workload, whichever doorman pod a click reaches | The asleep page, a `429`, asking to try again in a minute | | Wakes | Per source IP, a burst of 30, then one every 2 seconds. Overall, a burst of 200, then 10 a second. Only a request that would wake a paused workload counts, and one refused overall doesn’t use its source’s allowance | Browsers get the waking-up page, and the rest are held. The wake is retried every 2 seconds until allowed or `maxWait` runs out | | Bytes read from held callers | 1MiB per connection, 32MiB in all, each connection’s first 8KiB not counted | A larger body waits in the network until the workload is up, or other held connections free some of the 32MiB | | A passed-through connection that carries nothing either way | 10 minutes | Closed, so a vanished caller frees its place. Pings keep it open, as with WebSockets and database pools | | Connections still passing through the doorman once the workload is awake and its route drains | 5 minutes | Closed, so long-lived callers reconnect to the pods directly | The doorman reads what held callers send, so it can let go at once of one who gives up. The uncounted 8KiB keeps that working for ordinary requests while uploads fill the 32MiB. A caller who leaves partway through a larger body is let go once it’s read. A caller that closes its connection has left, whatever its protocol, and is never passed on. Over TCP, closing and shutting only the sending side look the same, and nearly every caller that does either has given up. So a client that sends, then shuts its side to wait, as `nc -N` does, is dropped while the workload is paused. Leave the connection open instead. The caps are per workload as well as per source, since the source IP is the address the doorman sees. Through an ingress controller, that’s the controller’s pod, shared by every user behind it. A burst to one environment then can’t crowd out the rest. The per-workload cap also stops one that never becomes Ready from taking every place. ## What the caller sees A browser gets the waking-up page, or with [`Ask`](#asking-before-waking) first the asleep page. Anything else, from a pod in the cluster or through an Ingress, usually gets one slow response. These are the exceptions: | When | The caller gets | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Behind ingress-nginx, a workload slower to start than the 60-second default `proxy-read-timeout` | A `504`. [Raise the timeout](#how-long-a-connection-is-held) | | Istio’s STRICT mTLS | A `503`. The woken pod’s sidecar refuses the doorman ([Compatibility](/next/reference/compatibility/#service-meshes)) | | Right after a wake, with no readiness probe, or one that passes too soon | An error from the app itself, such as a `503` ([Readiness probes](#readiness-probes)) | | As it pauses | An answer from a pod. A request that reaches the doorman is passed on to a Ready pod, or held, and [calls the pause off](/next/guides/pause/#a-request-calls-the-pause-off). Some go straight to the pods, unseen, and the pause goes on. That page lists which. The doorman is in front of the Service before the pods stop. So a new connection through it isn’t refused for want of a route | | As it pauses, a pod that exits the moment it gets `SIGTERM` | A refused connection, when kube-proxy sends it there in the second before it drops the pod, as in any rollout. Give the pod a `preStop` sleep of a few seconds, or have it go on serving briefly after `SIGTERM` | | Held past `maxWait`, or the woken pods can’t be reached | No response. Most clients report an empty reply or a connection reset. A `RequestNotServed` warning event names the Service, the wait and why. Each doorman pod emits it at most once a minute per workload | | Over a [limit](#limits) | Closed at once, or held while the wake is retried | | The workload [asks before waking](#asking-before-waking), for anything but a person’s browser | A `503` saying it’s asleep and how to wake it, until someone does | Held connections reach the woken pod from the doorman’s IP. Only connections that arrive while the workload is paused or waking, and some as it pauses, go through the doorman. It records the caller’s address on the ManagedWorkload as `napwake.dev/last-request-from`, so Napwake can [learn](/next/concepts/dependencies/#learned-dependencies) which workload sent it. Track wakes with `napwake_doorman_wakes_total` and `napwake_doorman_wait_seconds` ([Metrics](/next/reference/metrics/#doorman)). The `NapwakeDoormanWakesFailing` alert fires when a workload’s held connections keep timing out or failing. ## The wake setting It says what wakes a paused workload through the doorman. `request`, the default, is any request. `ask` is only a person’s click ([Asking before waking](#asking-before-waking)). | For | Set | Values | | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- | | A ManagedWorkload | `spec.wake.mode` | `Request`, the default, or `Ask` | | A workload opted in with the label | `napwake.dev/wake` on it, or on its namespace | Exactly `"request"` or `"ask"` | | Labelled workloads whose own and namespace’s annotations don’t set it | The Helm value `defaults.wake`, which sets the operator flag `--default-wake` | `request`, the default, or `ask` | The workload’s annotation wins, then its namespace’s, then `defaults.wake`. An annotation that can’t be read, such as `"Ask"` or `"true"`, asks. An `InvalidSetting` warning event on the workload says so. It asks whatever its namespace says, so a typo never lets robots wake it. ### Turning it off No setting turns waking off for one workload. A workload on a public URL can [ask](#asking-before-waking) instead. To keep the doorman away, use one of these: | Keep the doorman off | Set | Then | | --------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | One port of a Service, such as a metrics port something scrapes | Its name or number in `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports` on the Service ([example](#what-wakes-a-workload-and-what-doesnt)) | That port fails while the workload is paused | | The whole cluster | The Helm value `doorman.enabled: false`, or the operator flag `--doorman-service=""` | Requests to paused workloads fail. They report `WakeOnRequest=False`, reason `DoormanDisabled` | ## When a workload isn’t routed Requests to a Service that isn’t routed fail, as they would without Napwake. Routing problems never hold up a pause or a wake, and every pause works routing out afresh. Each reason is on the `WakeOnRequest=False` condition. | Situation | What happens | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | No Service selects the workload, or none has a TCP port outside `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports` | `NoServices`. A workload no request reaches, such as a queue worker, wakes on a [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) instead. That’s one that sees its demand, such as its queue’s length. Its forecast learns its paused hours from that query | | A headless Service (`clusterIP: None`) | Not routed, since callers resolve pod IPs from DNS. Wake the workload with an [activity annotation](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations) | | A Service without a selector, or an `ExternalName` Service | Not routed, since its endpoints aren’t the workload’s pods | | UDP or SCTP ports | Not routed. Only TCP ports are | | A Service another workload’s Ready pods serve | Not routed. `ServedByOtherPods` if no Service is ([shared Services](#services-shared-with-other-workloads)) | | `internalTrafficPolicy: Local` | Not routed. kube-proxy sends each caller only to endpoints on its own node, and the doorman’s pods are on one or two. `NodeLocalTraffic` if no Service is routed, otherwise the message names it. Use an activity annotation, or a Service with the default policy, `Cluster` | | An IP family no doorman pod has an address in | Not routed. `UnsupportedIPFamily` if no Service is | | Behind GKE container-native load balancing ([On GKE](#on-gke)) | Not routed, with a warning event, unless the [forwarder](#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs) is on. `UnsupportedLoadBalancer` if no Service is routed. Otherwise the condition stays `True`, and its message names the Services left out | | The same, with the forwarder on, before a forwarder pod is Ready | Routed once one is, usually a few seconds after the pause. `ForwarderNotReady` if no Service is routed, with why, such as an image that can’t be pulled or a quota refusing its pods. Otherwise the message names the Service | | The Deployment or StatefulSet is deleted | Routing is removed, since nothing would start the pods. `TargetAvailable=False`, reason `TargetNotFound` | | No doorman pod is Ready | `DoormanUnavailable`. Routing is removed until one is back | | All doorman ports are in use | `NoFreePort`. There are 10,000, one per paused Service port in the cluster | | The workload would hold over 32 doorman ports, or its namespace over 256 | `TooManyPorts`, so one tenant can’t use up the ports every namespace shares. The Helm values `doorman.portsPerWorkload` and `doorman.portsPerNamespace` set the caps | | An EndpointSlice write is refused. Or another EndpointSlice already has the doorman’s name for it (`<service>-o<hash>`), which the doorman never overwrites | `RoutingFailed`, with a warning event. Retried with backoff, from 5 seconds up to 5 minutes | | The workload is awake | `NotPaused`. A problem with its Services found at the last pause stays instead, its message starting `at its last pause:`. That’s `ServedByOtherPods`, `NodeLocalTraffic`, `UnsupportedIPFamily`, `UnsupportedLoadBalancer` or `TooManyPorts`. The next pause meets it again unless it’s fixed, so [`kubectl napwake status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) lists it under Needs attention | ### On GKE GKE adds `cloud.google.com/neg: '{"ingress": true}'` to every new Service of a VPC-native cluster, the default kind. It asks for container-native load balancing, with network endpoint groups (NEGs) of the Service’s pods. It does so only if a GKE Ingress uses the Service. So on its own it doesn’t count, and Napwake routes such a Service like any other. A Service that really is in NEGs isn’t routed without the [forwarder](#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs), reason `UnsupportedLoadBalancer`. GKE’s NEG controller refuses endpoints whose pods are in another namespace, as the doorman’s are. It can also stop syncing the load balancer altogether. Napwake looks again whenever an Ingress or a Service in the namespace changes. A Service comes off the doorman once a GKE Ingress uses it, and is routed again once none does. | A Service is in NEGs when | What GKE looks at | | ------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A GKE Ingress uses it, while it has `{"ingress": true}` | A GKE Ingress has `kubernetes.io/ingress.class` `gce`, `gce-internal`, `gce-regional-external` or `gce-multi-cluster`, or no class. GKE reads only the annotation, so an Ingress that sets `ingressClassName` isn’t GKE’s | | It has standalone NEGs | `exposed_ports` in its `cloud.google.com/neg`, as GKE Gateway sets on the Services its routes use | | GKE’s NEG controller lists NEGs of its pods | `cloud.google.com/neg-status` | To see what GKE has set on a Service: 
```bash
kubectl get service api -n preview-42 -o jsonpath='{.metadata.annotations}'
```
 With the forwarder off, requests reach a paused workload like this: | The request comes through | What happens | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A GKE Ingress on instance groups, its Service set to `cloud.google.com/neg: '{"ingress": false}'` | It reaches the doorman through the node port and wakes the workload, since the doorman tells Google’s health check it’s up. Setting that on the Ingress’s own Service gets it routed. But that moves a `gce` Ingress onto instance groups, which needs the Service to be `type: NodePort`. A `gce-internal` Ingress can’t do without NEGs. GKE warns against changing the annotation on a Service a load balancer uses, since that replaces its backends | | A GKE Ingress whose Service is in NEGs, or a GKE Gateway, which always uses NEGs | The Service isn’t routed, and the load balancer answers `502` | | An ingress controller that reads EndpointSlices, such as ingress-nginx, in place of the GKE Ingress or behind it | It wakes the workload. The GKE Ingress uses the controller’s Service, which never pauses. The controller sends requests on to the workload’s Service, which is routed | | A caller in the cluster, through a Service a GKE Ingress uses | Not routed. Give such callers a Service of their own (below) | With the forwarder on, the second and last rows wake the workload too. Without it, wake the workload another way, with `kubectl napwake wake`, an [activity annotation](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations) from your developer portal, or the forecast’s `autoResume`. No GKE Ingress uses a Service like this one, so it’s routed, and callers in the cluster can use it: 
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: api-internal
  namespace: preview-42
spec:
  selector:
    app: api
  ports:
    - name: http
      port: 80
      targetPort: 8080
```
 ## GKE container-native load balancing (NEGs) The forwarder is optional and off by default. Turn it on if people reach your paused environments through a GKE Ingress on NEGs, or a GKE Gateway: 
```yaml
doorman:
  negForwarder:
    enabled: true
```
 GKE’s NEG controller accepts only endpoints whose pods are in the Service’s own namespace. So a Service in NEGs can’t point at the doorman. GKE uses NEGs for an Ingress’s Services by default on VPC-native clusters, and always for a Gateway’s. Services neither uses wake workloads without the forwarder. ### What it runs While a workload with a Service in NEGs is paused, the operator runs a forwarder beside it. That’s a Deployment in its namespace, `napwake-forwarder-<workload>`. | | The forwarder | | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | What it does | It stands in for the workload’s pods in that Service’s endpoints, which GKE accepts, since it’s in the Service’s namespace. It passes every connection to the doorman, bytes untouched, and the doorman holds it and wakes the workload. The workload’s other Services, those no NEG uses, go to the doorman directly | | Lifetime | The operator deletes it a minute after the workload is `Running` and its route has drained. The ManagedWorkload owns it, so deleting the ManagedWorkload deletes it too | | Size | Two small pods by default, spread across nodes ([sizes](/next/reference/helm-values/#doorman)). A node drain then doesn’t cut the workload off while GKE registers a new pod. They run only while the workload is paused | | Access | The operator’s image, as the namespace’s `default` ServiceAccount without its token, with no access to the API server. Its pods meet the Pod Security Standards’ restricted level, and keep Istio’s and Linkerd’s sidecars out. It carries `napwake.dev/ignore`, so Napwake doesn’t manage it in a namespace opted in with the label | | Ports | The Services’ target ports, which GKE’s firewall rules let Google’s load balancers reach. Below 1024, it sets the `net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start` sysctl, which the restricted level allows, rather than run as root | | Readiness | Ready only while it reaches the doorman on every port it forwards. It finds the doorman through a headless Service the chart adds, `napwake-doorman-pods` for a release named `napwake` | To see it: 
```bash
kubectl get deployments -n preview-42 -l app.kubernetes.io/name=napwake-forwarder
kubectl get endpointslices -n preview-42 -l napwake.dev/forwarded=true
```
 A route through the forwarder shows `forwarded: true` in the ManagedWorkload’s `status.doorman`. ### Health checks behind NEGs Keep the backend service’s health check on the traffic port, over plain HTTP. A GKE Ingress sets that up for an HTTP backend. Google’s load balancer now checks the forwarder, which passes the check to the doorman. `GoogleHC/` gets `200 OK`, as on every route, and every other checker `503`. HTTPS and HTTP/2 checks can’t be read here either, so they wake the workload every run. A port of its own fails here, since the forwarder listens only on the ports it routes. The load balancer then sends nothing on. ### Timeouts behind NEGs For a slower starter, raise the backend service’s timeout to match `wake.maxWait`. Google’s load balancer waits 30 seconds for a backend by default, and only browsers get an answer at once. On a GKE Ingress, use a BackendConfig, which the Service names in its `cloud.google.com/backend-config` annotation as `'{"default": "slow-start"}'`. On a GKE Gateway, set `spec.default.timeoutSec` in a GCPBackendPolicy. 
```yaml
apiVersion: cloud.google.com/v1
kind: BackendConfig
metadata:
  name: slow-start
  namespace: preview-42
spec:
  timeoutSec: 120
```
 ### Network policies behind NEGs The chart’s doorman NetworkPolicy admits forwarders from any namespace, by their labels, `app.kubernetes.io/name: napwake-forwarder` and `napwake.dev/operator` naming Napwake’s namespace. Anyone who can create pods can give them those labels, and so reach the routing ports, as a caller in `ingressFrom` can. With a default-deny policy in the workload’s namespace, let whatever reaches the workload reach the forwarder. That includes Google’s load balancers and health checks, from `130.211.0.0/22` and `35.191.0.0/16`. Let the forwarder reach the doorman and DNS. If apps in the cluster call the Service too, add them to `ingress.from`. 
```yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-napwake-forwarder
  namespace: preview-42
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels: {app.kubernetes.io/name: napwake-forwarder}
  policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]
  ingress:
    - from:
        - ipBlock: {cidr: 130.211.0.0/22}
        - ipBlock: {cidr: 35.191.0.0/16}
  egress:
    - to:
        - namespaceSelector:
            matchLabels: {kubernetes.io/metadata.name: napwake-system}
          podSelector:
            matchLabels: {control-plane: doorman}
      ports:
        - {port: 20000, endPort: 29999, protocol: TCP}
    - ports:
        - {port: 53, protocol: UDP}
        - {port: 53, protocol: TCP}
```
 ### Limits behind NEGs | When | What happens | | ------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Right after a pause | GKE takes a while, typically under a minute, to register the forwarder’s pods and see them pass health checks. Until then, requests through the load balancer can get a `502`, as after any scale-down. The Service can also show GKE’s warning event `SyncNetworkEndpointGroupFailed`, `endpoint count from endpointPodMap cannot be zero`. That’s for the moment between the workload’s pods going and the forwarder’s arriving, and GKE retries within seconds | | After a wake | The Service goes back to the workload’s pods as soon as one is Ready. While the route drains, for 30 seconds, the forwarder passes what still reaches it to the doorman, and on to the workload | | The caller’s address | The doorman sees the forwarder’s. So dependencies aren’t [learned](/next/concepts/dependencies/#learned-dependencies) from requests through it, and the per-source [limits](#limits) count each forwarder pod as one source. Through a load balancer, the caller is one of Google’s proxies anyway | | Images from a private registry | The forwarder pulls the operator’s image in the workload’s namespace, as its `default` ServiceAccount. A mirror that needs a pull secret needs it there too. GKE nodes pull from Artifact Registry in their own project without one | | Turning it off | Running forwarders stay, since the operator may no longer delete them. Remove them with `kubectl delete deployments -A -l app.kubernetes.io/name=napwake-forwarder` | | The Kustomize manifests | Set it up by hand. Run the operator with the `--neg-forwarder` flags ([Configuration](/next/reference/configuration/#operator-flags)). Grant it `create` and `delete` on Deployments, and add a headless Service selecting the doorman’s pods | ## Network policies The doorman connects to woken pods directly, from Napwake’s namespace. If a workload’s namespace has a default-deny NetworkPolicy, allow ingress from the doorman. Callers reach the doorman on ports 20000-29999, so a default-deny policy in Napwake’s namespace must allow those too. 
```yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-napwake-doorman
  namespace: preview-42
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: [Ingress]
  ingress:
    - from:
        - namespaceSelector:
            matchLabels: {kubernetes.io/metadata.name: napwake-system}
          podSelector:
            matchLabels: {control-plane: doorman}
```
 ### Restricting who can reach the doorman The doorman is a way in to every paused workload Any client that reaches a doorman pod can reach every routed, paused workload, in any namespace, on its Service ports. A workload’s own NetworkPolicy sees the doorman as the source. So a policy that admits the doorman admits everyone who can reach it. The rate limits slow a port scan through the doorman, but don’t stop one. The chart’s NetworkPolicy (`networkPolicy.enabled`, off by default) leaves the routing ports open to all, until you say who may use them. List your paused workloads’ real callers in `doorman.networkPolicy.ingressFrom`, such as your ingress controller’s namespace and the namespaces whose apps call paused services: 
```yaml
networkPolicy:
  enabled: true
doorman:
  networkPolicy:
    ingressFrom:
      - namespaceSelector:
          matchLabels: {kubernetes.io/metadata.name: ingress-nginx}
      - namespaceSelector:
          matchLabels: {team: checkout}
```
 | | How the chart’s policy treats it | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Each `ingressFrom` entry | A NetworkPolicy peer, so `podSelector` and `ipBlock` work too. Everyone else is refused before reaching the doorman | | The operator’s own pods | Added by the chart. They connect to each doorman pod before a pausing workload’s Services are sent to it, to check it’s listening | | The doorman’s metrics port | Open to namespaces labelled `metrics: enabled` | | `ingressFrom` without `networkPolicy.enabled` | Refused at install, since it would create no policy and leave the doorman open | | A policy of your own beside the chart’s | Don’t add one. NetworkPolicies add up, so the chart’s open routing ports would still admit everyone | | Traffic from outside the cluster, through a NodePort or LoadBalancer Service | It usually arrives from a node’s address, since kube-proxy rewrites the source when it forwards to another node. Selectors don’t match nodes, so `ingressFrom` refuses it. Add your nodes’ CIDRs as an `ipBlock` entry, or the load balancer’s addresses if it keeps the client’s | | A routed Service with `externalTrafficPolicy: Local` | It keeps the client’s address. External callers then reach the doorman only through nodes that run a doorman pod ([Compatibility](/next/reference/compatibility/#service-proxies)) | Limit the doorman’s egress too. It dials only Pod-backed endpoints of EndpointSlices that the EndpointSlice controller manages. It never dials loopback, link-local or cloud metadata addresses ([Security](/next/reference/security/#the-doorman-is-a-way-in-to-paused-workloads) has the rules). An egress policy enforces the same bounds in the network. The chart can create one that allows only your pod CIDRs and the API server: 
```yaml
doorman:
  networkPolicy:
    egress:
      enabled: true
      podCIDRs: [10.244.0.0/16]
      apiServer: [172.18.0.2/32]   # kubectl get endpointslices -n default -l kubernetes.io/service-name=kubernetes
      apiServerPorts: [443, 6443]
```
 List every IP family your cluster uses, and the node CIDRs too if a routed workload uses `hostNetwork`. `apiServer` takes the API server’s endpoint addresses, not the `kubernetes` Service’s ClusterIP, since policies apply after the Service is resolved. Two admission policies are on by default (`doorman.admissionPolicy.enabled`), in the Kustomize manifests too. One lets the doorman’s ServiceAccount change only `napwake.dev/last-request` and `napwake.dev/last-request-from` on a ManagedWorkload. It may also set `napwake.dev/turned-away`, where it counts what it [turned away](#what-it-turns-away). The other lets no one else set the first two. Besides those, the doorman reads one Secret, its own [wake key](#the-wake-it-button), by name, and writes it once to fill it. So only the doorman can say a request woke a workload, or where it came from ([Security](/next/reference/security/#the-doorman-is-a-way-in-to-paused-workloads)). Next * [Idle detection →Activity annotations wake a workload the same way.](/next/concepts/idle-detection/) * [Dependencies →Waking a workload also wakes its dependencies.](/next/concepts/dependencies/) * [Compatibility →The ingress controllers and load balancers checked.](/next/reference/compatibility/)

# HPA and KEDA

> Napwake works alongside a HorizontalPodAutoscaler or a KEDA ScaledObject, with nothing to set up.

The ManagedWorkload’s `Autoscaled` condition says what scales it: 
```plaintext
Autoscaled=True   HPA web scales it between 2 and 10 replicas; Napwake pauses it at zero, where the HPA stops scaling, and resumes it within that range
```
