 | | HPA | KEDA ScaledObject | | -------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | paused | Napwake scales it to zero, where an HPA stops scaling | Napwake sets `autoscaling.keda.sh/paused-replicas: "0"` on the ScaledObject. KEDA then holds it at zero, instead of scaling it back to `minReplicaCount` or on a trigger | | resuming | Napwake restores its replicas, within `minReplicas` and `maxReplicas` if they changed. Then the HPA takes over | KEDA holds it at the restored replicas until all are Ready, or Napwake [stops waiting](/next/guides/pause/#resume). Then Napwake puts back your own `paused-replicas`, or removes the annotation | CPU from the workload’s own pods counts as activity whatever the HPA does. So a workload busy enough for its HPA to scale it up isn’t idle. ## KEDA KEDA is released only once the wake is done. Released sooner, a ScaledObject with `minReplicaCount: 0` and no active trigger could take a starting workload straight back to zero. A request the doorman held would then wait for nothing. [Letting go](/next/guides/pause/#when-napwake-lets-go) of a paused workload releases it the same way. If the ScaledObject or KEDA goes while the workload is paused, it wakes as usual. The annotation isn’t in your manifests, so Argo CD and Flux leave it alone. Napwake leaves a workload KEDA took to zero, such as on an empty queue, to KEDA, as [already at zero](/next/guides/pause/#workloads-already-at-zero). Pausing it would stop KEDA’s triggers from starting it. Such a workload gains little. Napwake adds waking on the first HTTP request, holding dependencies awake, and the forecast. It also pauses by activity it sees without a trigger. Pausing frees capacity on a KEDA workload with a `minReplicaCount` above zero, such as one kept warm in working hours. Without KEDA, Napwake looks for ScaledObjects every 10 minutes, in case it’s installed later. `Autoscaled` shows a ScaledObject change within 15 seconds, and a pause or wake reads them afresh. Next * [Pause and resume →What a pause changes, and what wakes it.](/next/guides/pause/) * [GitOps tools →Leave replicas to Napwake.](/next/guides/gitops/)

# Cluster Autoscaler configuration

> Set your node autoscaler to remove the nodes pausing empties, so what Napwake frees comes off the bill.

Napwake scales idle workloads to zero, at the pod layer. You pay for nodes, so what a pause frees comes off the bill once an autoscaler removes them. 1. `Napwake pauses pods` 2. `Capacity freed on their nodes``freedThisMonth`, the CPU and memory its pods requested 3. `Autoscaler removes nodes`What this page sets up 4. `Saved``savedThisMonth` With no node autoscaler, or scale-down blocked, pausing saves nothing. Saved is measured every 5 minutes from each node pool’s spare capacity ([Freed and saved](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#freed-and-saved)). It grows fastest on [nodes of their own](/next/guides/node-pools/), which pausing empties. ## What Napwake detects Every 10 minutes, the operator looks for what scales the nodes. It logs what it finds when that changes, and exports it as [`napwake_node_autoscaler`](/next/reference/metrics/#savings). | Found | From | | ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Karpenter | `nodepools.karpenter.sh` NodePools (`karpenter.sh/v1`), or nodes labelled `karpenter.sh/nodepool`. Covers AKS node auto-provisioning | | EKS Auto Mode | Nodes labelled `eks.amazonaws.com/compute-type: auto` | | Cluster Autoscaler | The `cluster-autoscaler-status` ConfigMap it writes in `kube-system`. Self-managed, or run for you by GKE Standard and AKS | | GKE Autopilot | That ConfigMap, and nodes named `gk3-` | | `none` | Nothing, anywhere. The operator logs that pausing won’t save until something scales nodes down | | `unknown` | Nothing, but it couldn’t look everywhere. A `watchNamespaces` install can’t read that ConfigMap in `kube-system` | `kubectl napwake status` and `scan` name it at the top, and [`kubectl napwake nodes`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-why-nodes-dont-scale-down) adds what keeps each node. ## Settings * Cluster Autoscaler [Cluster Autoscaler](https://github.com/kubernetes/autoscaler/tree/master/cluster-autoscaler) manages node groups on EKS, GKE, AKS and self-managed clusters. cluster-autoscaler-values.yaml 
  ```yaml
  autoDiscovery:
    clusterName: my-cluster


  extraArgs:
    # How long a node must be unneeded before it's removed. The default is 10m.
    # A paused workload's node keeps costing money for this long, so keep it
    # short next to idleAfter.
    scale-down-unneeded-time: 5m


    # A node is unneeded when the CPU or memory its pods request, whichever is
    # higher, is below this fraction of what it can allocate. 0.5 is the default.
    # It counts requests, not usage: a node frees up once paused pods are gone,
    # not when they go quiet.
    scale-down-utilization-threshold: "0.5"


    # How long after a scale-up before scale-down is considered again. The
    # default is 10m. A wake can scale nodes up, so a shorter delay lets the
    # next pause free them sooner; much shorter, and nodes churn.
    scale-down-delay-after-add: 5m


    # How long after removing a node before scale-down is considered again.
    # The default, 0s, lets the nodes of several workloads paused together go
    # one after another, so leave it.
    scale-down-delay-after-delete: 0s


    # How often the autoscaler evaluates the cluster. 10s is the default and
    # needs no change for Napwake.
    scan-interval: 10s
  ```
 | Provider | Setting these flags | | -------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | GKE | You can’t. Its `optimize-utilization` profile scales down sooner than the default `balanced` | | AKS | Cluster-wide, in its cluster autoscaler profile (`az aks update --cluster-autoscaler-profile`) | See [Node pools](/next/guides/node-pools/#cluster-autoscaler-gke-aks-and-eks-managed-node-groups) for both. | When Napwake | Cluster Autoscaler | | --------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Pauses (scale to 0) | The pods go. A node whose remaining pods request under the threshold goes after `scale-down-unneeded-time`, unless something on it blocks that | | Wakes (scale back up) | Adds nodes if there’s no room | ### What keeps a node | However empty, a node stays with | When | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A pod annotated `cluster-autoscaler.kubernetes.io/safe-to-evict: "false"` | Always | | A pod whose PodDisruptionBudget allows no disruptions | Always | | A pod with a `hostPath` volume, or an `emptyDir` not in memory | While `--skip-nodes-with-local-storage` is on, as it is upstream by default. AKS turns it off, and GKE 1.22 and later ignores local storage | | A pod no controller would recreate elsewhere | Always | | A `kube-system` pod without a PodDisruptionBudget | While `--skip-nodes-with-system-pods` is on, its default | | `cluster-autoscaler.kubernetes.io/scale-down-disabled: "true"` on the node | Always | | DaemonSet and static pods | Never | Napwake’s pauses set replica counts and never evict, so PodDisruptionBudgets don’t stop them. What’s left on the node holds it, and `kubectl napwake nodes` lists that per node, with the fix. * Karpenter [Karpenter](https://karpenter.sh) is an open-source node autoscaler from Kubernetes SIG Autoscaling, for AWS, Azure (as AKS node auto-provisioning) and other clouds. Napwake reads its `karpenter.sh/v1` NodePools. nodepool.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: karpenter.sh/v1
  kind: NodePool
  metadata:
    name: default
  spec:
    template:
      spec:
        nodeClassRef:
          group: karpenter.k8s.aws
          kind: EC2NodeClass
          name: default
        requirements:
          - key: karpenter.sh/capacity-type
            operator: In
            values: ["on-demand", "spot"]
          - key: kubernetes.io/arch
            operator: In
            values: ["amd64"]
        # Maximum time a node can live, regardless of utilization, so nodes
        # pick up new images. In v1 it's here, not under disruption.
        expireAfter: 720h
    disruption:
      # Removes empty nodes, and moves pods off underutilized ones to remove
      # those too. WhenEmpty never touches a node that runs anything but
      # DaemonSets, so pausing one workload on a shared node saves nothing.
      consolidationPolicy: WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized


      # How long after a pod was last added to or removed from a node before
      # it's considered. A pause is deliberate, not transient, so there's no
      # need to wait long. Never turns consolidation off.
      consolidateAfter: 1m
  ```
 | With Karpenter | | | ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `nodeClassRef` | Your provider’s node class. `EC2NodeClass` in `karpenter.k8s.aws` on AWS, `NodeClass` in `eks.amazonaws.com` on EKS Auto Mode | | After a pause | The node is empty or underused, and is consolidated after `consolidateAfter`. Karpenter repacks underused nodes too, so more of what pausing frees on shared nodes is saved, sooner | | On a wake | Instead of growing a node group, Karpenter provisions a node sized for the pending pods, so it doesn’t over-provision | | What keeps a node | `karpenter.sh/do-not-disrupt: "true"` on it or on one of its pods, or a pod whose PodDisruptionBudget allows no disruptions | | Without `budgets` | It disrupts at most 10% of a NodePool’s nodes at once | | Spot | Suits non-prod. Napwake prices spot nodes at their spot list price ([Spot nodes](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#spot-nodes)) | `kubectl napwake nodes` shows each NodePool’s consolidation settings and what holds each node. * Autopilot and serverless | Service | How it works | With Napwake | | -------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | | GKE Autopilot | Google manages the nodes, and bills per pod request | Freed stops being billed with the pods. No autoscaler settings | | EKS with Fargate | Each pod gets its own microVM. No shared nodes | Pausing a pod stops its billing. No autoscaler settings | | AKS node auto-provisioning | Karpenter, run by Azure | Use the Karpenter settings | | EKS Auto Mode | Karpenter, run by AWS. You pay for its nodes | Use the Karpenter settings in your own NodePools | On Autopilot and Fargate, what pausing frees is what you stop paying. Saved still measures spare node capacity, so it can read lower than your bill’s drop, never higher. Set `costTracking.rates` to the provider’s per-pod prices to price freed at what you pay. ## Verifying it works 1. Pause a workload now, without waiting for its idle clock: 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake pause my-api -n staging
   ```
 It returns once it’s `Paused`, or says why not, such as workloads that depend on it being awake ([Pause now](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now)). 2. Watch the nodes: 
   ```bash
   kubectl get nodes -w
   ```
 One goes after the autoscaler’s delay. If none does, `kubectl napwake nodes` says what keeps each. 3. Watch SAVED grow with FREED: 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake status -n staging
   ```
 The node’s going shows within 5 minutes, and in the workload’s status within another 5. 4. Wake it, with a request to its Service or: 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake wake my-api -n staging
   ```
 It returns once it’s `Running`, all replicas Ready. Its idle clock restarts, so it pauses again once idle. 5. Check a node is provisioned and the pods get Ready. A wake that waits for a node may need a longer `wake.maxWait` ([What waking costs](/next/guides/node-pools/#what-waking-costs)). CheckpointAwake A node went with the pause and came back with the wake. SAVED grew with FREED. If nodes stay after a pause: | Check | For | | ---------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `kubectl napwake nodes` | What keeps each node | | The autoscaler’s logs | Why it skipped scale-down | | The nodes’ other pods | Pods Napwake doesn’t manage. A [pool of their own](/next/guides/node-pools/) for non-prod avoids them | | PodDisruptionBudgets | Any that allow no disruptions | | `scale-down-utilization-threshold` | A threshold set too low | See [Freed but not saved](/next/operations/troubleshooting/#freed-but-not-saved). Next * [Node pools for real savings →Nodes that pausing empties.](/next/guides/node-pools/) * [Cost tracking →How freed and saved are measured.](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/)

# Dry run guide

> Dry run mode lets you observe what Napwake would do without it ever reducing a workload's availability.

1. Awake Running 2. The clock runs out, or a pause is requested 3. Awake Idle A would-be pause, counted. Never scaled down. The next activity ends the would-be pause Use it when you first opt a workload or namespace in, after changing its settings, or wherever you check first. It can’t show what happens once a workload is paused. That’s how long a wake on request takes, and whether a GitOps tool scales it straight back up. 1. Turn it on. * Workload deployment.yaml 
     ```yaml
     apiVersion: apps/v1
     kind: Deployment
     metadata:
       name: my-api
       namespace: staging
       labels:
         napwake.dev/managed: "true"
       annotations:
         napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"
     ```
 * Namespace Every opted-in workload in it is measured, unless it sets its own `napwake.dev/dry-run: "false"`. 
     ```yaml
     apiVersion: v1
     kind: Namespace
     metadata:
       name: staging
       labels:
         napwake.dev/managed: "true"
       annotations:
         napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"
     ```
 * Cluster Set `defaults.dryRun: true` in the Helm values, for every opted-in workload whose own annotations and namespace’s don’t say otherwise. * ManagedWorkload managedworkload.yaml 
     ```yaml
     apiVersion: napwake.dev/v1alpha1
     kind: ManagedWorkload
     metadata:
       name: my-api
       namespace: staging
     spec:
       target:
         kind: Deployment
         name: my-api
       idlePolicy:
         idleAfter: 1h
       prediction:
         mode: balanced
       dryRun: true
     ```
 On one created from the label, `spec.dryRun` follows the annotations, and an edit by hand is overwritten. A `napwake.dev/dry-run` value that can’t be read, such as `"True"` or `"yes"`, turns dry-run on, with an `InvalidSetting` warning event. 2. Let it measure for a few days, across a weekend if it’s used on weekdays. Then see what it would have freed. 
   ```
   kubectl get managedworkloads -n staging
   NAME     PHASE   FREED   SAVED   WOULD FREE   AGE
   my-api   Idle    $0.00   $0.00   $7.50        4d
   ```
 [`kubectl napwake status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) leads with it too, in its summary and `WOULD FREE` column. `kubectl napwake scan` adds how often it would have paused, and for how long. The [events](#events) say when. 3. End dry run. 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake enable my-api -n staging
   kubectl napwake enable --all -n staging   # every workload in the namespace
   ```
 `enable` sets the workload’s own `napwake.dev/dry-run` to `"false"`, or `spec.dryRun: false` on a ManagedWorkload you wrote. For `--all` and GitOps, see [`kubectl napwake enable`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#enable-a-workload). CheckpointAwake · pausing when idle `status.dryRun` is gone, and `status.cost` records what pausing actually frees. ## What happens in dry run | | In dry-run | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Idle detection | The clock runs, and the phase becomes `Idle` when it runs out. No pause | | Forecast veto and dependency holds | Applied and reported, as `IdleVetoed`, `HeldByDependents` and `DependencyCycle`. A dry-run dependent that would have paused stops holding its dry-run dependencies. So a whole app counts every pause it would make. When its would-be pause ends, theirs end too and their clocks restart, as its wake would have woken them. See [Dependencies](/next/concepts/dependencies/#edge-cases) | | A [pause request](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now) | A would-be pause. The phase becomes `Idle`, with a `[dry-run]` `PauseRequested` event and `PauseRequest=False`, reason `DryRun`. Later activity ends it. A Prometheus query already active when it was asked ends it only once it has been quiet | | A gap in watching, such as an operator restart, or metrics-server or Prometheus out | Doesn’t end a would-be pause. Activity seen after the gap does. For use during it, see [What dry run measured](#what-dry-run-measured) | | A workload not in a would-be pause, after a gap | Its clock restarts as usual, with an `Unobserved` event saying how long Napwake wasn’t watching. See [Restarts and outages](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#restarts-and-outages) | | A workload Napwake had paused | Woken at once, with a `DryRunWake` event. Dry-run never leaves a workload paused | | Wakes | Never held back. `kubectl napwake wake` and activity work as usual | | Cost tracking | All awake time. See [Cost tracking](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#in-dry-run) | | The forecast | Learns, and builds confidence, as usual | | Status and events | Say what would happen. `status.dryRun` sums it up, and events for what dry-run holds back carry a `[dry-run]` prefix | ## What dry run measured Real pauses can be shorter than would-be ones, since some wakes don’t show in dry-run. A would-be pause starts when the activity clock runs out. It ends at the next activity, when a paused workload would have woken. It also ends, counted up to then, when Napwake stops measuring the workload. That’s when it’s scaled to zero outside Napwake, labelled `napwake.dev/ignore`, or its namespace becomes protected (see [When Napwake lets go](/next/guides/pause/#when-napwake-lets-go)). | A wake that doesn’t show | In dry-run | | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A request to a paused workload | Counts only through the activity it shows, its CPU use or a [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/) | | `autoResume`, ahead of hours the forecast expects to be busy | Not counted | | Use during a gap in watching, such as an operator restart | Counts only through a trace that outlasts the gap | An activity annotation outlasts a gap, and so does a Prometheus query whose range covers it. CPU use, or a query over a shorter range, is gone when the gap ends. A request in the gap would have woken a workload paused for real. So a long gap can keep a would-be pause going, and overstate what pausing would have freed. For a workload that serves the odd light request, add a query on its request rate at its ingress. It needs the operator’s `prometheus.url` set. Dry-run then sees requests as the doorman would. Use the ingress’s metrics, since a paused workload has no pods to export the app’s own. On a labelled workload, the ManagedWorkload Napwake created keeps it: 
```bash
kubectl patch managedworkload my-api -n staging --type=json -p '[{"op":"add","path":"/spec/idlePolicy/activity/prometheus","value":[{"promQL":"sum(rate(nginx_ingress_controller_requests{exported_namespace=\"staging\",exported_service=\"my-api\"}[5m]))"}]}]'
```
 The ingress keeps counting once the workload pauses for real, so the query [wakes it then too](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused). The scan shows each dry-run workload’s COULD SLEEP, WAKES and COULD FREE/MO. Its headline has a total and the command to start pausing. It uses its own prices, so `--cpu-price` and `--memory-price` apply: 
```
kubectl napwake scan
  2 workloads are in dry-run: measured by Napwake since starting, they would have slept 140 hours, freeing $18.20,
  about $134 a month.

  NAMESPACE   WORKLOAD         STATE            BECAUSE                                     COST/MO   COULD SLEEP   WAKES   COULD FREE/MO
  preview-7   deployment/api   idle (dry-run)   no activity for 5h; measuring since Oct 1   $96       96h           4       $91
```
 The whole summary is in `status.dryRun`, from when dry-run starts until it ends: 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging -o jsonpath='{.status.dryRun}'
```
 status.dryRun 
```yaml
since: "2026-10-01T09:00:00Z"
pauses: 4
slept: 96h12m0s
freedCPUHours: "192400m"
freedMemoryHours: "384800m"
estimatedFreed: $7.50
```
 The hours are the record, to a billionth of an hour. A would-be pause under way, since `status.lastTransitionTime`, is added when it ends. `estimatedFreed` prices them at the workload’s [cost rates](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#prices) for display. It counts up to the last status write, at most 5 minutes ago. Freed becomes money only once [nodes go](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#how-saved-is-measured). The [API reference](/next/reference/api/) has every field. ## Events 
```bash
kubectl describe managedworkload my-api -n staging
```
 When the clock runs out, and when activity ends the would-be pause: 
```plaintext
[dry-run] my-api: idle for 1 hour, since its CPU use was last above the activity threshold; would have paused
[dry-run] my-api: activity resumed (cpu): would have slept 3 hours 12 minutes, freeing $0.25; since Oct 1: would have paused 4 times, slept 4 days, freeing $7.50
```
 The prefix marks what dry-run holds back. That’s `IdleDetected`, `PausedEarly`, `ActivityResumed`, `IdleVetoed`, `HeldByDependents`, `DependencyCycle`, and `PauseRequested` for a pause request. What really happens, such as a wake, a forecast update or a metrics problem, has no prefix. Next * [Opting in →Every setting.](/next/guides/opt-in/) * [Prometheus activity →Count requests at the ingress.](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/)

# GitOps tools

> Tell Argo CD, Flux, Fleet, kapp or Kluctl once to leave a workload's replicas to Napwake, so its syncs don't undo pauses.

Napwake pauses by setting replicas to zero. A GitOps tool that applies `replicas` from Git sees the drift and sets them back. With an HPA or KEDA under Argo CD or Flux, you usually have the fix, since autoscalers change replicas too. Before you start 
```bash
kubectl napwake scan
```
 [`scan`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#scan-a-cluster) lists the workloads whose replicas Git sets, by tool. ## Leave the replicas to Napwake * Argo CD 1. Ignore Deployment and StatefulSet replicas for every app, in `argocd-cm`: argocd-cm 
     ```yaml
     data:
       resource.customizations.ignoreDifferences.apps_Deployment: |
         jsonPointers:
           - /spec/replicas
       resource.customizations.ignoreDifferences.apps_StatefulSet: |
         jsonPointers:
           - /spec/replicas
     ```
 With Argo CD’s Helm chart, which writes `argocd-cm` from its values, use `configs.cm`: argo-cd-values.yaml 
     ```yaml
     configs:
       cm:
         resource.customizations.ignoreDifferences.apps_Deployment: |
           jsonPointers:
             - /spec/replicas
         resource.customizations.ignoreDifferences.apps_StatefulSet: |
           jsonPointers:
             - /spec/replicas
     ```
 2. Add `RespectIgnoreDifferences=true` to each app’s sync options, so a sync leaves them alone too, as the diff already does: application.yaml 
     ```yaml
     spec:
       syncPolicy:
         syncOptions:
           - RespectIgnoreDifferences=true
     ```
 It’s Argo CD’s HPA setting, so apps with it need nothing more. For some apps only, put the `jsonPointers` in their `spec.ignoreDifferences`. * Flux Leave `replicas` out of the manifest in Git, since Flux sets only the fields a manifest has. A new Deployment starts at one replica. For a Helm chart that always renders them, use a post-renderer: helmrelease.yaml 
  ```yaml
  spec:
    postRenderers:
      - kustomize:
          patches:
            - target: {kind: Deployment, name: my-api}
              patch: |
                - op: remove
                  path: /spec/replicas
  ```
 * Fleet Leave `replicas` out of the manifest in Git, since Fleet applies what its bundle renders. For a chart that always renders them, use a kustomize patch in the bundle’s `fleet.yaml`. * kapp Keep the replicas kapp finds in the cluster with a rebase rule, as its docs give for an HPA: kapp-config.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: kapp.k14s.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Config
  rebaseRules:
    - path: [spec, replicas]
      type: copy
      sources: [existing, new]
      resourceMatchers:
        - apiVersionKindMatcher: {apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment}
        - apiVersionKindMatcher: {apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: StatefulSet}
  ```
 * Kluctl Leave `replicas` out of the manifest in Git. For a chart that always sets them, patch them out in the deployment item’s `kustomization.yaml`. Conflict settings such as `kluctl.io/ignore-conflicts-manager` can’t help. Scaled through the scale subresource, the replicas have no field manager. So Kluctl’s next deploy sets them back without a conflict. ## Checking the fix is in Checkpoint [`kubectl napwake enable`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#enable-a-workload) and [`pause`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#pause-a-workload) warn while a GitOps tool will undo pauses. Once it’s told, they print nothing about it. | Tool | How the warning tells | | ------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Argo CD | Reads the Application, since Argo CD keeps owning the replicas it ignores. Names only the missing step | | Flux, Fleet, Kluctl | Checks whether the API server records the tool owning `spec.replicas` | | kapp | Says it can’t tell. kapp keeps as its own the replicas a rebase rule names, and the rule isn’t on the workload | Without access to Argo CD’s namespace, or with Argo CD in another cluster, they can’t read the Application or `argocd-cm`. They say why, and to make the fix if you haven’t. ## What Napwake does Napwake never fights Git. 1. Asleep · paused Napwake pauses it 2. Counted as activity 3. Awake · running A sync sets the replicas from Git A `GitOpsConflict` condition and warning event say what to change. 4. An hour later, so they don't loop 5. Asleep · paused Napwake pauses it again If it holds for an hour, the condition clears. `status.lastScaledUp` records when, how many replicas, and the field manager, such as `argocd-controller`, `kustomize-controller` or `kubectl-client-side-apply`. A scale through the scale subresource, as by `kubectl scale`, an HPA or KEDA, shows as `unknown`. For Argo CD, Flux, Rancher Fleet, kapp and Kluctl, the condition links to the tool’s tab: 
```plaintext
GitOpsConflict=True   Argo CD set the replicas from Git, undoing the pause; Napwake pauses it again from 14:20 UTC. To stop it, have Argo CD ignore replicas, as for an HPA: https://docs.napwake.dev/guides/gitops/#argo-cd
```
 [`kubectl napwake status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) shows `pauses at 14:20 UTC (after GitOps conflict)`. A pause woken within the hour proves nothing, so the condition stays until one holds. Then it clears with a `GitOpsConflictResolved` event. ## Opting in a whole namespace The opt-in label is on the Namespace, which isn’t in Git when the tool creates it. See [A whole namespace](/next/guides/opt-in/#a-whole-namespace). | The tool creates it with | Put the label in | | ---------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | | Argo CD’s `CreateNamespace=true` | The Application’s `managedNamespaceMetadata` | | A Flux HelmRelease’s `install.createNamespace` | A Namespace you add to the kustomization | ## Ending dry-run Dry-run is the workload’s `napwake.dev/dry-run` annotation, its namespace’s, or a ManagedWorkload’s `spec.dryRun`. If Git has it, change it there, or the tool’s next sync puts it back. [`kubectl napwake enable`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#enable-a-workload) finds the tool, Argo CD by label (`app.kubernetes.io/instance`, its default) or annotation. | Who set it | `enable` | | ----------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A GitOps tool, from Git | Changes nothing, prints the change to make in Git, and exits non-zero. `--force` changes the cluster anyway | | Plain `helm` | Changes the cluster, but the next `helm upgrade` restores the chart’s value. Set `"false"` in the chart’s values too | Next * [Opting in →Label a workload, or a whole namespace.](/next/guides/opt-in/) * [Dry run →Measure a workload before it pauses.](/next/guides/dry-run/) * [kubectl plugin →`scan`, `status`, `enable` and `pause`.](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/)

# ManagedWorkload guide

> The ManagedWorkload CR is the core resource in Napwake.

1. Name the target. managedworkload.yaml 
   ```yaml
   apiVersion: napwake.dev/v1alpha1
   kind: ManagedWorkload
   metadata:
     name: my-api
     namespace: staging
   spec:
     target:
       kind: Deployment
       name: my-api
   ```
 Only `target` is required, and the rest takes its defaults. Napwake watches it, tracks its cost and learns its forecast, but pauses it only when you ask, with [`kubectl napwake pause`](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now). 2. Add an idle policy, and what else you need. managedworkload.yaml 
   ```yaml
   apiVersion: napwake.dev/v1alpha1
   kind: ManagedWorkload
   metadata:
     name: my-api
     namespace: staging
   spec:
     target:
       kind: Deployment
       name: my-api


     idlePolicy:
       idleAfter: 1h
       activity:
         cpuThreshold: 10
         prometheus:
           - promQL: 'sum(rate(nginx_ingress_controller_requests{exported_namespace="staging", exported_service="my-api"}[5m]))'
       autoResume: true


     wake:
       mode: Request
       maxWait: 2m
       page: true


     prediction:
       mode: balanced


     costTracking:
       rates:
         cpuPerHour: "0.031"
         memoryPerHour: "0.004"
         storagePerMonth: "0.08"


     dryRun: false
   ```
 With an `idlePolicy`, Napwake pauses it on its own. Each field’s type, default and limits are in the [API reference](/next/reference/api/). CheckpointAwake · until idle Once `my-api` has had no activity for `idleAfter`, Napwake pauses it. A request to its Service wakes it. ## What each part does | Field | What it does | | -------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `target` | The workload, in the same namespace. It can’t change once set, since a paused target would be left at zero. The API server refuses the edit. Create another ManagedWorkload for another workload | | `idlePolicy` | When it’s idle, and pauses. Deploys and the `napwake.dev/last-activity` and `napwake.dev/active-until` annotations always count as activity. See [Idle detection](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations) and [Prometheus activity](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/) | | `dependsOn` | Up to 20 workloads this one needs. They aren’t paused while it’s awake, and waking it wakes them. See [Dependencies](/next/concepts/dependencies/) | | `wake` | What wakes it through the doorman while paused. `mode: Request` is any request, and `mode: Ask` only a person’s click. See [Wake on request](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking) | | `prediction` | How soon the forecast acts. Leave it out, or write `prediction: {}`, for the default. See [Forecast setting](/next/concepts/forecasting/#forecast-setting) and [Forecasting](/next/concepts/forecasting/#phase-lifecycle) | | `costTracking` | Your own `rates`, in place of the nodes’ list price. None may be negative. See [Cost tracking](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#prices) | | `dryRun` | Measures without pausing, waking the workload if it was paused. See [Dry run](/next/guides/dry-run/) | ## Edit the one the label created Settings with more than one value aren’t annotations. Set them on the label’s ManagedWorkload, such as with `kubectl patch`, and Napwake keeps them. | The annotations own these. Edits are overwritten | Yours, and kept | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `dryRun`, `idlePolicy` (`idleAfter`, `activity.cpuThreshold`, `autoResume`, `pauseEarly`), the workloads `dependsOn` lists, `wake` | `idlePolicy.activity.prometheus`, `waitForReady` on a listed dependency, `costTracking`, and `prediction`, whose `confidence` percentage overrides `napwake.dev/forecast` | ## Scaled up outside Napwake A paused workload scaled up by anything else, such as `kubectl scale` or a deploy, wakes. Whoever did it wants it running. `status.lastScaledUp` records when, and what did it. For GitOps, see [GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/). While it runs, Napwake leaves its replicas to its team or HPA. ## One workload per target Only one ManagedWorkload can manage a Deployment or StatefulSet. A newer one for the same target gets a `DuplicateTarget` condition naming the other. It does nothing until the older one is gone. Next * [Pause and resume →What a pause changes.](/next/guides/pause/) * [API reference →Every field and its default.](/next/reference/api/)

# Node pools for real savings

> Give non-prod workloads node pools of their own, so pausing an environment empties whole nodes and what Napwake frees is saved.

Pausing frees what a workload’s pods requested on their nodes. You pay for nodes, so freed is saved only when a node goes ([Freed and saved](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#freed-and-saved)). Every environment on the pool is asleep, and nothing else runs there. Its nodes are empty, so the autoscaler removes them. ## Why a pool of their own On a node shared with busy workloads, a pause frees space but no node, and the bill doesn’t change. Consolidation helps. Karpenter’s `WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized` repacks pods, and Cluster Autoscaler removes nodes under its threshold. But packing is never perfect. A node a quarter full costs as much as a full one, and [some pods can’t move](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/) at all. On a pool of their own, staging, preview and dev are all that hold its nodes. As they pause the pool empties, the autoscaler removes its nodes, and saved rises to meet freed. At zero nodes, it saves all its paused workloads freed. | You need | Why | | ----------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A taint on its nodes | Keeps off what doesn’t tolerate it. That’s always-on workloads, and most `kube-system` pods, which hold a node Cluster Autoscaler won’t remove. It’s also Napwake’s operator and doorman, which are never paused and must stay up | | A label on its nodes | For the workloads’ `nodeSelector` (or node affinity), with a matching toleration | | An autoscaler that removes empty nodes promptly | And a minimum size of zero, where the provider allows it | ## Karpenter A non-prod NodePool with its own taint and label, which consolidates and prefers spot. It’s for `karpenter.sh/v1` and AWS’s `EC2NodeClass`. Elsewhere, or on EKS Auto Mode, change `nodeClassRef` (on Auto Mode, `group: eks.amazonaws.com` and `kind: NodeClass`). nodepool.yaml 
```yaml
apiVersion: karpenter.sh/v1
kind: NodePool
metadata:
  name: non-prod
spec:
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        example.com/tier: non-prod
    spec:
      nodeClassRef:
        group: karpenter.k8s.aws
        kind: EC2NodeClass
        name: default
      taints:
        - key: example.com/tier
          value: non-prod
          effect: NoSchedule
      requirements:
        - key: karpenter.sh/capacity-type
          operator: In
          values: ["spot", "on-demand"]
        - key: kubernetes.io/arch
          operator: In
          values: ["amd64"]
      expireAfter: 720h
  disruption:
    consolidationPolicy: WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized
    consolidateAfter: 5m
    budgets:
      - nodes: "100%"
        reasons: [Empty]
      - nodes: "10%"
  limits:
    cpu: "200"
    memory: 800Gi
```
 | Field | Why | | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized` | Repacks underused nodes too, for when only some environments are paused. `WhenEmpty` needs nodes with only DaemonSets left, rare in a shared pool. `Balanced` repacks only when the saving outweighs the disruption | | `consolidateAfter: 5m` | Restarts whenever a pod is added or removed. A pause is deliberate, after `idleAfter`, so there’s no need to wait long. Much shorter, and a workload woken soon after its pause finds its node gone | | `budgets` | When a whole environment pauses, the first lets every empty node go together. The second limits repacking busy ones | | `limits` | Caps the pool, so a runaway preview environment can’t grow it | | Spot | Why | | ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Suits non-prod | Staging can live with the odd interruption. Karpenter picks spot when the requirements allow | | Emptied spot nodes | Deleted by default. Swapping one for a cheaper spot node needs the `SpotToSpotConsolidation` feature gate | | Its price | Napwake prices spot at its list price, so freed and saved are close to what you pay. AWS’s spot price moves through the day ([Spot nodes](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#spot-nodes)) | [Cluster Autoscaler configuration](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/) has Karpenter’s defaults, `Never`, `expireAfter`, and what keeps a node. ## Cluster Autoscaler, GKE, AKS and EKS managed node groups With Cluster Autoscaler, managed or your own, the pool is its own node group. It has a taint, a label and a minimum of zero. * GKE Standard 
  ```bash
  gcloud container node-pools create non-prod \
    --cluster my-cluster --location us-central1 \
    --machine-type e2-standard-4 --spot \
    --enable-autoscaling --total-min-nodes 0 --total-max-nodes 20 \
    --node-labels example.com/tier=non-prod \
    --node-taints example.com/tier=non-prod:NoSchedule


  gcloud container clusters update my-cluster --location us-central1 \
    --autoscaling-profile optimize-utilization
  ```
 | For GKE Standard | | | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Zero nodes | GKE never scales a Standard cluster to zero, since system pods need a home. Keep another pool for them. This one can go to zero | | Labels | Its nodes also carry `cloud.google.com/gke-nodepool: non-prod`, which you can select instead | | `optimize-utilization` | Scales down sooner and more often than the default `balanced`, across the whole cluster | * AKS 
  ```bash
  az aks nodepool add \
    --resource-group my-rg --cluster-name my-cluster \
    --name nonprod --mode User \
    --node-vm-size Standard_D4s_v5 \
    --priority Spot --eviction-policy Delete --spot-max-price -1 \
    --enable-cluster-autoscaler --min-count 0 --max-count 20 \
    --labels example.com/tier=non-prod \
    --node-taints example.com/tier=non-prod:NoSchedule


  az aks update --resource-group my-rg --name my-cluster \
    --cluster-autoscaler-profile scale-down-unneeded-time=5m,scale-down-delay-after-add=5m
  ```
 | For AKS pools | | | ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Zero nodes | User pools scale to zero with `--min-count 0`. System pools can’t | | Spot | Spot pools get AKS’s own `kubernetes.azure.com/scalesetpriority=spot:NoSchedule` taint, so tolerate that too | | The profile | Applies to every autoscaled pool in the cluster | | Labels | Nodes carry `kubernetes.azure.com/agentpool: nonprod` | * EKS managed node group nodegroup.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: eksctl.io/v1alpha5
  kind: ClusterConfig
  metadata:
    name: my-cluster
    region: us-east-1
  managedNodeGroups:
    - name: non-prod
      instanceTypes: ["m6i.xlarge", "m5.xlarge", "m6a.xlarge"]
      spot: true
      minSize: 0
      desiredCapacity: 1
      maxSize: 20
      labels:
        example.com/tier: non-prod
      taints:
        - key: example.com/tier
          value: non-prod
          effect: NoSchedule
      iam:
        withAddonPolicies:
          autoScaler: true
  ```
 | For EKS node groups | | | ------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Scaling | EKS doesn’t scale managed node groups, but tags their Auto Scaling groups for Cluster Autoscaler. Run it with `--node-group-auto-discovery=asg:tag=k8s.io/cluster-autoscaler/enabled,k8s.io/cluster-autoscaler/my-cluster` | | Zero nodes | To scale up from zero, Cluster Autoscaler must know the nodes’ labels and taints first. Give its IAM role `eks:DescribeNodegroup` to read them from the EKS API. Or tag the group with `k8s.io/cluster-autoscaler/node-template/label/example.com/tier: non-prod` and `k8s.io/cluster-autoscaler/node-template/taint/example.com/tier: non-prod:NoSchedule` | | Labels | Nodes carry `eks.amazonaws.com/nodegroup: non-prod` | * Self-managed Cluster Autoscaler cluster-autoscaler-values.yaml 
  ```yaml
  autoDiscovery:
    clusterName: my-cluster
  extraArgs:
    scale-down-unneeded-time: 5m
    scale-down-delay-after-add: 5m
    scale-down-utilization-threshold: "0.5"
  ```
 Set the group’s minimum to zero in your cloud. Set its labels and taints as your provider’s Cluster Autoscaler docs say, so it scales up from zero. How soon a paused environment’s nodes go (what each setting means is in [Cluster Autoscaler configuration](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/)): | Setting | Default | For a non-prod pool | | ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `scale-down-unneeded-time` | `10m` | A paused environment’s node costs money this long. `5m` is plenty after an hour idle | | `scale-down-utilization-threshold` | `0.5` | An emptied pool doesn’t depend on it, since an empty node is always under | | `scale-down-delay-after-add` | `10m` | A wake scales up, so a shorter delay lets the next pause’s nodes go sooner | | `--skip-nodes-with-local-storage` | `true` upstream, `false` on AKS | Annotate `emptyDir` and `hostPath` pods with `cluster-autoscaler.kubernetes.io/safe-to-evict-local-volumes` rather than turning it off cluster-wide | | `--skip-nodes-with-system-pods` | `true` | The pool’s taint keeps most `kube-system` pods off | The settings follow the [Cluster Autoscaler FAQ](https://github.com/kubernetes/autoscaler/blob/master/cluster-autoscaler/FAQ.md), its [AWS provider README](https://github.com/kubernetes/autoscaler/blob/master/cluster-autoscaler/cloudprovider/aws/README.md), GKE’s [cluster autoscaler](https://cloud.google.com/kubernetes-engine/docs/concepts/cluster-autoscaler) and [`node-pools create`](https://cloud.google.com/sdk/gcloud/reference/container/node-pools/create) pages, and AKS’s [cluster autoscaler](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/aks/cluster-autoscaler) page. They live outside Napwake and change with your provider’s releases, so check them for your versions. ## Steering workloads to the pool Give the workloads both. The toleration lets them past the taint, and the `nodeSelector` keeps them on the pool. In the manifests you keep in Git: deployment.yaml 
```yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: my-api
  namespace: staging
  labels:
    napwake.dev/managed: "true"
  annotations:
    napwake.dev/wake-max-wait: "5m"
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: my-api
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: my-api
    spec:
      nodeSelector:
        example.com/tier: non-prod
      tolerations:
        - key: example.com/tier
          operator: Equal
          value: non-prod
          effect: NoSchedule
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: ghcr.io/example/my-api:1.4.2
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: 250m
              memory: 512Mi
```
 `example.com/tier: non-prod` stands for your label and taint. `wake-max-wait` is there because a wake may wait for a node ([What waking costs](#what-waking-costs)). For a whole namespace, add the selector and toleration as pods are created. Any of these works, and Napwake depends on none: | Way | How | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A policy engine | A Kyverno `mutate` rule or a Gatekeeper `Assign` mutation, matching pods in your namespaces | | The `PodNodeSelector` and `PodTolerationRestriction` admission plugins | They read defaults from the namespace’s annotations. Only where your control plane enables them, which managed providers mostly don’t | | Your templating | A Helm chart’s or Kustomize component’s `nodeSelector` and `tolerations`, in every non-prod release | Keep anything that never sleeps off the pool, such as a shared database no ManagedWorkload pauses. One always-on pod holds its node, and every pod that fits beside it. If paused workloads depend on it, consider managing it. Napwake [won’t pause it](/next/concepts/dependencies/) while a dependent is awake, and wakes it first when they wake. ## What waking costs On a pool scaled to nothing, a waking workload needs a node first. After a quiet night, the first request waits for one to be provisioned and join, often 30 to 90 seconds. A node group starting from zero takes longer. Then the images pull onto the fresh node, and the app starts. | Do | Why | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Raise `wake.maxWait` from its 2-minute default, such as to `5m` (`napwake.dev/wake-max-wait` with the label) | Otherwise the doorman gives up on held requests before the node is ready. The wake carries on ([How long a connection is held](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#how-long-a-connection-is-held)) | | Raise your ingress controller’s timeout to match | ingress-nginx gives up on an upstream after 60 seconds by default. Browsers get the [waking-up page](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#browsers) at once | | Use `autoResume`, for traffic the forecast can learn such as a team in at 9 every weekday | It wakes the workload 15 minutes before an hour it expects to be busy. The node is up before anyone arrives ([Resume](/next/guides/pause/#resume)) | | Keep one node warm, if every first request must be quick | A minimum of one, or in Karpenter a small always-on workload in the pool. You pay for that node, and pausing saves the rest | ## Check it’s working 
```bash
kubectl napwake status
```
 [`status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) shows freed beside saved this month, per workload and in total. SAVED follows FREED a little behind, as a node takes `consolidateAfter` or `scale-down-unneeded-time` to go. If under half the freed was saved, Needs attention says nodes aren’t scaling down. 
```bash
kubectl napwake nodes
```
 [`nodes`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-why-nodes-dont-scale-down) names the autoscaler, and for each node what keeps it, in BLOCKED BY. A non-prod node with low utilisation and something in BLOCKED BY is the one to look at. [Freed but not saved](/next/operations/troubleshooting/#freed-but-not-saved) explains each reason. The metrics, by `pool`, the node pool’s name: 
```promql
# What the pool's paused workloads requested, against its spare capacity
napwake_capacity_paused_cpu_cores{pool="non-prod"}
napwake_capacity_spare_cpu_cores{pool="non-prod"}


# What pausing takes off the bill an hour, in the pool
napwake_savings_realised_dollars_per_hour{pool="non-prod"}


# Which node autoscaler the operator found
napwake_node_autoscaler
```
 As nodes go, spare capacity falls towards zero, and saved an hour rises towards what the paused replicas would cost. Spare capacity above what’s paused means the nodes are still there, so check `kubectl napwake nodes` ([Metrics reference](/next/reference/metrics/#savings)). CheckpointAsleep · the pool's nodes gone SAVED in `kubectl napwake status` follows FREED, a little behind. Next * [Cluster Autoscaler configuration →Settings for each node autoscaler.](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/) * [Cost tracking →How freed and saved are measured.](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/)

# Opting in

> Install Napwake once per cluster, then label what it should manage.

1. Label the workload, measuring first. deployment.yaml 
   ```yaml
   apiVersion: apps/v1
   kind: Deployment
   metadata:
     name: checkout-api
     namespace: preview-42
     labels:
       napwake.dev/managed: "true"
     annotations:
       napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"      # measure first; nothing is paused
       napwake.dev/idle-after: "2h"
   ```
 Napwake creates a [ManagedWorkload](/next/guides/managed-workload/) for it, kept in step with the annotations. The label is usually one line in Helm values or manifests in Git. 2. See what pausing would free. 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake scan
   ```
 It lists every workload in [dry-run](/next/guides/dry-run/#what-dry-run-measured). 3. Start pausing. 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake enable checkout-api -n preview-42
   kubectl napwake enable --all -n preview-42     # every workload in the namespace
   ```
 `enable` sets the workload’s own `napwake.dev/dry-run` to `"false"`, which wins wherever the dry-run came from. See [`enable`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#enable-a-workload) for `--all`, GitOps and Helm. CheckpointAwake · until idle Napwake pauses the workload once it has had no activity for `idle-after`, 2 hours here. Put the label on the workload itself A chart’s `podLabels` and `podAnnotations` label the pods, where Napwake doesn’t look, and a `LabelIgnored` warning event says so. For Kustomize, see [With Kustomize](#with-kustomize). ## A whole namespace Label a namespace to opt in every Deployment and StatefulSet in it, with its annotations as their defaults. It suits dev and preview namespaces, and third-party charts you can’t label. * Namespace 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: Namespace
  metadata:
    name: preview-42
    labels:
      napwake.dev/managed: "true"
    annotations:
      napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"
      napwake.dev/idle-after: "2h"
  ```
 * Argo CD An Application with `CreateNamespace=true` has no Namespace in Git. Put the label and annotations in `managedNamespaceMetadata`, which Argo CD sets on the namespace it creates. application.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Application
  metadata:
    name: preview-42
    namespace: argocd
  spec:
    project: default
    source:
      repoURL: https://github.com/your-org/environments.git
      targetRevision: main
      path: previews/preview-42
    destination:
      server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
      namespace: preview-42
    syncPolicy:
      automated: {}
      managedNamespaceMetadata:
        labels:
          napwake.dev/managed: "true"
        annotations:
          napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"
          napwake.dev/idle-after: "2h"
      syncOptions:
        - CreateNamespace=true
  ```
 Argo CD applies it only in a sync with `CreateNamespace=true`, so an existing namespace gets it at the next sync. Adding it starts that sync only with `prune: true` and `selfHeal: true`. Otherwise press **Sync** in the UI, which takes the sync options, or give them with kubectl: 
  ```bash
  kubectl patch application preview-42 -n argocd --type merge -p '{"operation":{"sync":{"syncOptions":["CreateNamespace=true"]}}}'
  ```
 To stop, remove the label from `managedNamespaceMetadata` and sync. Removing `managedNamespaceMetadata` leaves the label on, as Argo CD then leaves the namespace alone. Leave replicas to Napwake too, as in [With GitOps](#with-gitops). * Flux Flux doesn’t create a Kustomization’s namespace, so it’s in Git. Label and annotate it there, as `namespace.yaml` beside the kustomization: kustomization.yaml 
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: kustomize.config.k8s.io/v1beta1
  kind: Kustomization
  namespace: preview-42
  resources:
    - namespace.yaml
    - ../../base
  ```
 A HelmRelease’s `install.createNamespace` leaves its namespace unlabelled, so add the Namespace to the Kustomization that applies it. To stop, remove the label and keep the Namespace. With `prune: true`, Flux deletes a Namespace that leaves Git, and everything in it. To leave one workload out, label it `napwake.dev/ignore: "true"` or `napwake.dev/managed: "false"`. ## With Kustomize Use one of these. Each leaves the workloads’ selectors alone. * The Namespace If the kustomization has the Namespace, label and annotate it there, as in [A whole namespace](#a-whole-namespace). Nothing on the workloads changes. * Every resource `labels` with `includeSelectors: false` labels each resource’s own metadata only: kustomization.yaml 
  ```yaml
  resources:
    - ../../base
  labels:
    - pairs:
        napwake.dev/managed: "true"
      includeSelectors: false
  ```
 Leave out `includeTemplates`. It labels the pods too, restarting them for nothing. It also labels a StatefulSet’s `volumeClaimTemplates`, which fails as the selector does. Services and ConfigMaps get the label, which Napwake ignores. So does a Namespace in the kustomization, opting in the whole namespace. * Chosen workloads A patch changes only the workload’s own labels and annotations, settings included: kustomization.yaml 
  ```yaml
  resources:
    - ../../base
  patches:
    - target:
        kind: Deployment
        name: checkout-api
      patch: |
        apiVersion: apps/v1
        kind: Deployment
        metadata:
          name: checkout-api
          labels:
            napwake.dev/managed: "true"
          annotations:
            napwake.dev/dry-run: "true"
            napwake.dev/idle-after: "2h"
  ```
 Keep the `target`. Without it, the build fails with `no resource matches strategic merge patch` if the manifest has a namespace the patch doesn’t give. For every Deployment and StatefulSet, target `kind: Deployment|StatefulSet` with no name, and keep the patch’s `name`, which is ignored. Don’t use `commonLabels` It changes selectors, which Kubernetes doesn’t let change. Kustomize puts it in each workload’s `spec.selector` and a StatefulSet’s `volumeClaimTemplates` too. | Applied to | What happens | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | An existing workload | The apply fails with `field is immutable`. Its Service can take the new selector, which no running pod has, and stop sending them traffic | | An existing workload, by a tool that recreates what it can’t change, such as Flux with `force: true` | The tool deletes and recreates the workloads | | A new workload | It applies, but removing the label later fails the same way | `labels` with `includeSelectors: true`, which `kustomize edit fix` makes of `commonLabels`, does the same. `commonAnnotations` puts settings on the pod templates too. Adding or changing one, such as `dry-run`, then restarts every workload’s pods. Napwake counts each restart as a [deploy](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#what-counts-as-activity), which is activity. Use the Namespace or a patch. ## Settings Set them on the workload or its namespace. [Settings annotations](/next/reference/labels-annotations/#settings-annotations) lists the values each accepts. | Annotation | Default | What it does | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake.dev/dry-run` | `"false"` | Measures, and records what pausing would free, without pausing | | `napwake.dev/idle-after` | `1h` | Time without activity before pausing, at least `1m` | | `napwake.dev/cpu-threshold` | `10` | CPU use, as a percentage of requests, above which it’s active | | [`napwake.dev/auto-resume`](/next/concepts/forecasting/) | `"false"` | Wakes it ahead of the demand a confident forecast predicts | | [`napwake.dev/pause-early`](/next/concepts/forecasting/#pausing-early) | `"false"` | Pauses before `idle-after` runs out when a confident forecast expects quiet. Never with `wake: "ask"` | | [`napwake.dev/forecast`](/next/concepts/forecasting/#forecast-setting) | `"balanced"` | How soon the forecast acts. `"eager"`, `"balanced"` or `"cautious"` | | [`napwake.dev/depends-on`](/next/concepts/dependencies/) | none | Workloads this one needs | | [`napwake.dev/wake`](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#the-wake-setting) | `"request"` | What wakes it through the doorman. `"request"` is any request. `"ask"` is only a person’s click, for a [public URL](/next/guides/preview-environments/) | | `napwake.dev/wake-max-wait` | `2m` | How long a request is held while it wakes, at most `1h` | | `napwake.dev/wake-page` | `"true"` | Shows browsers a waking-up page | Leave one out for its default. A value that can’t be read is skipped, with an `InvalidSetting` warning event, and the next place below decides. An unreadable `dry-run` turns dry-run on instead, and an unreadable `wake` asks. ### Which setting wins 1. 1The workload's annotation 2. 2Its namespace's 3. 3The Helm defaults`defaults.idleAfter`, `defaults.cpuThreshold`, `defaults.dryRun`, `defaults.pauseEarly`, `defaults.forecast`, `defaults.wake` 4. 4The built-in default So a namespace can measure everything with `dry-run: "true"` while one workload in it goes live with `dry-run: "false"`. ## Protected namespaces 
```bash
kubectl label namespace payments napwake.dev/protected=true
```
 Napwake never manages anything in a protected namespace, such as production, whatever the labels. A warning event on each workload says why. | In a protected namespace | What happens | | ----------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | | A labelled workload | It isn’t opted in | | A ManagedWorkload you wrote | It reports `Protected=True` and never pauses | | A workload Napwake had paused | It’s woken, so protecting never leaves anything off | | Always protected, with no label | So that | | --------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- | | `kube-system` and every other `kube-*` | Labelling every namespace can’t pause CoreDNS or metrics-server | | Napwake’s own namespace, and the doorman’s if it runs elsewhere | Napwake can’t pause itself | Protect more by name, such as every `prod-*`, with the Helm value `protectedNamespaces`. Only `napwake.dev/allow-protected: "true"`, set on purpose, lets Napwake manage a protected namespace. Napwake never sets either label. The operator’s and the doorman’s own Deployments are never managed, since nothing would be left to wake them. To keep Napwake out by Kubernetes RBAC, install it with [`watchNamespaces`](/next/reference/helm-values/#namespaces). ## With GitOps GitOps tools leave the ManagedWorkload alone, but must leave the replicas to Napwake. The ManagedWorkload is owned by the workload, like a Deployment’s ReplicaSets, so Argo CD shows it under the workload. It never takes the workload’s labels, so a tool’s tracking label can’t make the tool claim and prune it. A tool would scale a paused workload back up. [GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/) shows how to stop that for Argo CD, Flux, Fleet, kapp and Kluctl. Under Flux, many charts let you leave `replicas` out, such as when their autoscaling value is on. ## What Napwake does with the label | When you | Napwake | | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Label a workload | Creates a ManagedWorkload named after it, owned by it and marked `napwake.dev/from-label: "true"`, with a `Managed` event on the workload. When the name is taken, as by a StatefulSet named like a Deployment, it adds the kind, such as `api-statefulset` | | Change its annotations | Updates the ManagedWorkload within seconds | | Remove the label, or add `napwake.dev/ignore` | Deletes it, first scaling a paused workload back to its replicas | | Delete the workload | Deletes its ManagedWorkload too | | Wrote a ManagedWorkload for it | Yours wins, and the label does nothing | | Set the label to anything but `"true"`, such as `"dry-run"` | Doesn’t opt it in, and says so in an event. Settings like dry-run are annotations | Settings with more than one value, such as Prometheus activity queries, aren’t annotations. [Set them on the ManagedWorkload](/next/guides/managed-workload/#edit-the-one-the-label-created) the label created, which keeps them. Next * [Dry run →What dry-run measures.](/next/guides/dry-run/) * [ManagedWorkload →Settings annotations don't cover.](/next/guides/managed-workload/) * [GitOps tools →Leave replicas to Napwake.](/next/guides/gitops/)

# Pause and resume

> Napwake's one action is to pause a workload, scaling it to zero replicas.

1. Awake · running Pods running 2. No activity for `idleAfter`, or `kubectl napwake pause` 3. Asleep · paused Scaled to zero Its PVCs and their data stay. 4. A request, activity, `autoResume` or `kubectl napwake wake` 5. Waking up · resuming Scaled back up To the replicas it had, within its HPA's or ScaledObject's range. Its replicas are Ready Napwake never deletes a workload or its storage. A StatefulSet whose `persistentVolumeClaimRetentionPolicy.whenScaled` is `Delete` has its own controller delete its PVCs as it scales down. So Napwake never pauses one. It reports `Protected=True`, reason `PVCsDeletedOnScaleDown`, until `whenScaled` is `Retain`. ## Pause now When you’re done with a workload for the day, such as a preview environment, pause it without waiting for `idleAfter`. 1. Ask for the pause. 
   ```bash
   kubectl napwake pause my-api -n staging
   ```
 Name the workload as `kubectl napwake status` shows it. It needs no `idlePolicy`, and works on a ManagedWorkload from the label too. 2. Read the answer in the `PauseRequest` condition. 
   ```bash
   kubectl describe managedworkload my-api -n staging
   ```
 Its message says what happened and when, in UTC. It reads `paused as requested at 2026-10-21 03:32 UTC`, `already paused at …`, or `not paused at 2026-10-21 03:32 UTC:` and why. It answers the last request until the next one, through any wakes and pauses, so it outlives the events. CheckpointAsleep · paused The workload is `Paused`, the same as after an idle pause. Anything in [Resume](#resume) wakes it. The request runs the idle clock out now, and the pause follows the rules of an idle one: | The workload | What Napwake does | `PauseRequest` | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `Running`, `Idle` or `Resuming` | Pauses it, after the wake if it’s `Resuming`, with a `PauseRequested` event, then `Paused` | `True`, `Paused` | | A request comes as it pauses | [Calls the pause off](#a-request-calls-the-pause-off), and says so. `kubectl napwake pause` says it wasn’t paused | `False`, `PauseCancelled` | | `Pausing` or `Paused` | Finishes any pause under way, then marks the request handled | `True`, `AlreadyPaused` | | In the hour Napwake waits after a GitOps tool undid its last pause | Pauses it, since you asked. If the tool undoes it again, that’s a new [`GitOpsConflict`](/next/guides/gitops/) | `True`, `Paused` | | Busy within the hour, by a confident forecast | Leaves it running and says when, since it would likely be woken straight back. `kubectl napwake pause` asks whether to pause anyway, and `--yes` does | `False`, `ForecastExpectsDemand` | | In dry-run | Never scales it, and [counts a would-be pause](/next/guides/dry-run/#what-happens-in-dry-run) | `False`, `DryRun` | | Held awake by `napwake.dev/active-until` on it or its workload, depended on by awake workloads, in a `dependsOn` cycle, in a [protected namespace](/next/guides/opt-in/#protected-namespaces), or Napwake’s own operator or doorman | Leaves it running, with a Warning event, or `HeldByDependents` for dependents | `False`, `ActiveUntil`, `HeldByDependents`, `DependencyCycle` or `Protected` | | Scaled to zero outside Napwake | Nothing, since it’s off already | `False`, `ScaledToZero` | | Missing, labelled `napwake.dev/ignore`, or managed by another ManagedWorkload | Nothing, with a Warning event | `False`, `TargetNotFound`, `TargetIgnored` or `DuplicateTarget` | | Already on the ManagedWorkload as Napwake takes it on, or takes a pause up again after its status was lost | Nothing. It may have been answered before, and brought back with the ManagedWorkload, as by a backup restored without status. Set a new value to ask | `False`, `PredatesStatus` | A request Napwake won’t act on is answered once, so ask again once the reason is gone. Napwake keeps its record of answered requests in the ManagedWorkload’s status. So a request already on it as Napwake takes it on counts as answered. Otherwise a backup restored without status would bring back requests answered long ago. So would `kubectl replace --force`, or an old manifest applied again. Set the annotation once the ManagedWorkload is `Running`. Activity from before the request doesn’t undo it. Only an activity annotation changed since wakes it. A query already active at the pause, such as a queue with messages, wakes it once it has been quiet. ## What happens in a pause Before the pods stop, the [doorman](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) steps in front of the workload’s Services, beside its pods. So no request is refused as they stop. | When | What happens | | -------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The move to `Pausing` | The doorman’s routes go into `status.doorman`, and every doorman pod listens on them. `status.pause.routesWrittenAt` records when they were stored | | 5 seconds after, once every doorman pod answers on those ports | The operator checks that by connecting to each. The doorman’s EndpointSlice then stands beside the pods’ own. The doorman passes what it gets straight to a Ready pod. `status.pause.routedAt` records when | | 5 seconds after that | kube-proxy and ingress controllers send to it too. The pause goes on, however late Napwake gets to it | | A workload the doorman doesn’t route | The pause goes on at once | | At most | None waits more than a minute for the doorman | Then Napwake scales the workload to zero. [Lifecycle](/next/concepts/lifecycle/#idempotency) has what it records before it scales anything. ### Work in progress at a pause Kubernetes stops the pods as it stops any pod. Each gets `SIGTERM`, then its `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` (30 seconds by default) to finish before `SIGKILL`. Napwake waits for them to stop ([how long](/next/operations/troubleshooting/#workload-paused-with-pods-still-running)). Requests that come meanwhile go to the doorman, which holds them. An app that should finish a job or drain connections needs to handle `SIGTERM`, as in a rollout. One that exits the moment it gets `SIGTERM` can refuse a connection. kube-proxy may send it one in the second before it drops the pod. A `preStop` sleep of a few seconds closes that gap, in a pause as in any rollout. ### A request calls the pause off A request while a workload pauses shows it’s in use. Whatever would wake it once paused calls the pause off, if it comes while it’s `Pausing`: * a request that reaches the doorman, which holds it, or passes it on to a pod that’s still Ready * for a workload that [asks before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking), only a person’s click on the asleep page * a [wake request](#wake-now) * an activity annotation, which a waking [dependent](/next/concepts/dependencies/) stamps The workload is `Running` again, scaled back up if its pods had begun to stop. The request is answered, held while no pod is Ready. A `PauseCancelled` event says what came: 
```text
a request came as it paused; the pause is cancelled
```
 The doorman doesn’t see every request as a workload pauses. The pods answer these, and the pause goes on: * requests in the first 5 seconds or more, before the doorman’s EndpointSlice stands beside the pods’ own * what kube-proxy and ingress controllers still send to the pods, for about 5 seconds more, until they’re scaled down * requests on connections opened before the pause * every request after the first on a connection the doorman passed on * traffic from a GKE load balancer through [NEGs](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs), which goes to the pods’ own addresses A probe, a scrape, a hunt for hidden files, or a TCP check that sends nothing doesn’t call it off. Dry-run never pauses, so there’s nothing to call off. A pause the idle clock made, called off this way, counts as [made in use](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#paused-while-in-use). The clock then waits longer before the next. ## Resume | What happens | The paused workload | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | A request reaches its Service | Wakes, through [wake on request](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) | | An [activity annotation](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations), `napwake.dev/last-activity`, `napwake.dev/last-request` or `napwake.dev/active-until`, changes on the ManagedWorkload or its target | Wakes, whatever time it states | | A new `napwake.dev/wake-requested` value on the ManagedWorkload, as [`kubectl napwake wake`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#wake-a-workload) sets it | Wakes, whatever the wake setting, with a `WakeRequested` event ([Wake now](#wake-now)) | | A [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) shows activity, such as messages waiting for a queue worker | Wakes | | `autoResume: true`, and a confident forecast expects demand in the coming hour | Wakes 15 minutes before the hour | | Something else scales it up, such as `kubectl scale` | Counts it as activity, and goes straight to `Running`. For a GitOps tool, see [GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/) | | [Dry-run](/next/guides/dry-run/) is turned on | Wakes, with a `DryRunWake` event. Dry-run never leaves a workload paused | Napwake looks at a paused workload at least every 5 minutes, or every minute with Prometheus activity queries. It also looks 15 minutes before each hour, on the hour, and whenever the workload or its target changes. ### Wake now 
```bash
kubectl napwake wake my-api -n staging
```
 `wake` asks Napwake to wake the workload now, whatever its [wake setting](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#the-wake-setting). So it wakes one that [asks before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking) too. A paused workload wakes with its [dependencies](/next/concepts/dependencies/), and an awake one’s idle clock restarts. `--for 2h` keeps it awake at least that long, as for a demo. `wake` waits for Napwake’s answer, then until it’s Running. Any tool can ask the same way, such as a pull request bot, a developer portal or a chat command. It sets `napwake.dev/wake-requested` on the ManagedWorkload, as `wake` does. The value only has to differ from the last request’s, such as the time or a CI run’s ID: 
```bash
kubectl annotate managedworkload my-api -n staging --overwrite \
  napwake.dev/wake-requested="$(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ)"
```
 A ManagedWorkload from the label is named after its workload. When that name is taken, it adds the kind, such as `my-api-statefulset`. It’s an annotation, like `pause-requested`, so a GitOps tool that owns the ManagedWorkload’s spec leaves it alone. | To | It takes, on `managedworkloads` in the namespace | | --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ | | Set it with `kubectl annotate` | `get`, `patch` | | Set it from an HTTP client | `patch` | | Wait for the answer with `kubectl wait` | `get`, `list`, `watch` | | Run `kubectl napwake wake` | `get`, `list`, `patch` | The built-in `edit` and `admin` roles include all of them once Napwake is installed. [Public preview environments](/next/guides/preview-environments/#the-access-tooling-needs) gives a minimal Role. Napwake acts on each value once, recording it in `status.lastWakeRequest`, which survives an operator restart. The `WakeRequest` condition says what came of it: | The workload | What Napwake does | `WakeRequest` | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- | | `Paused` | Wakes it, with a `WakeRequested` event, then `Resumed` once it’s Ready | `True`, `Woken` | | `Pausing` | [Calls the pause off](#a-request-calls-the-pause-off), and has it `Running` again | `True`, `Woken` | | `Resuming` | Lets the wake finish, then marks the request handled | `True`, `AlreadyAwake` | | `Running` or `Idle` | Restarts its idle clock, as activity does | `True`, `AlreadyAwake` | | In [dry-run](/next/guides/dry-run/) | Restarts its idle clock, which ends a would-be pause | `True`, `AlreadyAwake` | | Scaled to zero outside Napwake | Leaves it off. Napwake only wakes what it paused | `False`, `ScaledToZero` | | In a [protected namespace](/next/guides/opt-in/#protected-namespaces), Napwake’s own operator or doorman, or a StatefulSet that deletes its PVCs when scaled down | Nothing, since Napwake never pauses it | `False`, `Protected` | | Missing, labelled `napwake.dev/ignore`, or managed by another ManagedWorkload | Nothing, with a warning event | `False`, `TargetNotFound`, `TargetIgnored` or `DuplicateTarget` | | Its ManagedWorkload is being deleted | Hands the workload back, with the replicas it had | `False`, `BeingDeleted` | | Set before Napwake has taken the ManagedWorkload on, as by a tool that creates it and asks at once | Takes it on `Running`, so it’s awake, and restarts its idle clock | `True`, `AlreadyAwake` | | Already on the ManagedWorkload as Napwake takes a pause up again after its status was lost | Nothing. It may have been answered before, as with a backup restored without status, so the workload stays paused. Set a new value to ask | `False`, `PredatesStatus` | The condition answers the last request until the next one, through every pause and wake since. Its message says what happened and when, in UTC. It reads `woken as requested at 2026-10-23 09:05 UTC`, `already awake at …`, or `not woken at 2026-10-23 09:05 UTC:` and why. 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="WakeRequest")]}'
```
 A request Napwake can’t act on is answered once, not retried. Ask again once the reason is gone. Unlike a pause request, a wake request may be set any time, even before Napwake has taken the ManagedWorkload on. At worst an old one restarts the idle clock. A resume wakes the workload’s [dependencies](/next/concepts/dependencies/), then scales it back to `status.pause.previousReplicas`. It’s `Running` once those replicas are Ready, by a status its own controller wrote after the scale-up. Once it’s scaled back up, its autoscaler owns the replicas. An HPA scaling it down as it starts isn’t undone. A workload with a pod crash-looping before its pause would never get there. So Napwake stops waiting, runs it with its Ready replicas, and sets `ResumeIncomplete=True`. With none Ready, its doorman routes go anyway. Requests then fail until a pod is Ready, rather than wait at the doorman for a workload its own controller reports stuck.
