A StatefulSet with the default `OrderedReady` pod management starts its pods one at a time. A slow-starting one may have only its first pods Ready when the 2 minutes are up. It runs with those, and the rest follow. [Lifecycle](/next/concepts/lifecycle/#transition-triggers) has the timings. ## When Napwake lets go When Napwake stops managing a paused workload, it hands it back. It scales it to the replicas it had and releases it from KEDA, without waiting for Ready. Its Services stop routing to the doorman. A `Resumed` event says why, and how many replicas it has. That happens when: * The ManagedWorkload is deleted, or the `napwake.dev/managed` label that created it is removed. * The workload is labelled `napwake.dev/ignore: "true"`. * Its namespace becomes [protected](/next/guides/opt-in/#protected-namespaces). * It’s a StatefulSet that deletes its PVCs when scaled down. * It’s Napwake’s own operator or doorman, the Deployments labelled `control-plane: controller-manager` or `doorman` in Napwake’s namespace. Those are never paused, whatever the namespace allows. * An older ManagedWorkload for the same workload takes it over, and this one becomes the `DuplicateTarget`. So no longer managing a workload never leaves it switched off. The `napwake.dev/cleanup` finalizer makes sure of it when a ManagedWorkload is deleted, retrying a failed hand-back. In a namespace being deleted, the workload goes too. So after 5 minutes the ManagedWorkload [lets go](/next/operations/troubleshooting/#a-managedworkload-let-go-of-its-workload-without-handing-it-back) with a `HandBackAbandoned` Warning event. If the target itself is deleted while paused, its Services stop routing to the doorman. Nothing would start the pods. `TargetAvailable` turns `False`, reason `TargetNotFound`. ## Workloads already at zero A workload scaled to zero outside Napwake is off on purpose. That’s by `kubectl scale`, a pipeline, or KEDA with no active trigger. Napwake leaves it off, so scaling to zero yourself keeps a workload off until someone starts it. | When | What happens | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Its idle clock runs out, or you ask for a pause | Nothing. A [pause request](#pause-now) gets `ScaledToZero`, since a pause would record zero replicas to restore | | A request, an activity annotation, `autoResume` or `kubectl napwake wake` | Nothing wakes it. Napwake only wakes what it paused | | A request to one of its Services | It fails, as without Napwake. Its Services aren’t routed to the doorman, so nothing waits for pods nothing will start | The ManagedWorkload stays `Running`, with the `ScaledToZero` condition and one `ScaledToZero` event. Once whoever scaled it down sets its replicas above zero, Napwake manages it again. That counts as activity, so its idle clock starts afresh. Napwake never scales up a workload it didn’t scale down. A pause recorded at zero replicas is handed back at zero, with a `Resumed` event saying so. ## While it’s paused A paused workload costs only its storage. [Cost tracking](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/), always on, counts the rest: | While paused | Accrues as | | -------------------------------------------------------- | ---------- | | What its replicas requested in CPU and memory | Freed | | The part of that its nodes’ spare capacity couldn’t hold | Saved | | Its PVCs, since they stay | Cost | To remove one nobody comes back to, delete it the way it was created. That’s from Git, by the pipeline that made it, or with a TTL tool such as kube-janitor. Those know when an environment is finished. Napwake only knows it’s idle. Napwake records what a pause changes before it scales anything. A pause interrupted by a restart is then finished or undone from the record. [Lifecycle](/next/concepts/lifecycle/#idempotency) has how. Next * [Wake on request →How a request wakes it.](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/) * [HPA and KEDA →Pausing an autoscaled workload.](/next/guides/autoscalers/) * [Lifecycle →Every phase, condition and event.](/next/concepts/lifecycle/)

# Public preview environments

> Have a preview environment on a public URL ask before waking, so people wake it and robots don't.

1. Asleep · paused Asks first A browser gets the asleep page, with a **Wake it** button. Robots get a `503`. 2. A person clicks **Wake it** 3. Waking up · resuming Woken by a person The waking-up page carries on. 4. A pod is Ready 5. Awake · running Up The page they opened loads. On the public internet, most requests are robots. Crawlers, link previewers and scanners try every address, and many pass for browsers. A preview that wakes on any request wakes every few minutes, and hardly ever stays paused. Its hostname doesn’t stay hidden. Once it has an HTTPS certificate, it’s in public certificate logs within minutes, and scanners reach it by name. With `ask`, a person’s browser gets a page with a **Wake it** button, and only the click wakes the preview. Your tooling wakes it with an annotation. Behind a VPN, single sign-on or an allowlist, keep the default, `request` ([Environments on the public internet](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#environments-on-the-public-internet)). 1. Opt in each preview’s namespace, asking first. * Namespace namespace.yaml 
     ```yaml
     apiVersion: v1
     kind: Namespace
     metadata:
       name: preview-42
       labels:
         napwake.dev/managed: "true"
       annotations:
         napwake.dev/wake: "ask"
     ```
 Leave `replicas` out of the workloads’ manifests, and Kubernetes starts a new Deployment at one replica. A deploy that sets them scales a paused preview up, which wakes it. A GitOps tool that sets them undoes every pause ([GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/)). * Argo CD An Application that creates the namespace, with `CreateNamespace=true`, sets them in `managedNamespaceMetadata`. It must leave the replicas to Napwake too, or it scales the preview up after every pause. application.yaml 
     ```yaml
     apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
     kind: Application
     metadata:
       name: preview-42
       namespace: argocd
     spec:
       project: default
       source:
         repoURL: https://github.com/your-org/previews.git
         targetRevision: main
         path: previews/preview-42
       destination:
         server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
         namespace: preview-42
       ignoreDifferences:
         - group: apps
           kind: Deployment
           jsonPointers:
             - /spec/replicas
       syncPolicy:
         automated: {}
         managedNamespaceMetadata:
           labels:
             napwake.dev/managed: "true"
           annotations:
             napwake.dev/wake: "ask"
         syncOptions:
           - CreateNamespace=true
           - RespectIgnoreDifferences=true
     ```
 Argo CD sets `managedNamespaceMetadata` only in a sync with `CreateNamespace=true`. For a namespace it created before, see the Argo CD tab of [A whole namespace](/next/guides/opt-in/#a-whole-namespace). For StatefulSets, see [With GitOps](/next/guides/opt-in/#with-gitops). * Kustomize With Flux, or anything that applies a kustomization, put the Namespace in it, as `namespace.yaml` beside it. kustomization.yaml 
     ```yaml
     apiVersion: kustomize.config.k8s.io/v1beta1
     kind: Kustomization
     namespace: preview-42
     resources:
       - namespace.yaml
       - ../base
     ```
 Leave `replicas` out of the base’s manifests. Flux sets the replicas a manifest has, so it would scale the preview up after every pause. That’s a `GitOpsConflict`, which holds off the next pause for an hour ([Flux](/next/guides/gitops/#flux)). * Every workload values.yaml 
     ```yaml
     defaults:
       wake: ask
     ```
 Every opted-in workload asks, whatever its namespace, unless its own annotations or its namespace’s say `"request"`. * ManagedWorkload managedworkload.yaml 
     ```yaml
     spec:
       wake:
         mode: Ask
     ```
 For a [ManagedWorkload](/next/guides/managed-workload/) you wrote. The spec spells it `Ask`, and the annotation `ask`. A workload’s own `napwake.dev/wake` wins over its namespace’s, and that over the Helm default ([Which setting wins](/next/guides/opt-in/#which-setting-wins)). The annotation takes exactly `"request"` or `"ask"`. Any other value asks too, with an `InvalidSetting` warning event, so a typo never lets robots wake a preview. 2. Check it once the preview has paused, after an hour with no activity by default. 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkloads -n preview-42 \
     -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,WAKE:.spec.wake.mode,PHASE:.status.phase,DOORMAN:.status.conditions[?(@.type=="WakeOnRequest")].reason'
   ```
 
   ```text
   NAME   WAKE      PHASE    DOORMAN
   api    Request   Paused   DoormanRouted
   web    Ask       Paused   DoormanRouted
   ```
 Any other reason under DOORMAN says why requests can’t reach the doorman ([When a workload isn’t routed](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#when-a-workload-isnt-routed)). 3. Ask for it as a robot would. 
   ```bash
   curl -i https://pr-42.preview.example.com/
   ```
 It gets a `503` saying how to wake it ([what robots get](#what-robots-and-api-clients-see)), and the preview stays paused. 4. Open it in a browser, and click **Wake it**. CheckpointWaking up · woken by the click The waking-up page carries on until a pod is Ready, then the page you opened loads. `curl` woke nothing. It needs plain HTTP The doorman reads each request to tell a browser from a robot. So end TLS at the ingress or load balancer in front of the preview, as is usual. A workload that ends TLS itself is never woken by a click. Behind a GKE Ingress or Gateway, it also needs the doorman’s [forwarder](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs). ## A preview of more than one workload Every workload in the namespace asks, which suits what people open in a browser. An API the frontend calls wakes with it when it’s a [dependency](/next/concepts/dependencies/) of the frontend. So does a database or a queue worker. Napwake learns that from the frontend’s environment, such as an `API_URL` naming the API’s Service. Or declare it with `napwake.dev/depends-on`. One click then wakes the whole preview, and its dependencies stay up as long as the frontend does. To see what wakes with a workload: 
```bash
kubectl napwake deps web -n preview-42
```
 
```text
preview-42/web (Deployment, Paused)
Depends on:
  preview-42/api   learned from API_URL   Paused
Depended on by:
  nothing
Learned links come from the dependent's environment or its requests; napwake.dev/ignore-dependencies drops one.
```
 A workload that’s nobody’s dependency is woken only by tooling while it asks. Its callers are other workloads, which are turned away. A database’s protocol isn’t HTTP, so its connections are closed. Have it wake on any request instead, with the annotation on the workload, which wins over the namespace’s. No robot reaches a Service that no Ingress exposes, so only its real callers wake it. deployment.yaml 
```yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: api
  annotations:
    napwake.dev/wake: "request"
```
 See [When to keep waking on request](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#when-to-keep-waking-on-request). ## What people see Someone opens the preview, say `https://pr-42.preview.example.com/orders/7`, and gets the asleep page. | | The asleep page | | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Says | The preview is asleep, with a **Wake it** button | | Is sent as | `200 OK`, with `Cache-Control: no-store`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex` and `Referrer-Policy: same-origin` | | Runs | Only its own style and script. No other site can show it in a frame | | Names | No namespace, workload or Service | | Reloads | Every minute, with a fresh token, so a page left open doesn’t go stale | | Wakes | Nothing. Only the click does | The button posts back to the same address, with a token the doorman signed for this preview and this pause. It’s good for 10 minutes. A current browser says the click came from the page, with `Sec-Fetch-Site` over HTTPS, or its `Origin` over plain HTTP. The preview then wakes, with what it depends on, and a `WokenByRequest` event says a person asked. Next comes the waking-up page. It says the preview is starting, and for how long, and reloads every 3 seconds. Once a pod is Ready, the next reload is the page they opened, `/orders/7`. | When | What happens | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Others open it after the click | The waking-up page, then the app, without clicking | | A page left open whose reloads failed, as after a laptop slept | Its token expired. The page says so, and offers the button again | | A browser from before about 2020 | It may not say where the click came from, so nothing wakes. The page says so, and how else to wake it | | The app’s own form, such as its login, posted after the preview paused | The asleep page. Nothing wakes until someone clicks | | Once it’s awake | It pauses again after `idleAfter` with no activity, an hour by default | The other ways to wake it are `kubectl napwake wake`, a [wake request](#waking-it-from-tooling), or a current browser. [The Wake it button](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#the-wake-it-button) has the token, and what a click from another site gets. ## What robots and API clients see Anything but a person’s browser opening a page, or posting a small form, gets a `503`. That’s `curl`, scripts and API clients, and a page’s own `fetch`. Crawlers and link previewers such as `Googlebot`, headless browsers and scanners get it too. It says the preview is asleep and how to wake it, and nothing wakes: 
```bash
curl -i https://pr-42.preview.example.com/
```
 
```text
HTTP/1.1 503 Service Unavailable
Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8
Cache-Control: no-store
Retry-After: 300
X-Robots-Tag: noindex


This environment is asleep. It was paused because it looked unused, to save money.
Requests don't wake it. To wake it, do one of these, then try again:
- Open it in a browser and press Wake it.
- Run kubectl napwake wake NAME -n NAMESPACE.
- From tooling, set the annotation napwake.dev/wake-requested to a new value on its ManagedWorkload.
More at https://docs.napwake.dev/guides/preview-environments/
```
 | The caller | What it gets | | -------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | | A client that sends `Accept: application/json` | The same, in JSON | | A probe or health check the doorman knows | Answered as for any workload | | A connection that isn’t HTTP, such as a database’s | Closed | | Anyone, once someone has woken the preview | Served as usual | See [What browsers, robots and API clients see](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-browsers-robots-and-api-clients-see). ## Waking it from tooling Tooling sets `napwake.dev/wake-requested` on the preview’s ManagedWorkload to a new value, whatever its wake setting. Napwake acts on each value once, records it in `status.lastWakeRequest`, and answers in the `WakeRequest` condition ([Wake now](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now)). A ManagedWorkload from the label is named after its workload, such as `web`. Waking it wakes what it depends on too. ### When a pull request is pushed A deploy that leaves the replicas to Napwake, as each tab above sets up, doesn’t wake a paused preview. Its new pods start at the next wake. One that sets `replicas` scales the preview up, which wakes it as a scale-up from outside. Under Flux, that’s a `GitOpsConflict`, and the next pause waits an hour ([GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/)). To have it up for whoever reviews each push, wake it from the workflow that deploys it, and wait until it’s Running. In GitHub Actions: .github/workflows/preview\.yaml 
```yaml
name: Preview
on:
  pull_request:


jobs:
  preview:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      # ...the steps that deploy the preview and give kubectl access to the cluster
      - name: Wake the preview
        env:
          NS: preview-${{ github.event.pull_request.number }}
          TOKEN: ${{ github.run_id }}-${{ github.run_attempt }}
        run: |
          kubectl wait managedworkload/web -n "$NS" --for=create --timeout=1m
          kubectl annotate managedworkload web -n "$NS" --overwrite napwake.dev/wake-requested="$TOKEN"
          kubectl wait managedworkload web -n "$NS" --for=jsonpath='{.status.lastWakeRequest}'="$TOKEN" --timeout=10m
          answer=$(kubectl get managedworkload web -n "$NS" \
            -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="WakeRequest")].status} {.status.conditions[?(@.type=="WakeRequest")].message}')
          case "$answer" in
            True*) ;;
            *) echo "::error::${answer#* }"; exit 1 ;;
          esac
          kubectl wait managedworkload web -n "$NS" --for=jsonpath='{.status.phase}'=Running --timeout=10m
```
 | Part | Why | | ---------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The first `kubectl wait` | On the first push, Napwake makes the ManagedWorkload moments after the deploy. `--for=create` needs kubectl 1.31 or later | | A request set before Napwake takes it on | Answered `already awake`, as for any awake preview | | The token | The run’s ID and attempt, so each run asks afresh, a re-run included | | The second wait | Until Napwake takes the request up. One made as the preview pauses is answered at once, and calls the pause off. One made as it wakes is answered once the wake finishes ([Wake now](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now)). So this wait can last as long as the start, and has the same 10 minutes as the last | | When Napwake won’t wake it | The step fails with the reason, such as `not woken at 2026-10-23 21:01 UTC: it's scaled to zero outside Napwake, which leaves it off until its replicas are set above zero` | | The last wait | Until the preview is Running. Its dependencies wake with it. To wait for them too, add a `kubectl wait` for each | ### From a terminal 
```bash
kubectl napwake wake web -n preview-42
```
 
```text
waking preview-42/web...
preview-42/web is Running after 2s
```
 `--wait`, the default, waits for Napwake’s answer, then until it’s Running. Both fit in `--timeout`, 5 minutes by default. A request made as the preview pauses calls the pause off. One made as it wakes is answered once the wake finishes. That’s inside the same 5 minutes. For a slower start, give it more, such as `--timeout 10m` ([Wake a workload](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#wake-a-workload)). ### From a portal or a chat bot Anything that talks to the Kubernetes API can ask. A portal that has just made the environment gets `404 Not Found` at first. Napwake makes its ManagedWorkload a moment after the workload, so the portal retries until it exists. With `curl`, a `PATCH` sets the annotation, and prints nothing unless it fails. `$APISERVER` is the API server’s address, `ca.crt` its CA certificate, and `$KUBE_TOKEN` a token for the bot’s ServiceAccount: 
```bash
NS=preview-42
REQUEST=chat-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ)
curl --fail-with-body -sS -o /dev/null --cacert ca.crt -X PATCH \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $KUBE_TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/merge-patch+json' \
  -d '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"napwake.dev/wake-requested":"'"$REQUEST"'"}}}' \
  "$APISERVER/apis/napwake.dev/v1alpha1/namespaces/$NS/managedworkloads/web"
```
 Then read the ManagedWorkload every second or two until Napwake has answered. A request made as the preview pauses is answered at once, and calls the pause off. One made as it wakes is answered once the wake finishes. So keep reading as long as it takes to start: 
```bash
curl --fail-with-body -sS --cacert ca.crt -H "Authorization: Bearer $KUBE_TOKEN" \
  "$APISERVER/apis/napwake.dev/v1alpha1/namespaces/$NS/managedworkloads/web" |
  jq -r --arg request "$REQUEST" 'select(.status.lastWakeRequest == $request)
    | .status.conditions[] | select(.type == "WakeRequest") | "\(.status) \(.message)"'
```
 
```text
True woken as requested at 2026-10-23 21:03 UTC
```
 It prints nothing until then. `False` gives the reason in the message. Once it’s `True`, `.status.phase` says when the preview is `Running`. ### The access tooling needs A Role in the preview’s namespace, bound to your tooling’s ServiceAccount, such as one in a `ci` namespace: preview-waker.yaml 
```yaml
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  name: preview-waker
  namespace: preview-42
rules:
  - apiGroups: [napwake.dev]
    resources: [managedworkloads]
    verbs: [get, list, watch, patch]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata:
  name: preview-waker
  namespace: preview-42
roleRef:
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
  kind: Role
  name: preview-waker
subjects:
  - kind: ServiceAccount
    name: preview-waker
    namespace: ci
```
 | Tool | Verbs on `managedworkloads` | | ---------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `kubectl annotate` | `get`, `patch` | | `kubectl wait` | `get`, `list`, `watch`. It lists the ManagedWorkload by name before watching it. Without `list`, it waits out its timeout, even once the condition is met | | `kubectl napwake wake` | `get`, `list`, `patch` | | An HTTP client, such as a portal’s | `patch` to ask, and `get` or `watch` to read the answer | People with the built-in `edit` or `admin` role in the namespace have all of these once Napwake is installed. ### What else wakes it | Also wakes it | What to know | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | An [activity annotation](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations), from CI or a script | Wakes it, as with `request` | | A [dependent](/next/concepts/dependencies/) waking | Wakes it, since a wake takes its dependencies with it | | Something else scaling it up | It’s up, with a `ScaledUp` event naming what did it | | A [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) | Wakes it when it sees activity | A query that counts ingress requests counts robots’ requests too, so it would let them wake it. For a public environment, count something else, or filter bots out. ## Up before people come With `ask`, only people wake a paused preview. So behind the doorman, the forecast learns from every hour it’s paused. An hour is busy if someone woke it, with a click, a wake request or an activity annotation. Otherwise it’s quiet. If someone had needed it, they’d have woken it. Some hours are learned only if someone woke it. Those are hours the doorman isn’t routing it, or its activity queries can’t be evaluated. So an outage can’t teach the forecast quiet hours ([What an hour records](/next/concepts/forecasting/#what-an-hour-records)). Turn on `autoResume`. Once the forecast is confident, it wakes the preview 15 minutes before the hour people usually come. namespace.yaml 
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: preview-42
  labels:
    napwake.dev/managed: "true"
  annotations:
    napwake.dev/wake: "ask"
    napwake.dev/auto-resume: "true"
```
 | | What to expect | | ------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A weekday pattern | It acts after 8 days, and is fully active after 14 ([How long it takes](/next/concepts/forecasting/#how-long-it-takes)) | | From then | Someone who clicks at about 09:05 on weekdays finds it up from 08:45. Weekends stay asleep | | A new preview | It starts from nothing, with no history to [learn from](/next/concepts/forecasting/#learning-from-history). One for a pull request merged within a week is gone before the forecast acts | | Where it pays off | Previews that last, such as one per team or long-lived branch, or a demo environment | | Wakes from tooling | They count as people’s. A workflow that wakes it on every push teaches the hours your team pushes in | | Pausing early | Never, with `ask`. Someone who came while the forecast expected quiet would find it asleep ([Pausing early](/next/concepts/forecasting/#pausing-early)) | The forecast says what it learns from: 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload web -n preview-42 -o jsonpath='{.status.prediction.message}'
```
 
```text
learning: it has seen 0 of the 24 hours of the day, and can't act until it has seen them all; while it's paused, it learns from people waking it (a click on its asleep page, a wake request or an activity annotation)
```
 ## What it turned away The doorman counts the requests it turns away during each pause. That’s the `503`s and closed connections, not people shown the asleep page, nor probes and health checks. It’s a count only, with nothing of the requests themselves. It’s in the ManagedWorkload’s status, brought up to date about once a minute. It covers the current pause, or the last one once the preview is awake. 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload web -n preview-42 -o jsonpath='{.status.turnedAway}'
```
 
```text
{"pausedAt":"2026-10-23T20:41:44Z","requests":3}
```
 [`kubectl napwake status`](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#see-what-napwake-is-doing) lists the workloads that turned requests away: 
```text
Turned away while asleep, by a doorman that asks before waking: something automated may call these.
  preview-42/web   3 requests during the pause from 6m ago
```
 On a public URL, crawlers and scanners account for some. That’s what asking is for. More than you’d expect can mean something of yours calls the preview. A person is never turned away, so find out what it is. | It’s | Then | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | An uptime monitor or health checker | Add its `User-Agent` to the Helm value `doorman.healthCheckUserAgents`. It’s then answered as a health check, and not counted | | Something that needs the preview awake, such as a test suite or a webhook | Have it set a [wake request](#waking-it-from-tooling) first | `napwake_doorman_wakes_total{result="turned_away"}` counts them as they happen ([Metrics](/next/reference/metrics/#doorman), [What it turns away](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-it-turns-away)). ## What it can’t do | It can’t | Why | | ------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Stop a robot that clicks | One that loads the page and clicks wakes the preview as a person does. That’s at most once a minute, whichever doorman pod it reaches, and the preview then stays up for `idleAfter`. It’s rare, and costs a wake | | Keep a preview private | Asking keeps robots from waking it, not people from using it. Anyone with its URL can click and see it. For anything private, use a VPN, single sign-on at the ingress, or an allowlist, and keep `request` | Next * [Asking before waking →How the doorman tells a person from a robot.](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking) * [Wake now →Every answer to a wake request.](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now) * [Forecasting →How `autoResume` learns when people come.](/next/concepts/forecasting/)

# Prometheus activity guide

> Count requests, connections or queue messages as activity with a Prometheus query, so a workload stays awake while used and wakes when needed.

CPU tells Napwake whether a workload is working. A Prometheus query tells it whether anyone uses it. That’s requests through an ingress, open connections or messages on a queue. Any result above zero keeps the workload awake, and [wakes it](#while-the-workload-is-paused) once it’s paused. ## Point Napwake at Prometheus Set `prometheus.url` in your Helm values. It passes `--prometheus-url` to the operator: napwake-values.yaml 
```yaml
timezone: Europe/London
prometheus:
  url: http://prometheus.monitoring.svc.cluster.local:9090
```
 Upgrade with the file, at the chart version you run (`helm list -n napwake-system`), or Helm upgrades the chart too: 
```bash
helm upgrade napwake oci://ghcr.io/napwake/charts/napwake --version X.Y.Z \
  --namespace napwake-system -f napwake-values.yaml
```
 Installed without a values file, see [Keeping your settings](/next/operations/upgrading/#keeping-your-settings). | URL | Rule | | ----- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Is | The base of the Prometheus HTTP API, `http` or `https`. The operator refuses to start with any other | | Gets | `/api/v1/query` appended, with any path prefix kept, so `https://mimir.example.com/prometheus` works as-is | | Sends | No credentials or headers. Reach a Prometheus that needs them through a proxy that adds them | With a URL, each new workload’s forecast also starts from up to four weeks of CPU history. So `autoResume` can act from day one ([Learning from history](/next/concepts/forecasting/#learning-from-history)). Where Prometheus holds several clusters’ series, `prometheus.selector` (`--prometheus-selector`), such as `cluster="staging"`, narrows that history query to this cluster. Activity queries run as written, so narrow those in the query. ## Add a query managedworkload.yaml 
```yaml
spec:
  idlePolicy:
    idleAfter: 1h
    activity:
      prometheus:
        - promQL: 'sum(rate(nginx_ingress_controller_requests{exported_namespace="staging", exported_service="my-api"}[5m]))'
```
 Queries add to CPU, deploys and activity annotations, the other activity sources. Any one keeps the workload awake. There’s no annotation for queries. On a ManagedWorkload created from the `napwake.dev/managed` label, add them to its `spec.idlePolicy.activity.prometheus`. The opt-in controller keeps them when it updates the rest from the annotations. ## Test the query directly 
```bash
curl -s 'http://prometheus:9090/api/v1/query' \
  --data-urlencode 'query=sum(rate(nginx_ingress_controller_requests{exported_namespace="staging", exported_service="api"}[5m]))'
```
 Then check what kept the workload awake: 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging -o jsonpath='{.status.activity.lastActivitySource}'
```
 CheckpointAwake `prometheus` means a query was the most recent source of activity. ## How a result counts Every check, about once a minute, awake or paused, runs each query in turn as an instant query (`/api/v1/query`). It stops at the first that shows activity. Each has 2 seconds, and all of a workload’s 5 seconds together. | The result | Means | | ------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Any series above zero | Activity. The [activity clock](/next/concepts/idle-detection/) resets. With a series per pod, one busy pod is enough | | Zero, or empty | No activity from this source. The clock keeps running | | `NaN` or infinite | Ignored, as no evidence either way. `NaN` is what a ratio of two idle counters gives (0/0). A result with nothing else in it counts as empty, and the other sources decide | | A range vector (`metric[5m]` on its own) | `QueryFailed`. Return an instant vector or a scalar | | Over 4 MiB, such as a series per pod across the cluster | Refused too. Aggregate with `sum` or `max` | ## While the workload is paused 1. Asleep · paused Paused Its queries still run every minute, not every 5. 2. Activity shows in Prometheus 3. Waking up · resuming A query shows activity A `WokeByActivity` event says `a Prometheus activity query shows activity, waking`. Its dependencies wake with it. 4. Within a minute 5. Awake · running Awake `lastActivitySource: prometheus` That’s how a workload no request reaches wakes on its own. No Service routes requests to a queue worker, so query its queue’s length: managedworkload.yaml 
```yaml
spec:
  idlePolicy:
    idleAfter: 30m
    activity:
      prometheus:
        - promQL: 'sum(rabbitmq_queue_messages{queue="tasks"})'
```
 | While paused | What happens | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | What to query | Something that exists while it’s paused, such as the queue’s broker. The workload’s own metrics have no series while it has no pods | | A query already showing activity as a [pause you ask for](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now) begins | It doesn’t undo the pause, such as with messages still queued. It wakes the workload only after a check finds it quiet, then active again. An idle pause begins only once every query is quiet | | A query that can’t be evaluated | Never wakes it. `PrometheusAvailable` is `False`, as while awake. Its message says the queries can’t wake it until Prometheus answers, and whether a request through the doorman still does | | The [forecast](/next/concepts/forecasting/#what-an-hour-records) | Learns the hours the queries watch, quiet or busy. So a worker paused every night still learns whole days. With `autoResume`, it wakes ahead of its first messages once the forecast acts | ## When a query can’t be evaluated Napwake can’t see that activity. It doesn’t pause the workload, and a failing query doesn’t wake a paused one. The time isn’t counted as idle either ([When a source can’t be read](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#when-a-source-cant-be-read)). `PrometheusAvailable`, only on workloads with queries, says why: | Reason | Meaning | | ----------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `EndpointNotConfigured` | Queries are configured, but the operator has no `--prometheus-url` | | `QueryFailed` | Prometheus returned an error, a range vector or an oversized result. Or it didn’t answer a query within 2 seconds, or all of the workload’s within 5. The message has the details | 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="PrometheusAvailable")]}'
```
 ### A query that matches nothing A query that answers but matches no series looks like a quiet workload, as does a misspelt metric or label. Napwake can’t tell them apart, so the workload pauses on its other sources as usual. | `PrometheusQueryEmpty` | | | ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Set to `True` | Reason `NoSeries`, naming the queries, for a query that has matched nothing since its first evaluation. A `PrometheusQueryEmpty` warning event marks when it begins | | Cleared | Once the query matches a series, even one at zero. A counter created on the first request, as many client libraries’ labelled counters are, matches once it exists | | After an operator restart | Checked from each query’s first evaluation again, since matches are kept in memory | ### When Prometheus is down When Prometheus fails, every workload’s queries fail at once. When one query fails, only the workloads that use it do. | When | What happens | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Prometheus doesn’t answer, answers with a server error or `429`, or times out on three queries in a row | The operator doesn’t ask it again for a minute. Every workload’s queries fail at once, as `QueryFailed` with the error. None waits out a timeout and holds up queued wakes. The message stays the same for the whole outage, whether or not a check asked Prometheus | | It’s out for longer than a couple of checks | The clock restarts (`lastActivitySource: unobserved`) | | Prometheus rejects a query, such as one that doesn’t parse | It fails only for the workloads that use it | | A query times out while others answer | It fails only for the workloads that use it, so one tenant’s slow query doesn’t hold off everyone’s | ## Example queries | Use case | PromQL | | ------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Requests through ingress-nginx | `sum(rate(nginx_ingress_controller_requests{exported_namespace="staging", exported_service="api"}[5m]))` | | Requests seen by the app | `sum(rate(http_requests_total{service="api"}[5m]))` | | Open WebSocket connections | `sum(websocket_connections{service="api"})` | | Messages waiting on a queue | `sum(rabbitmq_queue_messages{queue="tasks"})` | | Kafka consumer lag | `sum(kafka_consumer_lag{group="worker"})` | ingress-nginx labels each request with its namespace and Service. Scraped by the Prometheus Operator, which keeps `namespace` and `service` for the scrape target, they become `exported_namespace` and `exported_service`. Give both. Preview environments often share Service names, and another’s traffic would keep this one awake. In [dry-run](/next/guides/dry-run/#what-dry-run-measured), nothing goes through the doorman, so a request-rate query is how it sees requests. Without one, a request counts only through the CPU it uses. ## Writing good queries * Measure activity, not idleness. Ask “how many requests per second”, not “are there no requests”. * `rate(...[5m])` stays above zero for about five minutes after the last request. That’s usually right. One request keeps the workload awake, and the clock starts once traffic stops. * Keep queries cheap. Aggregate, and use recording rules for anything heavy. * Leave out traffic that isn’t people. Health checks, uptime monitors and scrapers counted by the query keep a workload awake, and wake it once paused. Filter them by path or user agent where your metrics allow: 
```promql
sum(rate(nginx_ingress_controller_requests{exported_namespace="staging", exported_service="api", path!~"/healthz|/metrics"}[5m]))
```
 Next * [Idle detection →Every source of activity, and when a workload pauses.](/next/concepts/idle-detection/) * [Dry run →Measure a workload before it pauses.](/next/guides/dry-run/)

# Monitoring

> Scrape Napwake's metrics, load its alerting rules, and check the operator's and the doorman's health and logs.

The operator and the doorman serve Prometheus metrics, and Napwake ships alerting rules. Every metric is in the [Metrics reference](/next/reference/metrics/). ## Prometheus ### Scraping Metrics are served over HTTPS, only to a scraper whose token may `get` the `/metrics` URL. * Helm Turn on the ServiceMonitors, for the operator and the doorman, and bind your Prometheus’s ServiceAccount to the chart’s `<fullname>-metrics-reader` ClusterRole: napwake-values.yaml 
  ```yaml
  metrics:
    serviceMonitor:
      enabled: true
    prometheusRule:
      enabled: true
    readerSubjects:
      - kind: ServiceAccount
        name: prometheus-k8s
        namespace: monitoring
  ```
 * install.yaml or Kustomize Apply the ServiceMonitors and alerting rules into `napwake-system` from the `config/prometheus` base, pinned to your release. Or add it to your kustomization’s `resources`: 
  ```bash
  kubectl apply -k "https://github.com/napwake/napwake//config/prometheus?ref=v0.2.0-rc.9"
  ```
 Then bind your Prometheus’s ServiceAccount to the `napwake-metrics-reader` ClusterRole: 
  ```bash
  kubectl create clusterrolebinding napwake-metrics-reader \
    --clusterrole=napwake-metrics-reader --serviceaccount=monitoring:prometheus-k8s
  ```
 | You see | Fix | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Targets down with `401` or `403`, and `NapwakeDown` and `NapwakeDoormanDown` firing | Bind the ServiceAccount, as above. Or set `metrics.secure: false` for plain HTTP with no binding, which anyone who can reach the port can read | | Targets that never appear | Give the ServiceMonitors a label your Prometheus’s `serviceMonitorSelector` (and `serviceMonitorNamespaceSelector`) matches. The chart’s carry none a Prometheus selects by default | | Alerts naming the wrong namespace, or merging same-named workloads | In your own scrape config, set `honor_labels: true`, as the ServiceMonitors’ `honorLabels: true` does. Without it, the workload’s `namespace` label becomes the scraped pod’s (`napwake-system`) | | Prometheus refusing `bearerTokenFile`, under `arbitraryFSAccessThroughSMs.deny: true` | Scrape with `metrics.secure: false` behind a NetworkPolicy, or with a scrape config of your own | [kube-prometheus-stack](https://github.com/prometheus-community/helm-charts/tree/main/charts/kube-prometheus-stack) selects `release: <its release name>`, and its Prometheus runs as the `<its release name>-kube-prometheus-prometheus` ServiceAccount. For a stack installed as `kube-prometheus-stack` in `monitoring`: napwake-values.yaml 
```yaml
metrics:
  serviceMonitor:
    enabled: true
    additionalLabels:
      release: kube-prometheus-stack
  prometheusRule:
    enabled: true
    additionalLabels:
      release: kube-prometheus-stack
  readerSubjects:
    - kind: ServiceAccount
      name: kube-prometheus-stack-kube-prometheus-prometheus
      namespace: monitoring
```
 Check what yours selects (and its `ruleSelector`, for the PrometheusRule), and its ServiceAccount: 
```bash
kubectl get prometheus -A -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}: {.spec.serviceMonitorSelector}{"\n"}{end}'
kubectl get prometheus -A -o jsonpath='{.items[*].spec.serviceAccountName}'
```
 The ServiceMonitors authenticate with the Prometheus’s own ServiceAccount token, through `bearerTokenFile`. The Prometheus Operator deprecates that for `authorization`, which takes a token only from a Secret. You’d have to create and keep a long-lived one. ### Key metrics to watch **Cluster health dashboard:** 
```promql
# Is the operator acting on workloads?
rate(napwake_lifecycle_transitions_total[1h])


# Are there errors?
rate(napwake_reconcile_errors_total[5m]) > 0


# Are requests to paused workloads waking them?
sum by (result) (rate(napwake_doorman_wakes_total[1h]))
```
 **Per-workload health:** 
```promql
# Is the prediction engine learning?
napwake_prediction_confidence_percent{season="daily"}


# Are workloads cycling too fast?
rate(napwake_lifecycle_transitions_total[1h])


# Is a workload paused while it's in use?
increase(napwake_wrong_pauses_total[1h]) > 0
```
 A workload paused while in use gets a `PausedWhileInUse` warning, with what to change ([Paused while in use](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#paused-while-in-use)). ## Alerting rules The chart creates these with `metrics.prometheusRule.enabled: true`. For `install.yaml` or the Kustomize manifests, [`config/prometheus`](#scraping) creates the same rules. | Alert | Fires when | Severity | | ---------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------- | | `NapwakeReconcileErrorsHigh` | Reconcile errors exceed 0.1/sec for 10 minutes | critical | | `NapwakeDown` | No healthy operator target has been scraped for 5 minutes, so nothing is paused or woken | critical | | `NapwakeDoormanDown` | No healthy doorman target has been scraped for 5 minutes, so requests to paused workloads fail instead of waking them. Only with the doorman enabled | critical | | `NapwakeDoormanCantListen` | For 5 minutes, a doorman pod can’t listen on a routed port. Or two routes claim one port, which every pod refuses. Requests to the paused workloads behind those ports fail | critical | | `NapwakeWorkloadStuck` | A workload stays in `Pausing` or `Resuming` for 15 minutes | warning | | `NapwakeTargetUnavailable` | A ManagedWorkload’s target goes missing more than 3 times in an hour | warning | | `NapwakeDoormanWakesFailing` | More than 3 requests to one paused workload timed out or failed (`result` `timeout` or `error`) in 15 minutes, for 5 minutes | warning | | `NapwakeWakesSlow` | For 5 minutes, one in ten requests the doorman held in the last 30 minutes waited over a minute. A minute is half the default `wake.maxWait`. Requests passed straight to woken pods don’t count, and it takes 3 waits at least | warning | | `NapwakeDoormanNearCapacity` | For 5 minutes, a doorman pod carries over 80% of the 2048 held or 1024 proxied connections it takes. Past them it refuses or delays requests ([Limits](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#limits)) | warning | | `NapwakePausesUndone` | Something outside Napwake, such as a GitOps tool, scaled a paused workload back up at least 3 times in 6 hours | warning | | `NapwakeActivityUnreadable` | For 30 minutes, Napwake can’t read a workload’s CPU while it’s awake, or its Prometheus activity queries. Until it can, it doesn’t pause the workload | warning | | `NapwakeResumeIncomplete` | A woken workload has run with replicas not Ready for 15 minutes (`ResumeIncomplete`) | warning | | `NapwakePausedWhileInUse` | At least 3 times in a day, Napwake paused a workload while it was in use. Each time, something soon woke it | warning | `NapwakeDown` and `NapwakeDoormanDown` match the ServiceMonitors’ scrape job. Without it they’d fire forever, so the chart creates them only with `metrics.serviceMonitor.enabled`. Scraping Napwake another way, write them on your own job’s `up` series. ## Health checks Both serve health endpoints on port 8081, which the chart and the Kustomize manifests use as liveness and readiness probes. Forward the port to check one, here the operator’s from a Helm install named `napwake`: 
```bash
kubectl port-forward -n napwake-system deployment/napwake 8081:8081


# Liveness
curl http://localhost:8081/healthz


# Readiness
curl http://localhost:8081/readyz
```
 ## Operator logs Logs are JSON by default. These label selectors work for every install: 
```bash
kubectl logs -n napwake-system -l control-plane=controller-manager -f
kubectl logs -n napwake-system -l control-plane=doorman -f
```
 The Deployments are `napwake` and `napwake-doorman` for a Helm release named `napwake`, or `napwake-controller-manager` and `napwake-doorman` from `install.yaml` or Kustomize. | Log entry | Means | | --------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | | `"phase transition"` | A lifecycle state change, with `from`, `to` and `reason` | | `"paused workload scaled up outside Napwake"` | Someone scaled up a paused workload | | `"managing workload from its label"` | The opt-in controller created a ManagedWorkload | | `"routing requests through the doorman"` | An error routing a paused workload’s Services to the doorman | | `"ignoring malformed activity annotation"` | An activity annotation that isn’t an RFC 3339 time | Forecast regime changes, idle detection and wakes are ManagedWorkload [events](/next/reference/conditions-and-events/#events), not log entries. Next * [Metrics reference →Every metric, and what it measures.](/next/reference/metrics/) * [Troubleshooting →Common problems, and how to fix them.](/next/operations/troubleshooting/)

# Troubleshooting

> What you see when Napwake doesn't do what you expect, why, and the command that fixes it.

Opting in and pausing * [Labelled, but no ManagedWorkload](#a-labelled-workload-has-no-managedworkload) * [`InvalidSetting`](#an-invalidsetting-warning) * [Idle, but not paused](#workload-is-idle-but-not-being-paused) * [`pause` didn't pause it](#kubectl-napwake-pause-didnt-pause-it) * [Paused, pods still running](#workload-paused-with-pods-still-running) Waking * [Requests fail, it doesn't wake](#requests-to-a-paused-workload-fail-instead-of-waking-it) * [Wake it doesn't wake it](#wake-it-doesnt-wake-the-workload) * [Stuck in Resuming](#workload-stuck-in-resuming) * [Running, replicas not Ready](#workload-resumed-with-replicas-not-ready) * [Keeps waking up](#workload-keeps-cycling-between-paused-and-running) * [Paused while in use](#workload-paused-while-in-use) * [Need it awake now](#you-need-a-paused-workload-awake-now) Dependencies, activity, forecast * [A dependency is left out](#a-dependency-isnt-held-awake-woken-or-waited-for) * [A query never matches](#a-prometheus-query-never-matches) * [An annotation is ignored](#an-activity-annotation-is-ignored) * [Confidence stays at 0](#prediction-confidence-stays-at-0) Cost * [$0.00](#cost-data-shows-000) * [Freed but not saved](#freed-but-not-saved) * [Saved reads `unknown`](#saved-reads-unknown) ManagedWorkloads * [Target not found](#target-not-found) * [Two for one workload](#two-managedworkloads-target-one-workload) * [`ManagedByAnotherInstall`](#a-managedbyanotherinstall-warning) * [Lost its status](#a-paused-workloads-managedworkload-lost-its-status) * [Let go without handing back](#a-managedworkload-let-go-of-its-workload-without-handing-it-back) Installing and removing * [`helm uninstall` refused](#helm-uninstall-fails-with-job-napwake-uninstall-guard-failed) * [`helm upgrade` refused](#helm-upgrade-fails-with-job-napwake-upgrade-guard-failed) * [At zero, operator gone](#workloads-stay-at-zero-after-the-operator-was-removed) * [None of these](#debug-checklist) ## Opting in and pausing ### A labelled workload has no ManagedWorkload `kubectl get managedworkloads -n <namespace>` doesn’t list it. The workload’s own events say why: 
```bash
kubectl describe deployment <name> -n <namespace>
```
 | Event | Cause and fix | | ----------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `LabelIgnored`, on the pod template | On the pods (a chart’s `podLabels`), where Napwake doesn’t look. Put it on the workload or namespace ([Opting in](/next/guides/opt-in/)) | | `LabelIgnored`, not `"true"` | Only `"true"` opts in. Settings such as `dry-run` are annotations | | `LabelIgnored`, Napwake’s own | The operator’s and doorman’s Deployments are never managed | | `Protected` | A [protected](/next/guides/opt-in/#protected-namespaces) namespace, such as `kube-system` | | No event | `napwake.dev/ignore: "true"`, or a ManagedWorkload someone wrote, which wins. Under Kustomize, `commonLabels` fails on an existing workload ([With Kustomize](/next/guides/opt-in/#with-kustomize)) | ### An `InvalidSetting` warning A settings annotation on the workload or its namespace has a value Napwake can’t read. The warning event is on the workload, and names the annotation, where it is, and what Napwake did instead: 
```plaintext
napwake.dev/wake="Ask" on the workload isn't "request" or "ask", so the doorman asks before waking it, to be safe
```
 | The annotation | What a value that can’t be read does | | --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Most settings | It’s skipped. The namespace’s value decides, then the Helm default if there is one, then the built-in one | | `napwake.dev/dry-run` | Turns dry-run on | | `napwake.dev/wake` | The doorman [asks before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking), whatever the namespace says | Fix the value. [Settings annotations](/next/reference/labels-annotations/#settings-annotations) lists what each accepts, such as exactly `"request"` or `"ask"`, in lower case, for `wake`. ### Workload is idle but not being paused 1. **Dry-run?** 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging -o jsonpath='{.spec.dryRun}'
   ```
 `true` means it evaluates but doesn’t act. `kubectl napwake enable my-api -n staging` ends it, however it was set up. On a labelled workload, `spec.dryRun` follows `napwake.dev/dry-run` (workload, namespace, then `defaults.dryRun`), and an unreadable value, such as `"False"`, means dry-run ([by hand](/next/guides/dry-run/)). 2. **Last active?** 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging -o jsonpath='{.status.activity}'
   ```
 `pauseAt` is when it pauses. If it keeps moving, `lastActivitySource` says what still counts as activity: | Source | Cause and fix | | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `cpu` | Over `activity.cpuThreshold`% of requests, as polling or cron loops can be. Raise it if expected. It takes a decimal, such as `"0.5"` | | `annotation` | `napwake.dev/last-activity` refreshed, or `napwake.dev/active-until` ahead, on it or its target | | `rollout` | A recent pod template change | | `prometheus` | An `activity.prometheus` query above zero | | `unobserved` | A gap in watching, which isn’t activity. An `Unobserved` event says how long it was | | `unobserved`, its cause | The operator was down or restarted, or CPU or a query was unreadable for over a couple of checks | | `unobserved`, the clock | The gap outlasted [the one allowed](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#restarts-and-outages), so the clock restarted as watching resumed | | `unobserved`, repeating | Check `MetricsAvailable` and `PrometheusAvailable` | 3. **CPU readable?** Without CPU data a busy workload looks idle, so Napwake doesn’t act. 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging \
     -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="MetricsAvailable")]}'
   ```
 | `False` reason | Cause and fix | | -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `NoPodMetrics` | No metrics from its Ready pods for over 3 minutes. Check metrics-server is installed, with `kubectl top pods -n staging` | | `NoCPURequests` | No CPU requests. Set them, or a LimitRange default, then restart its pods, as a default applies only to new ones | | `MetricsUnavailable` | The Metrics API failed, error in the message. No `metrics.k8s.io` API means no metrics-server | Zero replicas, and `Unknown` just after a wake or rollout, [aren’t errors](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#when-a-source-cant-be-read). 4. **The forecast?** 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging \
     -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="IdleVetoed")]}'
   ```
 A confident forecast expecting demand in the hour under way or the next defers the pause. The `IdleVetoed=True` message says when the forecast expects demand, and an `IdleVetoed` event marks the start. 5. **Anything else?** `kubectl napwake status` gives the reason under NEXT. Or: | You see | Cause and fix | | --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | No `idlePolicy` | Nothing pauses it but [`kubectl napwake pause`](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now) | | `PrometheusAvailable=False` | A query fails, or no `--prometheus-url` | | `Protected=True` | `ProtectedNamespace`, `NapwakeComponent` (Napwake’s own), or `PVCsDeletedOnScaleDown`, for a StatefulSet whose `persistentVolumeClaimRetentionPolicy.whenScaled: Delete` would lose its PVCs. Set `whenScaled: Retain` | | `GitOpsConflict=True` | A GitOps tool undid the last pause. Napwake retries after an hour | 6. **Depended on?** 
   ```bash
   kubectl get managedworkload postgres -n staging \
     -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="HeldByDependents")]}'
   ```
 `HeldByDependents=True` names its awake dependents, declared in `dependsOn` or [learned](/next/concepts/dependencies/#learned-dependencies). It pauses after them. `kubectl napwake deps postgres -n staging` shows each link’s source. `DependencyCycle=True` means two depend on each other, so break the [cycle](/next/concepts/dependencies/). ### `kubectl napwake pause` didn’t pause it 
```bash
kubectl napwake pause my-api -n staging
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="PauseRequest")]}'
```
 `pause` waits for Paused, or prints why not. `PauseRequest` keeps that answer until the next request, whatever happens since. It’s `True` (`Paused`, `AlreadyPaused`), as in `paused as requested at 2026-10-21 03:32 UTC`, or `False`, starting `not paused at`, with a reason. Each request is answered once, so fix the reason and run `pause` again ([Pause now](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now)). | Reason | Cause | | ---------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `ForecastExpectsDemand` | Demand expected within the hour. `kubectl napwake pause --yes` pauses anyway | | `HeldByDependents`, `DependencyCycle` | Its dependents are awake, or it’s in a `dependsOn` cycle | | `ActiveUntil` | `napwake.dev/active-until` holds it | | `DryRun`, `Protected`, `ScaledToZero` | Dry-run, protected, or at zero already | | `TargetNotFound`, `TargetIgnored`, `DuplicateTarget` | Its target is missing, ignored, or another ManagedWorkload’s | | `PredatesStatus` | The request was already on the ManagedWorkload as Napwake took it on, as after a backup restored without status. Set a new value | ### Workload paused with pods still running `PauseIncomplete=True`, with a `PauseIncomplete` warning event instead of `Paused`, means pods outlived their termination grace period by a minute (one after another for a StatefulSet). Normally it’s `Paused` once pods stop. Here Napwake stopped waiting, so it’s `Paused` rather than stuck `Pausing`, and wakes as usual. It turns `False` once they’ve gone, or on a wake. A StatefulSet with the default `OrderedReady` stops pods one at a time, and not while one isn’t Ready. A Deployment’s can hang terminating on a finalizer or an unreachable node. `kubectl describe` the pods. ## Waking ### Requests to a paused workload fail instead of waking it 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions[?(@.type=="WakeOnRequest")]}'
```
 | `WakeOnRequest` | Cause and fix | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | None, or `NotPaused` | Not paused, so not routed yet | | Message starts `at its last pause:` | Awake now, and the next pause meets the problem named. Fix it now | | `NoServices`, `ServedByOtherPods`, `NodeLocalTraffic`, `UnsupportedIPFamily` | No Service it [can route](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#when-a-workload-isnt-routed). A queue worker can wake on a [Prometheus query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) | | `UnsupportedLoadBalancer`, or a Service named as not routed | In [GKE NEGs](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#on-gke). Use the [forwarder](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs) | | `ForwarderNotReady` | No forwarder pod Ready, normal for seconds after a pause. If it lasts, `kubectl describe pods -n staging -l app.kubernetes.io/name=napwake-forwarder` for an unpullable image (the operator’s), a quota or LimitRange (`doorman.negForwarder.resources`), or a [NetworkPolicy](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#network-policies-behind-negs) | | `DoormanUnavailable` | No doorman pod Ready. Check `kubectl get pods -A -l control-plane=doorman` | | `NoFreePort`, `TooManyPorts` | All doorman ports (20000-29999) taken, or over `doorman.portsPerWorkload` (32 by default) or `doorman.portsPerNamespace` (256), as the message says. Raise a cap, or list ports in `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports` | | `RoutingFailed` | EndpointSlice writes failed, error in the message, retried with backoff. Check the operator’s RBAC and logs | | `DoormanDisabled`, `TargetNotFound` | `doorman.enabled: false`, or the workload is gone | If it’s `DoormanRouted` and requests still fail ([What the caller sees](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-the-caller-sees)): | The caller sees | Cause and fix | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A close after a while, no response | Not Ready by `wake.maxWait`, or pods unreachable, as a `RequestNotServed` warning says. Raise `maxWait`, or see why pods are slow. Counted by `napwake_doorman_wakes_total{result=~"timeout\|error"}` | | A close at once, nothing sent | A [limit](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#limits), counted by `napwake_doorman_wakes_total{result="limited"}` | | The app’s own error, such as a `503`, just after a wake | Pods Ready before the app was, as with no readiness probe. A `NoReadinessProbe` warning, and the `WakeOnRequest` message, say so. Add a [readiness probe](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#readiness-probes) that passes once it can serve | | A `503` with no body | A health check or scraper `User-Agent`, such as `kube-probe/` or `Prometheus/`, which never wakes it | | A `503` saying it’s asleep and how to wake it | The workload [asks before waking](#wake-it-doesnt-wake-the-workload). Only a person’s click on **Wake it**, or tooling, wakes it. For a caller that isn’t a browser, such as another service or a webhook, set `napwake.dev/wake: "request"`. `status.turnedAway` counts these requests | | A 502 from ingress-nginx, or a refused request, as the workload paused | A pod exited the moment it got `SIGTERM`, before kube-proxy and nginx dropped it. The doorman stands in front of the Service before the pods stop, so it’s the pod’s shutdown. Give the app a `preStop` sleep of a few seconds ([Work in progress at a pause](/next/guides/pause/#work-in-progress-at-a-pause)) | | A 502 from a GKE Ingress or Gateway while paused | In NEGs without the forwarder, so there’s no healthy backend. Just after a pause, GKE takes up to a minute to see health checks pass | | A 504 from an Ingress at 60 seconds | Raise its upstream timeout, for ingress-nginx `nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-read-timeout` | | A refusal or timeout at once, every time | A NetworkPolicy blocking the doorman (ports 20000-29999), or it to the pods ([Network policies](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#network-policies)) | The doorman’s logs name the workload and Service of each held connection (in your install’s namespace): 
```bash
kubectl logs -n napwake-system -l control-plane=doorman
```
 ### Wake it doesn’t wake the workload With `ask`, a click on **Wake it** wakes the workload, and the waking-up page follows. So does tooling, with `kubectl napwake wake` or `napwake.dev/wake-requested`. Anything else gets a `503` and wakes nothing, by design. Check the setting first: 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkload my-api -n staging -o jsonpath='{.spec.wake.mode}'
```
 When the asleep page comes back after a click, its notice says why: | The notice | Cause and fix | | ------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | “Nothing woke”, a `403` | Its token expired because its reloads failed, as after a laptop slept. The page reloads with a fresh one every minute. Or it was shown during an earlier pause, or the browser said the click came from no page (`Sec-Fetch-Site: none`). Click the button on the page shown now | | The same, every time | The doorman’s replicas may sign with different keys. Their logs say `loading the key the Wake it button is signed with` when they can’t read the Secret `napwake-doorman-wake-key`. Check it exists, and the doorman’s Role still allows reading it. With the Kustomize manifests, or your own, include the Secret and its Role | | “The click came from another address”, a `403` | The browser says the click came from a page at another address. That’s another site or subdomain, by its `Sec-Fetch-Site`. Or, over plain HTTP, its `Origin` isn’t the address the doorman saw the page at. That’s the one at the top of the page. A proxy may rewrite the `Host` header, as ingress-nginx’s `upstream-vhost` does. Pass the browser’s `Host` on, or serve the preview over HTTPS. Clicking again won’t help, so wake it with `kubectl napwake wake` or `napwake.dev/wake-requested` meanwhile | | “Try a current browser” | The click came with neither `Sec-Fetch-Site` nor an `Origin`, or with `Origin: null`, as some browsers from before about 2020 send. So the doorman can’t tell it from a spider posting the page’s token. Open it in a current browser, or wake it with `kubectl napwake wake` or `napwake.dev/wake-requested` | | The same, from a current browser | Something between it and the doorman drops those headers, or a browser extension does. ingress-nginx and most proxies pass them on as they are ([How the doorman tells](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-browsers-robots-and-api-clients-see)) | | “Try again in a minute”, a `429` with `Retry-After: 60` | A click wakes a workload at most once a minute. Try again shortly | | “It didn’t wake” | The doorman couldn’t stamp the wake, as when the API server didn’t answer, or a wake rate limit was hit. Its logs say which | | They see | Cause and fix | | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A plain-text `503` in a browser | To the doorman it isn’t a person’s browser. Its `User-Agent` names a crawler or a headless browser, or doesn’t start with `Mozilla/`. Wake it with `kubectl napwake wake` or `napwake.dev/wake-requested` | | A `503` saying how to wake it, from an API client, `curl` or a script | Only a browser gets the button. Run `kubectl napwake wake`, or have the tool set [`napwake.dev/wake-requested`](/next/guides/preview-environments/#waking-it-from-tooling) | | No asleep page, and the request fails | It isn’t routed ([Requests to a paused workload fail](#requests-to-a-paused-workload-fail-instead-of-waking-it)) | | `Ask`, though nobody set it | Its namespace or `defaults.wake` says `ask`, or its value can’t be read ([`InvalidSetting`](#an-invalidsetting-warning)) | `status.turnedAway` counts what the doorman turned away during the pause, and `kubectl napwake status` lists the workloads that turned requests away ([What it turns away](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-it-turns-away)). ### Workload stuck in Resuming `WaitingForDependencies=True` means a `waitForReady` dependency isn’t Ready. The condition names it and its ready replicas, so check its pods. Otherwise the workload’s own pods aren’t getting Ready. Napwake waits for every restored replica, but no longer than 2 minutes after the first is Ready, or, while none is, the Deployment’s `progressDeadlineSeconds` (10 minutes by default, and for a StatefulSet). It’s then `Running` on its Ready replicas, possibly none, with `ResumeIncomplete=True` ([Resuming → Running](/next/concepts/lifecycle/#automatic)). A dependency that never gets Ready is [waited for only so long](/next/concepts/dependencies/#waiting-for-a-dependency-to-be-ready), then skipped with `DependencyWontStart`. The `NapwakeWorkloadStuck` alert fires at 15 minutes, and `kubectl napwake status` lists it under `Stuck` at 10. ### Workload resumed with replicas not Ready `ResumeIncomplete=True`, with a warning event of that name, means the resume stopped waiting with replicas not Ready. It’s `Running` on those that are, its KEDA ScaledObject scales it again, and requests go to its Ready pods. With none Ready, the doorman stops holding requests, so they fail until one is. It’s common when it wasn’t wholly Ready before the pause, with a pod crash-looping or unschedulable. `kubectl describe` its pods. Once every replica is Ready, later or at the next resume, it turns `False` with an `AllReplicasReady` event. ### Workload keeps cycling between paused and running The events say what woke it: | Event | Cause and fix | | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `WokeByActivity` | An activity annotation changed, or a held request (`WokenByRequest` names the Service). A monitor requesting on a timer wakes it each time. Point it elsewhere, put its port in the Service’s `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports`, or have the workload [ask before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking) (`napwake.dev/wake: "ask"`). So does a tool refreshing `napwake.dev/last-activity` on a timer, not on real use | | `AutoResume` | The forecast expected demand | | `ScaledUp` | Scaled up outside Napwake, by what the event and `status.lastScaledUp` name. A GitOps tool also sets `GitOpsConflict=True`. It sets the replicas from Git, undoing every pause until told to leave them ([GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/)) | The doorman already ignores connections that close without sending anything, and common probes and scrapers ([What wakes a workload](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-wakes-a-workload-and-what-doesnt)). A `PausedWhileInUse` warning means its use undid a pause soon after it ([Workload paused while in use](#workload-paused-while-in-use)). ### Workload paused while in use A `PausedWhileInUse` warning event, and `kubectl napwake status` under Needs attention, say Napwake paused it while it was in use. A request, a click, a wake request or an activity annotation undid the pause soon after it began. Napwake didn’t see the use, usually because the workload uses less CPU in use than its threshold. 
```bash
kubectl describe managedworkload my-api -n staging
```
 
```text
Warning  PausedWhileInUse  a request came 40 seconds after it paused, so it was in use: it now waits 10 minutes
without activity before pausing, not 5 minutes, until a pause holds. It used up to 0.6% of its CPU requests while
in use, and up to 0.2% idle: try napwake.dev/cpu-threshold: "0.4"
```
 Meanwhile its idle clock waits longer, doubling with each wrong pause up to `idleAfter` plus an hour. So it’s paused in use less often. Fix the cause, and it pauses on time again: | You see | Fix | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A suggested threshold | Set it, on the workload or the ManagedWorkload’s `idlePolicy.activity.cpuThreshold`. Napwake suggests one only when the workload’s CPU in use sits clearly above its idle CPU | | No threshold suggested | CPU can’t tell its use from its idle. Count its requests with a [Prometheus activity query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#example-queries), such as its ingress’s request rate | | A caller that keeps a connection open, such as a gRPC client or a connection pool from another workload | Add that workload with `dependsOn`, or opt it in, so it holds this one awake | The warning stops once a pause lasts 15 minutes, or `idleAfter` if shorter, without being undone. See [Paused while in use](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#paused-while-in-use). ### You need a paused workload awake now 
```bash
kubectl napwake wake my-api -n staging
```
 It wakes the workload whatever its wake setting, and waits for Running, or says why Napwake won’t wake it. The `WakeRequest` condition keeps that answer until the next request. `False`, with a message starting `not woken at` and the time, gives the reason ([Wake now](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now), [kubectl plugin](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#wake-a-workload)). ## Dependencies, activity and the forecast ### A dependency isn’t held awake, woken or waited for 
```bash
kubectl napwake deps my-api -n preview-42
```
 That lists its dependencies and their sources. The ManagedWorkload’s conditions say why one is left out: | You see | Cause and fix | | -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `DependencyNotAllowed=True` | Its namespace’s `napwake.dev/dependents-from` doesn’t list this one. Annotate it ([Across namespaces](/next/concepts/dependencies/#across-namespaces)) | | `DependencyNotFound=True` | A `dependsOn` workload is missing (`DependencyNotFound`), or outside `watchNamespaces` or unreadable (`DependencyNotVisible`). Neither blocks the workload | | A `DependencyWakeFailed` warning event | Its wake annotation was refused, such as by a policy engine, error in the message. The workload wakes anyway, and the dependency may stay paused | ### A Prometheus query never matches `PrometheusQueryEmpty=True`, reason `NoSeries`, with a warning event, means an activity query has matched no series since Napwake first ran it. It’s usually a misspelt metric or label. It can’t keep the workload awake or hold off a pause. [Test it in Prometheus](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#test-the-query-directly). ### An activity annotation is ignored An `InvalidActivityAnnotation` warning event means a `napwake.dev/last-activity`, `last-request` or `active-until` value isn’t RFC 3339, such as epoch seconds or a bare date. It neither keeps the workload awake nor wakes it. Write it as `date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ` does ([Activity annotations](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations)). ### Prediction confidence stays at 0 Daily confidence needs a full 24-hour window of observed hours, and weekly 168. An hour isn’t observed while the operator is down, CPU is unreadable, or the workload is paused with neither the doorman nor a [Prometheus query](/next/guides/prometheus-signals/#while-the-workload-is-paused) to see demand. With [`ask`](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking), a query that can’t be evaluated leaves it unobserved too, even behind the doorman. Paused that way nightly, it never gets a full day, and `status.prediction.message` says why requests don’t wake it, and the fix. `status.prediction.reason` and `message` say what the forecast awaits, and `startsActing` when it should act ([When it acts](/next/concepts/forecasting/#when-it-acts)). With a Prometheus URL, a new workload starts from its history, reported in `status.prediction.history`. Also check metrics-server returns data: 
```bash
kubectl top pods -n staging
```
 ## Cost ### Cost data shows $0.00 | Field | Why | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | All | Priced on what its pods request, so no requests means no cost | | `projectedMonthlyCost`, `projectedMonthlyFreed`, `projectedMonthlySavings` | `pending` until 24 hours of the month are tracked, on the first of each month too | | `freedThisMonth` | Not paused this month. Dry-run workloads record what they’d free in `status.dryRun` | | `savedThisMonth` | The nodes still fit everything paused, so nothing comes off the bill until nodes go ([Freed but not saved](#freed-but-not-saved)). `unknown`, with why in `savingsUnknown`, under `watchNamespaces` ([Saved reads `unknown`](#saved-reads-unknown)) | Totals restart at zero each calendar month, in UTC ([Cost tracking](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/)). ### Freed but not saved FREED THIS MONTH well above SAVED in `kubectl napwake status`, maybe with Needs attention saying nodes aren’t scaling down. The nodes are still there. Saved counts nothing while a pool’s spare capacity could hold everything paused there ([How saved is measured](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#how-saved-is-measured)). 1. **Is anything scaling the nodes?** The first lines of `kubectl napwake status` and `kubectl napwake nodes` name the node autoscaler, as does `napwake_node_autoscaler`. With `none found`, nothing removes nodes or comes off the bill.
