[`napwake.dev/cleanup`](#finalizer-napwake-dev-cleanup) ## Labels | Label | Value | On | Description | | ------------------------------ | -------------------- | ------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake.dev/managed` | `"true"` | Deployments, StatefulSets, Namespaces | Opts in the workload, or every Deployment and StatefulSet in the namespace, with a ManagedWorkload made from its annotations. `"false"` on a workload leaves it out of a managed namespace, like `napwake.dev/ignore`. Other values don’t opt in, and an event says so ([Opting in](/next/guides/opt-in/)) | | `napwake.dev/ignore` | `"true"` | Deployments, StatefulSets | Leaves the workload out, even in a managed namespace, scaled back up first if paused. A ManagedWorkload you wrote for it does nothing and reports `TargetAvailable=False`, reason `TargetIgnored` | | `napwake.dev/protected` | `"true"` | Namespaces | Nothing here is managed, whatever its labels, and what was paused wakes. `kube-*` and Napwake’s own namespace are protected without it ([Protected namespaces](/next/guides/opt-in/#protected-namespaces)) | | `napwake.dev/allow-protected` | `"true"` | Namespaces | Lets Napwake manage a namespace labelled protected, matching `protectedNamespaces`, or always protected, such as `kube-system` or its own. The operator’s and doorman’s Deployments are never managed. Set it on purpose. Napwake never does | | `napwake.dev/from-label` | `"true"` | ManagedWorkloads | On ManagedWorkloads made from the `managed` label, the only ones Napwake updates and deletes. Those without it are yours | | `napwake.dev/managed-workload` | ManagedWorkload name | EndpointSlices | On the EndpointSlices routing a paused workload’s Services to the doorman or its forwarder, with `endpointslice.kubernetes.io/managed-by: doorman.napwake.dev`. Names over 63 characters are shortened with a hash. Removed once Running | | `napwake.dev/operator` | namespace | EndpointSlices, forwarder pods | The install’s namespace, on the doorman EndpointSlices an operator writes and its [forwarders’](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs) pods. Another install doesn’t move them to its doorman ([More than one install](/next/getting-started/installation/#more-than-one-install)) | | `napwake.dev/forwarded` | `"true"` | EndpointSlices | On a doorman EndpointSlice pointing a Service behind GKE NEGs at the workload’s [forwarder](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs), not the doorman | | `app.kubernetes.io/name` | `napwake-forwarder` | Deployments, pods | On each forwarder Deployment the operator runs beside a paused workload behind GKE NEGs, and its pods. The Deployment also has `napwake.dev/ignore: "true"`, so Napwake doesn’t manage it in a namespace opted in by label | ### Usage 
```bash
kubectl label deployment my-api napwake.dev/managed=true
kubectl label namespace preview-42 napwake.dev/managed=true
```
 Opt in a workload, or a namespace. 
```bash
kubectl label deployment my-critical-service napwake.dev/ignore=true
```
 Leave one out of a managed namespace. 
```bash
kubectl get managedworkloads -l napwake.dev/from-label=true --all-namespaces
```
 List the ManagedWorkloads made from labels. ## Settings annotations On an opted-in Deployment or StatefulSet, or its Namespace for all its workloads. A setting is the first of these that can be read ([Opting in](/next/guides/opt-in/#settings), [Helm defaults](/next/reference/helm-values/#opt-in-defaults)). 1. 1The workload's annotation 2. 2Its namespace's 3. 3The Helm defaultsonly `idle-after`, `cpu-threshold`, `dry-run`, `pause-early`, `forecast` and `wake` have one 4. 4The built-in default | Annotation | Accepted values | Default | Description | | --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `napwake.dev/dry-run` | exactly `"true"` or `"false"` | `"false"` | Measure without pausing | | `napwake.dev/idle-after` | a Go duration of at least `1m`, such as `"90m"` or `"2h"` | `1h` | Time without activity before pausing | | `napwake.dev/cpu-threshold` | a percentage from `"0.1"` to `"100"`, to two places, such as `"10"` or `"0.5"` | `10` | CPU % of requests above which the workload is active | | `napwake.dev/auto-resume` | exactly `"true"` or `"false"` | `"false"` | Wake ahead of forecast demand | | `napwake.dev/pause-early` | exactly `"true"` or `"false"` | `"false"` | Pause after 20 idle minutes, before `idle-after`, when a confident forecast expects quiet until then and the doorman would wake it. Sets `spec.idlePolicy.pauseEarly` ([Pausing early](/next/concepts/forecasting/#pausing-early)). Never with `napwake.dev/wake: "ask"` | | `napwake.dev/forecast` | exactly `"eager"`, `"balanced"` or `"cautious"` | `"balanced"` | How soon the forecast acts, at confidence 60, 75 or 85. Eager acts sooner, waking it for nothing more often. Cautious acts later, for workloads costly to keep awake. Sets `spec.prediction.mode`, which `spec.prediction.confidence` overrides ([Forecast setting](/next/concepts/forecasting/#forecast-setting)) | | `napwake.dev/depends-on` | comma-separated `kind/name` or `namespace/kind/name`, kind `deployment` or `statefulset` | none | Workloads this one needs. Empty declares none, dropping the namespace’s. On a namespace, it leaves out the workloads it names and those they depend on by their own `depends-on`, as dependencies rather than dependents. One in another namespace needs its `napwake.dev/dependents-from` | | `napwake.dev/wake` | exactly `"request"` or `"ask"` | `"request"` | What wakes it through the doorman. `"request"` is any request. `"ask"` is only a person’s click on **Wake it**. Sets `spec.wake.mode` ([Asking before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking)) | | `napwake.dev/wake-max-wait` | a Go duration above zero, at most `1h` | `2m` | How long a request is held while it wakes | | `napwake.dev/wake-page` | exactly `"true"` or `"false"` | `"true"` | Show browsers a waking-up page | Values that can’t be read, such as `"True"`, `"yes"`, `"soon"` or `"0"`. | Annotation | A value that can’t be read | | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Any setting | Skipped, with an `InvalidSetting` warning event on the workload. The setting comes from the next place in the order, never the default in place of a namespace’s value. Others still apply | | `dry-run` | Turns dry-run on, wherever it is. A typo in the setting meant to stop pauses must never start them | | `wake` | Asks before waking, wherever it is. A typo in the setting meant to keep robots out must never let them in | | `depends-on` | A bad entry is skipped, the rest apply. Only a value with no readable entry falls through to the namespace’s | ## Activity annotations | Annotation | Value | On | Description | | ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake.dev/last-activity` | RFC 3339 time | ManagedWorkloads, Deployments, StatefulSets | Activity another tool saw, such as a developer portal. A future time counts as now. A change while paused wakes it ([Activity annotations](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#activity-annotations)) | | `napwake.dev/active-until` | RFC 3339 time | ManagedWorkloads, Deployments, StatefulSets | Awake until then, woken if paused | | `napwake.dev/last-request` | RFC 3339 time | ManagedWorkloads | Set by the doorman when it holds a request for a paused workload, and with `doorman.admissionPolicy.enabled` by no one else. To wake a workload yourself, set `wake-requested`. Wakes it like `last-activity`, the clock recording `request` ([Wake on request](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/)) | | `napwake.dev/last-request-from` | IP address | ManagedWorkloads | Where the waking request came from, set by the doorman with `last-request` and, like it, by no one else. The operator [learns](/next/concepts/dependencies/#learned-dependencies) from it which workload depends on this one | | The three that take a time | not an RFC 3339 time, such as epoch seconds | as above | Ignored. It neither keeps the workload awake nor wakes it, and an `InvalidActivityAnnotation` warning event on the ManagedWorkload says so, once per value | ## Other annotations | Annotation | Value | On | Description | | -------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `napwake.dev/pause-requested` | any value that differs from the last request’s, such as the time | ManagedWorkloads | Pause now, as if the idle clock had run out. Set by `kubectl napwake pause`. Each value acts once, recorded in `status.lastPauseRequest`. An annotation, so a GitOps tool owning the spec leaves it alone ([Pause now](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now)) | | `napwake.dev/pause-overrides-forecast` | `"true"` | ManagedWorkloads | With a pause request, pauses even when a confident forecast expects demand within the hour. Without it, such a request is declined, saying when. It applies to the request it comes with, so set or remove it with each. `kubectl napwake pause --yes` sets it ([Pause now](/next/guides/pause/#pause-now)) | | `napwake.dev/wake-requested` | any value that differs from the last request’s, such as the time | ManagedWorkloads | Wake now, whatever the wake setting, as for a pull request bot, a portal or a chat command. An awake workload’s idle clock restarts. Set by `kubectl napwake wake`. Each value acts once, recorded in `status.lastWakeRequest`, and the `WakeRequest` condition says what came of it. An annotation, so a GitOps tool owning the spec leaves it alone ([Wake now](/next/guides/pause/#wake-now)) | | `napwake.dev/dependents-from` | comma-separated namespace names, or `"*"` for any | Namespaces | Lets those namespaces’ workloads depend on this one’s. A dependency in another namespace, declared or learned, is held awake, woken and waited for only if its namespace lists the dependent’s. Otherwise the dependent reports `DependencyNotAllowed=True` ([Across namespaces](/next/concepts/dependencies/#across-namespaces)) | | `napwake.dev/paused-from` | JSON, set by Napwake | Deployments, StatefulSets | What the pause changed, the replicas and the KEDA ScaledObject with its own `paused-replicas`. Written before scaling to zero, removed when handed back. If the ManagedWorkload’s status is lost while paused, as in a backup restored without status, Napwake takes the pause up from it, so it still wakes to its replicas. Don’t set or edit it | | `napwake.dev/turned-away` | JSON, set by the doorman | ManagedWorkloads | Where the doorman counts the requests it turned away during a pause, for a workload with `wake.mode: Ask`. The operator copies it into `status.turnedAway`. Don’t set or edit it ([What it turns away](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-it-turns-away)) | | `napwake.dev/ignore-dependencies` | comma-separated `namespace/name`, or a name in the workload’s own namespace | Deployments, StatefulSets, ManagedWorkloads | Dependencies Napwake shouldn’t [learn](/next/concepts/dependencies/#learned-dependencies) | | `napwake.dev/operator` | namespace, set by Napwake | ManagedWorkloads | The install that took it on, written with its `napwake.dev/cleanup` finalizer. Other installs leave it alone and don’t count it in their uninstall and upgrade guards ([More than one install](/next/getting-started/installation/#more-than-one-install)) | | `napwake.dev/doorman-ignore-ports` | comma-separated port names or numbers | Services | Ports the doorman never routes, such as a metrics port scraped elsewhere. They fail while paused ([what wakes a workload](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-wakes-a-workload-and-what-doesnt)) | ## Finalizers | Finalizer | On | Description | | --------------------- | ---------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake.dev/cleanup` | ManagedWorkloads | Added by the operator, and removed once a paused workload is scaled back up and its Services stop routing to the doorman, so the ManagedWorkload can be deleted | Caution Don’t remove `napwake.dev/cleanup` by hand. Without it, a deleted ManagedWorkload leaves a paused workload at zero replicas, and its KEDA ScaledObject held there, until a new ManagedWorkload for it takes the pause up from `napwake.dev/paused-from`. Next * [Opting in →Labels and settings in use.](/next/guides/opt-in/) * [Opt-in defaults →The Helm defaults for six settings.](/next/reference/helm-values/#opt-in-defaults) * [API reference →The ManagedWorkload these labels create.](/next/reference/api/)

# Metrics reference

> Every Prometheus metric the Napwake operator and its doorman serve, with its type, labels and meaning.

How the metrics are served. | What | How | | -------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Names | All start `napwake_`, registered with the controller-runtime metrics registry | | Endpoint | `:8443`, over HTTPS, as the chart and the Kustomize manifests serve it | | Scraping | Needs a token allowed to `get` `/metrics` ([Monitoring](/next/operations/monitoring/#prometheus)) | | Series dropped | The operator’s, when the workload’s ManagedWorkload is deleted. The doorman drops its `napwake_doorman_wakes_total` series then too, at its next sync of routes, and keeps them while a workload merely stops being routed | ## Health Whether the operator is healthy, and each workload’s phase. | Metric | Type | Labels | Description | | ------------------------------------------- | --------- | --------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake_build_info` | Gauge | `version` | Always 1, labelled with the release the pod runs, such as `v0.2.0`. The doorman serves it too | | `napwake_workload_phase` | Gauge | `namespace`, `workload`, `phase` | 1 for each workload’s current lifecycle phase | | `napwake_activity_unreadable` | Gauge | `namespace`, `workload`, `source` | 1 while Napwake can’t read one of the workload’s activity sources. `source` is `cpu`, from metrics-server while it’s awake (`MetricsAvailable` is `False`), or `prometheus`, its activity queries (`PrometheusAvailable` is `False`). No series otherwise, nor for a workload Napwake doesn’t read, as ignored, protected, or whose target is gone | | `napwake_resume_incomplete` | Gauge | `namespace`, `workload` | 1 while the workload runs after a resume that finished with replicas not Ready (`ResumeIncomplete` is `True`). A pause clears it. No series otherwise | | `napwake_reconcile_errors_total` | Counter | `controller` | Reconciles that returned an error, by `controller`, either `managedworkload`, or `optin`, which creates ManagedWorkloads from the `napwake.dev/managed` label | | `napwake_lifecycle_transitions_total` | Counter | `from`, `to` | Phase transitions | | `napwake_lifecycle_action_duration_seconds` | Histogram | `action` | Duration of lifecycle actions (pause, resume) | ## What the operator does | Metric | Type | Labels | Description | | --------------------------------------- | ------- | --------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake_prediction_confidence_percent` | Gauge | `season`, `namespace`, `workload` | Forecast confidence, 1 − WAPE, as a percentage, for `season` `daily` or `weekly` | | `napwake_prediction_phase` | Gauge | `namespace`, `workload` | Engine phase, 0 to 4 ([values](#forecast-phases)) | | `napwake_prediction_data_points` | Gauge | `namespace`, `workload` | Data points collected | | `napwake_prediction_anomalies_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload` | Anomalies detected | | `napwake_idle_detections_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload` | Times each workload’s idle clock ran out | | `napwake_wrong_pauses_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload` | Pauses made while the workload was in use. A request, a click, a wake request or an activity annotation undid each soon after ([Paused while in use](/next/concepts/idle-detection/#paused-while-in-use)) | | `napwake_external_scale_ups_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload`, `by` | Times each paused workload was scaled up outside Napwake, by the GitOps tool that did it (`argo-cd`, `flux`, `fleet`, `kapp`, `kluctl`), or `other` | ## Debugging | Metric | Type | Labels | Description | | ----------------------------------------- | ------- | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake_idle_seconds` | Gauge | `namespace`, `workload` | Seconds since the last activity. Keeps growing while paused, updated at each check, every minute awake and every few minutes paused | | `napwake_prediction_regime_changes_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload` | Regime changes detected | | `napwake_dryrun_actions_total` | Counter | `action` | Actions dry-run would have taken. `action` is `idle_pause`, `early_pause` (one the forecast brought forward, `pauseEarly`), or `requested_pause` (from `kubectl napwake pause`) | | `napwake_target_unavailable_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload` | Target workload not found | ## Savings Each node pool’s figures from the operator’s [measure of what pausing saved](/next/concepts/cost-tracking/#how-saved-is-measured), every 5 minutes, by the leader. Not exported under `watchNamespaces`, which can’t measure them. | Metric | Type | Labels | Description | | ------------------------------------------- | ----- | ------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake_capacity_paused_cpu_cores` | Gauge | `pool` | vCPUs the replicas Napwake holds paused requested, by the pool they ran in | | `napwake_capacity_paused_memory_bytes` | Gauge | `pool` | Memory the replicas Napwake holds paused requested | | `napwake_capacity_spare_cpu_cores` | Gauge | `pool` | Allocatable vCPUs on the pool’s schedulable, Ready nodes that no bound, unfinished pod requests | | `napwake_capacity_spare_memory_bytes` | Gauge | `pool` | Allocatable memory on those nodes that no pod requests | | `napwake_savings_realised_dollars_per_hour` | Gauge | `pool` | What the paused requests the spare capacity can’t hold would cost an hour at the pool’s nodes’ list rates, averaged over them (the defaults for nodes with no list price). That’s what pausing takes off the bill each hour, at least | | `napwake_node_autoscaler` | Gauge | `kind` | 1 for each node autoscaler found, of `Karpenter`, `Cluster Autoscaler`, `EKS Auto Mode` and `GKE Autopilot`. Or `none`, when it read everywhere it looks and found none, so what pausing frees [doesn’t come off the bill](/next/guides/cluster-autoscaler/#what-napwake-detects). Or `unknown`, when it wasn’t allowed to look, as in a `watchNamespaces` install. Looked at every 10 minutes | One series for each pool with Ready nodes or paused replicas, by `pool`: | `pool` | For | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The node’s first label of `karpenter.sh/nodepool`, `cloud.google.com/gke-nodepool`, `eks.amazonaws.com/nodegroup` and `kubernetes.azure.com/agentpool` | Nodes with one of those labels | | `""` | Nodes with none | | `*` | Paused replicas whose pool wasn’t recorded, set against what every pool has left over | ## Doorman Served by the doorman pods, on the same port and path as the operator’s. | Metric | Type | Labels | Description | | ------------------------------------- | --------- | --------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `napwake_doorman_wakes_total` | Counter | `namespace`, `workload`, `result` | Connections the doorman accepted for a paused workload, by `result` (below) | | `napwake_doorman_wait_seconds` | Histogram | `result` | How long the doorman held a connection before passing it through or closing it | | `napwake_doorman_held_connections` | Gauge | | Connections held right now, waiting for their workload to wake | | `napwake_doorman_proxied_connections` | Gauge | | Connections passed to a woken workload and still being carried | | `napwake_doorman_port_conflicts` | Gauge | | Doorman ports claimed by more than one route, which the doorman refuses to serve. Should be 0 | | `napwake_doorman_listen_failures` | Gauge | | Routed doorman ports this pod can’t listen on, which refuse every connection. Should be 0. A pod that can’t listen on one for 30 seconds reports itself not Ready | The values of `result`. | `result` | Meaning | | ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `success` | Passed to a Ready pod | | `page` | A browser was shown the waking-up page | | `asked` | With `wake.mode: Ask`, a person’s browser was shown the page that asks before waking ([Asking before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking)) | | `turned_away` | With `wake.mode: Ask`, anything else, such as a crawler, a scanner or an API client, was told it’s asleep, and nothing woke. Many mean something automated calls it. `status.turnedAway` counts them for each pause | | `timeout` | No pod was Ready within `wake.maxWait` | | `error` | The woken pods couldn’t be reached, or the wake couldn’t be recorded, by `wake.maxWait` | | `canceled` | The caller left, or the doorman shut down, while the connection was held | | `ignored` | A health check or scraper known by its `User-Agent`, answered `503`, or [Google’s load balancer health check](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#health-checks-the-doorman-cant-recognise), answered `200`. A request for a hidden file such as `/.env`, answered `404`. A connection that closed without sending anything, as each forwarder pod’s check that it reaches the doorman does every 5 seconds | | `limited` | Refused by a cap on held connections or a wake rate limit ([Limits](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#limits)) | | `refused` | The workload’s route is draining and none of its pods is Ready | ## Forecast phases The values of `napwake_prediction_phase`. | Value | Phase | | ----- | ---------------- | | 0 | Observing | | 1 | DailySuggesting | | 2 | DailyActive | | 3 | WeeklySuggesting | | 4 | FullyActive | ## Queries 
```promql
# Workloads with low prediction confidence
napwake_prediction_confidence_percent{season="daily"} < 70


# Workloads that will pause within 10 minutes (with the default 1h idleAfter)
napwake_idle_seconds > 3000 and on (namespace, workload) napwake_workload_phase{phase="Running"} == 1


# How long a request to a paused workload waits for it to wake (p95)
histogram_quantile(0.95, sum by (le) (rate(napwake_doorman_wait_seconds_bucket{result="success"}[1h])))


# What pausing takes off the bill an hour, across the cluster
sum(napwake_savings_realised_dollars_per_hour)


# Pools where paused CPU still fits in spare capacity: freed, but not saved
(napwake_capacity_paused_cpu_cores > 0) <= on (pool) napwake_capacity_spare_cpu_cores
```

# Security

> What a security review of Napwake needs: what it can do in a cluster, what it talks to, and how to check that what you install is what was released.

## What it can do Each power Napwake has, and what limits it. [Data and access](/next/reference/data-and-access/) lists everything it reads, writes and sends, and [Helm values](/next/reference/helm-values/#rbac) the exact RBAC. | It | And the limit | | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Never deletes your resources | Its one action is to scale a Deployment or StatefulSet to zero and back, through the scale subresource. Its role may patch workloads, but its only other change is its own `napwake.dev/paused-from` annotation while paused. It deletes only its own ManagedWorkloads, EndpointSlices and forwarder Deployments | | Never reads your Secrets | The doorman reads one Secret of its own, by name, the key it signs the [Wake it button](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#the-wake-it-button) with. The chart creates it empty and the doorman fills it. Its Role names that Secret alone. The operator has no RBAC on Secrets. Like any role that may patch Deployments and StatefulSets, though, its token could change a pod template to mount one | | Shows the internet nothing of the cluster | For a workload that [asks before waking](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#asking-before-waking), its pages and `503`s name no namespace, workload or Service. The asleep page runs only its own script, and no other site may frame it, so none can trick someone into clicking **Wake it** | | Wakes on a click only from the page | The button’s token is signed for that workload and pause, good for 10 minutes, and checked in constant time. It wakes only from a browser’s `User-Agent` that says the click came from the page. That’s `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin` over HTTPS, with `Sec-Fetch-User: ?1` from Chromium and Firefox, or the page’s own `Origin` over plain HTTP. A robot that forges those, or drives a real browser, can still click, at most once a minute | | Can write any EndpointSlice in the namespaces it works in | It only creates, changes and deletes its own, labelled `endpointslice.kubernetes.io/managed-by: doorman.napwake.dev`, and never overwrites one without it. RBAC can’t limit a role by label, so whoever holds its token could redirect any Service there | | Can create and delete Deployments there, with the GKE NEG forwarder | Only with `doorman.negForwarder.enabled`, off by default, to run a [forwarder](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#gke-container-native-load-balancing-negs) beside each paused workload behind GKE container-native load balancing. RBAC can’t limit it to its own, so whoever holds its token could create Deployments there, and patch them, as it already can. It deletes only a Deployment of the forwarder’s name that its ManagedWorkload owns. Forwarders run at the Pod Security Standards’ restricted level, as the namespace’s `default` ServiceAccount, without its token or any API access | | Can be confined to namespaces | With [`watchNamespaces`](/next/reference/helm-values/#namespaces), the operator and doorman get a Role in each of those namespaces instead of a ClusterRole, so Kubernetes refuses them anything elsewhere. What stays cluster-wide only reads Namespaces, Nodes and Karpenter NodePools, or checks scrapers’ tokens ([RBAC](/next/reference/data-and-access/#rbac)) | | Can be kept out of namespaces | One labelled `napwake.dev/protected=true`, or matching [`protectedNamespaces`](/next/reference/helm-values/#namespaces), is never managed ([Protected namespaces](/next/guides/opt-in/#protected-namespaces)) | | Keeps a workload awake for another namespace only where allowed | A dependency in another namespace, declared in `dependsOn` or learned, is held awake, woken and waited for only if its namespace’s `napwake.dev/dependents-from` lists the dependent’s. If not, it’s ignored as `DependencyNotAllowed`, with the same message whether it exists or not, so it can’t probe another namespace ([Across namespaces](/next/concepts/dependencies/#across-namespaces)) | | Learns dependencies from requests only through the doorman | Only from a `napwake.dev/last-request-from` the doorman wrote, which the `<fullname>-request-stamps` admission policy lets no one else set. The operator also checks the field manager the API server records, and counts only a sender in a namespace the woken workload’s namespace allows | | Doesn’t fight people or tools | A paused workload scaled up by anything else wakes. A GitOps tool undoing a pause is reported, not fought ([GitOps tools](/next/guides/gitops/)) | | Runs locked down | The images run as a non-root user on a distroless base, with a read-only root filesystem. So do the chart’s guards, hook Jobs from the operator’s image that run only during `helm uninstall`, `helm upgrade` and `helm rollback`, each with a ServiceAccount that may only list ManagedWorkloads | ## What it talks to Nothing leaves the cluster. There’s no telemetry, usage reporting or update checks. Inside it, these are the connections. | From | To | Why | | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Operator, doorman | The Kubernetes API server | Everything it does | | Operator | Prometheus, at `prometheus.url` | Only if set, for [the queries listed here](/next/reference/data-and-access/#what-it-sends) | | Any client of a paused workload’s Service (a pod, an ingress controller, or a load balancer through a NodePort) | Doorman | It holds the connection, wakes the workload, and forwards it | | Doorman | The woken workload’s pods, in any routed namespace, on their Service ports | Passing the held connection through | | Google’s load balancers, and any other client of a paused workload’s Service behind GKE NEGs | Forwarder, in the workload’s namespace, with `doorman.negForwarder.enabled` | It stands in for the workload’s pods | | Forwarder | Doorman, on its routing ports | Passing connections on, untouched | | Prometheus | Operator and doorman metrics | Only with a token allowed to get `/metrics`, unless `metrics.secure` is off | ## The doorman is a way in to paused workloads Any client that can reach a doorman pod can reach every routed, paused workload, in any namespace, on its Service ports. A workload’s own NetworkPolicy sees the doorman as the source, never the client. So the control that matters is a NetworkPolicy on the doorman. The doorman listens on all its pod’s interfaces, since binding to the pod IP would only shut out its own pod’s containers. The chart’s NetworkPolicy (`networkPolicy.enabled`, off by default) leaves the routing ports open to every source unless you restrict them. Where untrusted pods share the network, enable `networkPolicy` and set `doorman.networkPolicy.ingressFrom`. ### What narrows it | Control | What it does | | ----------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `doorman.networkPolicy.ingressFrom` | Admits only the callers your paused workloads really have, such as your ingress controller’s namespace and the namespaces whose apps call paused services ([how](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#restricting-who-can-reach-the-doorman)) | | `doorman.networkPolicy.egress`, off by default | An egress policy allowing only your pod CIDRs and the API server ([Helm values](/next/reference/helm-values/#network-policy)). It enforces in the network the bounds the doorman keeps itself, the next two rows | | What the doorman dials | Only Pod-backed IP endpoints of EndpointSlices the EndpointSlice controller manages. Never loopback, link-local or cloud metadata addresses, AWS’s `fd00:ec2::254` and Alibaba Cloud’s `100.100.100.200` included | | Forged EndpointSlices | Anyone who can write EndpointSlices in a namespace can forge those labels. So the doorman also reads the pod each endpoint names, and dials only if that pod is in the Service’s namespace and has the address. A forged slice can send it only to that namespace’s own pods, never past a NetworkPolicy into another. The operator checks the same way before leaving a Service to pods it says already serve it, so a forged slice can’t stop a paused workload waking either | | Forwarders, with `doorman.negForwarder.enabled` | The chart’s policy admits them from any namespace by their labels, `app.kubernetes.io/name: napwake-forwarder` and `napwake.dev/operator` naming Napwake’s namespace. Anyone who can create pods can set those labels, and reach the routing ports as a caller in `ingressFrom` can. A forwarder passes on only its own workload’s ports, but the doorman can’t tell it from a pod that claims to be one | | [Rate limits](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#limits) | Slow a port scan through the doorman, but don’t stop one | | A cap per workload | One workload holds at most 256 of a doorman pod’s 2048 places, so one that never becomes Ready can’t stop other workloads’ requests being held | ### The request stamps The doorman’s ServiceAccount can patch ManagedWorkloads, to set `napwake.dev/last-request` and `napwake.dev/last-request-from`, the caller’s IP address. It also sets `napwake.dev/turned-away`, where it counts what it [turned away](/next/concepts/wake-on-request/#what-it-turns-away) from a workload that asks. The operator learns which workload depends on which from `last-request-from`, so the doorman must be the only one who sets it. Two ValidatingAdmissionPolicies, both on with `doorman.admissionPolicy.enabled` (the default), make sure of it. | Policy | Refuses | Scope | | --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | | `<fullname>-doorman` | A patch from the doorman’s ServiceAccount that changes a ManagedWorkload’s spec, labels, finalizers, owners or any annotation but those three | Cluster | | `<fullname>-request-stamps` | A create or change that sets `napwake.dev/last-request` or `napwake.dev/last-request-from` on a ManagedWorkload, from anyone but the doorman’s ServiceAccount, the operator and cluster admins included. Removing one is allowed | `watchNamespaces` if set, else the cluster | To wake a workload yourself, set `napwake.dev/wake-requested`, as `kubectl napwake wake` does. Without the request-stamps policy, the operator still ignores an address not written under Napwake’s field manager, but anyone who can patch a ManagedWorkload can write under that name with `--field-manager=napwake`. ## Verifying a release Every release is built from a tag by the [release workflow](https://github.com/napwake/napwake/blob/main/.github/workflows/release.yml) and signed keylessly with [Sigstore](https://www.sigstore.dev/). The signature is bound to that workflow and tag, so there’s no key to steal. Each command fails unless the artifact came from that workflow. The signatures are made with cosign 3, so verify them with cosign 3 or later. * Image Its signature, an SBOM for each platform, and build provenance: 
  ```bash
  IMAGE=ghcr.io/napwake/napwake:v0.2.0-rc.9
  cosign verify $IMAGE \
    --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/napwake/napwake/.github/workflows/release.yml@refs/tags/v' \
    --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com
  cosign verify-attestation $IMAGE --type spdxjson \
    --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/napwake/napwake/.github/workflows/release.yml@refs/tags/v' \
    --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com
  gh attestation verify oci://$IMAGE --repo napwake/napwake
  ```
 * Helm chart 
  ```bash
  cosign verify ghcr.io/napwake/charts/napwake:0.2.0-rc.9 \
    --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/napwake/napwake/.github/workflows/release.yml@refs/tags/v' \
    --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com
  ```
 * Plugin and release files The plugin, `install.yaml` and the other release files, through their signed checksums: 
  ```bash
  cosign verify-blob checksums.txt --bundle checksums.txt.sigstore.json \
    --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/napwake/napwake/.github/workflows/release.yml@refs/tags/v' \
    --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com
  sha256sum --check --ignore-missing checksums.txt
  gh attestation verify kubectl-napwake-linux-amd64.tar.gz --repo napwake/napwake
  ```
 ## Vulnerabilities | When | What runs | | ------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Every pull request | [govulncheck](https://go.dev/doc/security/vuln/) on the Go code, failing on any known vulnerability the code can reach. [Trivy](https://trivy.dev/) on the built image, failing on critical and high vulnerabilities that have a fix. One with no fix yet is reported, not blocking | | Every release | Both again, before anything is published. Trivy’s full report for each platform goes on the GitHub release, as `vulnerabilities-linux-amd64.txt` and `vulnerabilities-linux-arm64.txt`, beside the SBOMs, `sbom-linux-amd64.spdx.json` and `sbom-linux-arm64.spdx.json` | | Every day | Both against `main`, and Trivy against the released image, `ghcr.io/napwake/napwake:latest`, for both platforms. A vulnerability disclosed after a release is reported then, not at the next pull request | | Every week | Dependabot updates the Go modules, GitHub Actions and base images. Every Action is pinned to a commit, and the base images to a digest | To report a vulnerability, do it privately, through a GitHub security advisory, as [SECURITY.md](https://github.com/napwake/napwake/blob/main/SECURITY.md) describes.

# Contributing

> How to send Napwake a pull request: set up, build and test, the rules a change follows, and how a release goes out.

Before you start | Tool | For | | --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Go 1.26+ | Everything. `go.mod` pins the toolchain | | Docker | `make test-alerts`, which runs promtool in a container, so you don’t need promtool | | kubectl | | | [Kind](https://kind.sigs.k8s.io/) | The end-to-end and Helm tests | | Helm | Only `make check-chart` and `make test-alerts` | `make` downloads controller-gen, kustomize, setup-envtest and golangci-lint into `bin/` as it needs them. ## Send a pull request 1. Fork the repository, then clone and build: 
   ```bash
   git clone https://github.com/napwake/napwake.git
   cd napwake
   make build
   ```
 2. Create a feature branch from `main`, and make your change with tests. 3. Run the tests and the linter. `make lint test` must pass. 
   ```bash
   # Unit and integration tests (uses envtest for the K8s API)
   make test


   # Lint
   make lint


   # E2E tests: creates a Kind cluster, runs, and deletes it (KIND_NODE_IMAGE picks the Kubernetes version)
   make test-e2e


   # Install the Helm chart into Kind, and pause and wake a workload through the doorman
   make test-helm-smoke
   ```
 4. Go through the checklist below, then open a pull request that says what changed and why. | If you changed | Check | | --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Anything | Tests pass (`make test`), and lint passes (`make lint`) | | CRD types | CRD manifests regenerated (`make manifests`), DeepCopy regenerated (`make generate`), and generated code current (`make verify-generated`) | | The chart, or RBAC | The chart still lints and renders, with RBAC matching `config/rbac` (`make check-chart`) | | Alert rules | They still pass their tests (`make test-alerts`) | | User-facing behaviour | The documentation is updated | CI also runs the end-to-end tests on Kubernetes 1.30 and 1.37, the Helm smoke test, and `make vulncheck`. ### Run the E2E tests from several worktrees Each checkout gets its own Kind cluster, so you can run `make test-e2e` in two worktrees at once. | What | Default | Set it with | | -------------- | -------------------------------------------- | ---------------- | | Kind cluster | `napwake-e2e-<directory>-<hash of its path>` | `KIND_CLUSTER` | | Kubeconfig | `bin/e2e/<cluster>/kubeconfig` | `E2E_KUBECONFIG` | | Manager image | `napwake:<cluster>` | `E2E_IMG` | | kubectl plugin | `bin/e2e/<cluster>/kubectl-napwake` | `E2E_PLUGIN` | The run reaches its cluster only through its own kubeconfig. Your `~/.kube/config` and its current context stay as they are. The suite stops before it changes anything if kubectl’s current context isn’t `kind-<cluster>`. A second run from the same checkout needs its own name, such as `make test-e2e KIND_CLUSTER=napwake-e2e-second`. `make test-helm-smoke` names its cluster and image the same way, starting `napwake-smoke-`. If a run fails, its cluster stays for you to look into. The last lines say how: 
```bash
KUBECONFIG=bin/e2e/<cluster>/kubeconfig kubectl get pods -A
make cleanup-test-e2e   # deletes the cluster, its kubeconfig, image and plugin
```
 ## The rules ### Code * Comments say why, never what. None restate what the code does. * Wrap errors with context, as in `fmt.Errorf("doing X: %w", err)`. * Return early on errors, so the happy path flows straight down. * Accept interfaces, return structs. Define interfaces where they’re consumed. * Test behaviour, not implementation, with table-driven tests and `testify`. ### Commits Use [Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/), one logical change per commit: 
```plaintext
feat(idle): add idle detection with detector and webhook signal
fix(idle): reset idle timer on active signal
refactor(forecast): extract confidence scoring into separate type
chore(crd): regenerate CRD manifests
docs(guides): add Prometheus signals guide
test(policy): add table tests for scale constraints
```
 ### Generated code After changing CRD types in `api/v1alpha1/`, regenerate, and commit the result on its own, as `chore(crd): regenerate CRD manifests`: 
```bash
make generate   # DeepCopy methods
make manifests  # CRD YAML, RBAC
```
 ### Docs examples The docs are an Astro Starlight site in `docs/`, with their pages in `docs/src/content/docs/`. Run `npm ci`, then `npm run dev`, in `docs/`. `npm run build` fails on a broken link or anchor between pages. `make test` checks their examples: | A block | What `make test` does | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A ManagedWorkload in a `yaml` block in `README.md` or `docs/`, or in `config/samples/` | Creates it against the real CRD, so an example that doesn’t validate fails the build | | A partial one, a `spec:` block or spec fields such as `dependsOn` on their own | Wraps it into a whole ManagedWorkload first. Title it `managedworkload.yaml` or leave it untitled, since a block titled as another file, such as `application.yaml`, isn’t read as one | | Helm values, titled `napwake-values.yaml`, `values.yaml` or `values-<name>.yaml`, or untitled with only the chart’s keys | Checks it against `charts/napwake/values.schema.json`. Every value it sets must be one the chart has | | One after a `<!-- snippet -->` line, or `{/* snippet */}` in an `.mdx` page | Nothing, for a block that’s partial on purpose | | A link to `https://docs.napwake.dev` in the code | Fails unless it points at a page in `docs/` | A pull request’s docs build also checks every `https://docs.napwake.dev` address outside `docs/`, in the code, the chart and the README. Each must land on a page and anchor of the built site. To check it yourself, build the docs, then run `go test ./test/docs -run TestDocsAddressesLandInTheBuiltSite`. ## Where things are 
```plaintext
cmd/main.go                    # Operator entrypoint
cmd/kubectl-napwake/main.go  # kubectl plugin
api/v1alpha1/                  # CRD type definitions
internal/controller/           # Reconcilers
internal/forecast/             # Holt-Winters engine
internal/signal/               # Prometheus activity queries
internal/lifecycle/            # Pause and resume
internal/autoscaler/           # HPA and KEDA
internal/gitops/               # GitOps tool detection
internal/discovery/            # Cluster scan for kubectl napwake scan
internal/doorman/              # Wake on request
internal/cost/                 # Cost rates and node list prices
internal/metrics/              # Prometheus metrics, and the Metrics API reader
charts/napwake/              # Helm chart
config/                        # CRD, RBAC, kustomize manifests
test/e2e/                      # End-to-end tests, in Kind
test/docs/                     # Validates the docs' ManagedWorkloads against the CRD, and Helm values against the chart
tests/prometheus/              # promtool tests for the alert rules
docs/                          # This site (Astro Starlight)
hack/docsite/                  # Publishes a release's docs as a version of the site
hack/brewformula/              # Writes a release's Homebrew formula
```
 To run the operator against your current cluster, and to build the plugin: 
```bash
# Install CRDs into your current cluster
make install


# Run the operator locally (outside the cluster)
make run
```
 
```bash
make build-plugin
# Binary at bin/kubectl-napwake
```
 ## Releasing For maintainers. A release is a tag on `main`, `vX.Y.Z`, made and pushed like this: | Step | What it does | | ---------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `make release VERSION=X.Y.Z` | Bumps the chart’s version and appVersion, the kustomize image, and the docs’ install and verify commands and download links (the pages in the Makefile’s `VERSIONED_DOCS`). Dates the version’s section of `CHANGELOG.md`, commits that, and tags it. Pushing the tag starts the release workflow’s four steps | | 1. Check | Checks the chart, the kustomize image, the docs and the dated changelog name the tag’s version, and that no page’s install link is to `releases/latest` or another release (`make verify-version`). Then runs every check a pull request does, Trivy on the image included | | 2. Build and draft | Builds the plugin archives, the krew manifest, `install.yaml` and the chart. Pushes the image by digest, untagged, signs it and attaches its SBOMs and provenance. Drafts the GitHub release with every file, its signed checksums, and the changelog section, with a link to the release’s docs, as its notes | | 3. Publish | Tags the image, pushes and signs the chart, tags the image `latest`, and publishes the GitHub release, which moves `releases/latest` | | 4. Brew | Writes the Homebrew formula from the published archives and pushes it to [the tap](#the-homebrew-tap), for a release, and for a release candidate until the first stable release | Once it succeeds, the docs workflow publishes the tag’s docs, as [the docs site](#the-docs-site) describes. Nothing users install changes until step 3, so a run that fails earlier can be run again, and replaces its draft. So can one that fails in step 3. ### Rehearse with a release candidate Do this first. A tag with a hyphen runs the whole workflow as a pre-release: | What | A release candidate | | --------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Tag | Has a hyphen, such as `v0.2.0-rc.1` | | Published | Everything but `latest`, so the image, the chart and the GitHub release exist only for those who ask for that version | | Docs | Under `next`, with a banner saying it’s a release candidate | | Notes | Its version’s changelog section, which needn’t be dated yet | | Branch | A throwaway one, so `main` keeps the release’s version | 
```bash
git switch -c rehearse-v0.2.0-rc.1 main
make release VERSION=0.2.0-rc.1
git push origin v0.2.0-rc.1
```
 Once it succeeds, install it as the docs describe, with `--version 0.2.0-rc.1`, and check its signatures with the commands in [Security](/next/reference/security/#verifying-a-release). Then release from `main`: 
```bash
git switch main
make release VERSION=0.2.0
git push origin main v0.2.0
```
 ### The Homebrew tap `brew install napwake/tap/kubectl-napwake` installs the plugin from [napwake/homebrew-tap](https://github.com/napwake/homebrew-tap). | Part | How it works | | ------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | The formula | The release workflow’s last job writes the tap’s `Formula/kubectl-napwake.rb` with [`hack/brewformula`](https://github.com/napwake/napwake/tree/main/hack/brewformula), from the published macOS and Linux archives, and commits it as `kubectl-napwake X.Y.Z` | | Release candidates | Brewed only while the tap has no stable release, so people trying one before 0.2.0 can install it with Homebrew. Once a stable release is there, a release candidate never replaces it, since `brew install` and `brew upgrade` take whatever the formula is | | The token | The `HOMEBREW_TAP_TOKEN` secret, a fine-grained personal access token whose only permission is Contents read and write on `napwake/homebrew-tap`. Fine-grained tokens expire, so renew it before its expiry date | | A failed push | Without the secret, or if the push fails, the job warns and the tap stays on the last release, while the release and its docs go ahead. Once the token is fixed, re-run the job | To check a change to the formula, render it from a release’s archives, then audit and install it from a local tap: 
```bash
gh release download v0.2.0 -R napwake/napwake -D /tmp/release \
  -p 'kubectl-napwake-darwin-*.tar.gz' -p 'kubectl-napwake-linux-*.tar.gz'
go run ./hack/brewformula -tag v0.2.0 -dir /tmp/release > kubectl-napwake.rb
brew tap-new napwake/local --no-git
cp kubectl-napwake.rb "$(brew --repository napwake/local)/Formula/"
brew audit --strict --online --formula napwake/local/kubectl-napwake
brew install napwake/local/kubectl-napwake
brew test napwake/local/kubectl-napwake
brew uninstall napwake/local/kubectl-napwake
brew untap napwake/local
```
 ### Krew Each release carries a krew manifest, `krew-napwake.yaml`, which the release workflow writes with `hack/krew-manifest.sh` and checks with krew’s `validate-krew-manifest`. Until the plugin is in [krew-index](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/krew-index), the docs install it from that manifest’s URL. Once 0.2.0 is released, submit it to krew-index: 1. Check the release’s manifest installs on every platform. This needs [krew](https://krew.sigs.k8s.io/docs/user-guide/setup/install/). 
   ```bash
   gh release download v0.2.0 -R napwake/napwake -p krew-napwake.yaml
   go install sigs.k8s.io/krew/cmd/validate-krew-manifest@v0.5.0
   validate-krew-manifest -manifest krew-napwake.yaml
   ```
 2. Fork [kubernetes-sigs/krew-index](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/krew-index), add the manifest as `plugins/napwake.yaml`, and open a pull request. Krew’s maintainers review a new plugin by hand, against their [naming guide](https://krew.sigs.k8s.io/docs/developer-guide/develop/naming-guide/) and [best practices](https://krew.sigs.k8s.io/docs/developer-guide/develop/best-practices/). 3. Once it’s merged, change the README’s and the [plugin page’s](/next/getting-started/kubectl-plugin/#installation) Krew instructions to `kubectl krew install napwake`, without a manifest URL. For each later release, open a pull request to krew-index that sets `plugins/napwake.yaml`’s `version`, `uri`s and `sha256`s to those in the release’s `krew-napwake.yaml`. One that changes only those is tested and merged automatically, and [krew-release-bot](https://github.com/rajatjindal/krew-release-bot) can open it from the release workflow. ### The docs site Each minor release, `0.2`, `0.3` and so on, has its own version of the docs, built from its newest patch’s tag, so every page’s install commands name the release it documents. Once the release workflow succeeds, the docs workflow builds them and commits them to the `gh-pages` branch, which GitHub Pages serves. Each version’s folder is its own build, under its own base path. | Docs for | Go to | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A release | Its minor version, and `latest` when it’s the newest release | | A release candidate | `next`, with a banner saying they’re for a release candidate. The release it rehearses removes `next` | | The site’s root | Sends readers to `latest`, or to `next` until there’s a release | | A link without a version, such as `https://docs.napwake.dev/guides/gitops/#argo-cd`, as the operator and the plugin print | The same page in the version the root sends readers to, anchor kept. Every page of that version has a small page at its address without a version, which sends the browser on. Anything else goes through the `404.html` at the branch’s root | [`hack/docsite`](https://github.com/napwake/napwake/tree/main/hack/docsite) decides what a tag publishes, and never replaces a version’s docs with an older release’s. It lists what’s published in `versions.json`, at the branch’s root, which the version switcher reads. | To | Run | | -------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- | | Republish a release’s docs | The docs workflow, by hand, with its tag | | Preview the docs you’re editing, without versions | `npm run dev`, in `docs/` | | See the version switcher and banners, with two sample versions | `npm run site:local`, in `docs/` | | See what a tag would publish | This, with a checkout of the `gh-pages` branch at `../gh-pages` | 
```bash
go run ./hack/docsite -tag v0.2.0 -site ../gh-pages -dry-run
```
 Without `-dry-run`, it builds each folder and commits them to that checkout, without pushing.
